**⛔ THE DEITY DOCTRINE BINDS THIS TRANCHE ABOVE ALL.** Every faith space below is described as a CULTURAL and LITURGICAL-FUNCTIONAL building: processional axis, congregational floor, sanctuary/altar zone, sacristy/vestry, bell/tower, font/ablution, side chapels, cloister, chapter house, dormitory/refectory. Building types are researched as PARTI FAMILIES and functional grammars (basilican, centralized, hall, court, stave, meeting-house, temple-cella). Real buildings are cited as BOUNDED POSSIBILITIES or counterexamples — never as "the temple of X is how temples are". Deity names, scripture, creeds, doctrines: never; where a source's own vocabulary is liturgically specific (e.g. a named rite), this dossier translates it to the typed FUNCTION (ORIENTATION_WALL, ABLUTION, PROCESSION, CONGREGATION, READING_DESK, SCREEN). `EUROPEAN_FANTASY_BASE` morphology; this dossier mints no probabilities.

**Labeling discipline.** CONFIRMED = a source fetched or returned in a search digest THIS lane (URL inline; fetch date 2026-08-23 unless stated). PLAUSIBLE = synthesis or training knowledge — the grammar may NOT consume a PLAUSIBLE figure until P1c verifies it. Sub-lane CONFIRMED = fetched by a subagent of this lane on 2026-08-23; the parent did not re-open every URL (P1c owes re-verification on grammar-consumed figures, as R-INST-2 ruled). Contested points carry both sides. Finite semantics: every proposal is a closed enum the clerk composes from. Dates are absolute.

Families K (schools) and L′ (university) below are researched anyway, so that the grammar has a typed home ready if the owner adds the rows; each is flagged `NO CATALOG ENTRY` in its typed proposal.

## §1 · THE FAITH AND LEARNING SPINE (written LAST, from the sixteen merged families; the cruxes stated, then what the evidence settled)

**The cruxes this tranche had to settle** (stated at the checkpoint, tested below): (C1) is a faith building an AXIAL THROUGH_ROOM with a screen, and is that one parti or several? (C2) is the cloister walk the canonical GALLERY, and what does it carry? (C3) which small-tier entries are NO_BUILDING or HOSTED (the §462 test)? (C4) does the engine's Faith supply (deity rank major/minor/cult; `religiousCenter` exclusivity) license anything the evidence licenses? (C5) where does learning LIVE below the metropolis? (C6) what do the beyond-register partis ADD to the closed vocabulary?

**C1 — settled: ONE axial parti in the European register, NINE building partis in the closed enum (eleven with NONE_HOSTED and NO_BUILDING).** The parish church (§4) is an oriented `THROUGH_ROOM` sequence porch → nave → screen → chancel; its screen is a wall-with-passage whose passage is MEASURED at the floor (Escomb's chancel arch 5 ft 3 in — PARENT-VERIFIED) and becomes a CELL at the cathedral grade (the pulpitum with an internal stair and an organ loft — §8: `SCREEN_CELL`). The same axis scales from 4 m naves (Bradford, Escomb) to 16.4 m (Chartres) and 22.98 m (Girona), and from one screen to two in series (rood + pulpitum). But the evidence also holds six partis that are NOT axial through-rooms: the hypostyle GRID facing an orientation wall (§17 P1), the HALL with two foci and a sunken entry (P2), the CENTRALIZED ring under a dome (P3), the timber two-cell with an EXTERNAL walk (P4), the transverse MEETING_HOUSE with no focus wall (P5), and the windowless CELLA whose congregation stands outside (P6) — plus the court partis (CLAUSTRAL §7, COLLEGIATE_COURT §13) that wrap a church or a hall. The engine's `faith` template (nave front + sanctuary back + vestry) is exactly and only the BASILICAN two-cell floor; the `parti` field is the first thing the grammar needs.

**C2 — settled: the cloister walk IS the canonical GALLERY, and it carries FOUR functions at once.** Durham's alleys are "about 15 ft. wide" (4.6 m; PARENT-VERIFIED) round a garth "approximately 145 ft. square"; Rievaulx's garth "about 42 metres (140 feet) square" (PARENT-VERIFIED); New College's cloister 161 × 116 ft in 12 × 8 bays (PARENT-VERIFIED); Fontenay 36 × 38 m, Le Thoronet ~30 m (a trapezoid), Alcobaça ~51 m a side, the Certosa di Pavia 125 × 100 m. The walk is the house's corridor (every range opens off it), its study hall (Gloucester's twenty stone carrels 4 ft × 19 in × 6 ft 9 in — LOCAL Clark; Durham's three wainscotted carrels per window; Canterbury's glazed cubicles c. 1470), its wash-place (the lavatorium opposite the refectory door at three houses) and its processional ring — with the library built OVER it in the 15th c. (Wells 162 × 12 ft; Hereford over a 13-ft walk; Salisbury's "much too heavy" and cut down in 1758). The §452 GALLERY class needs a `functions[]` list and a `sizeFrom: host` rule for what sits on it. The ring has two cousins: the cathedral AMBULATORY round the choir (§8 — Bond's stated purpose: a pilgrim stream that must not block the choir) and the stave church's EXTERNAL ambulatory under the eaves (§17 P4) — one class, three positions (inside the envelope, round the focus, outside the wall).

**C3 — settled: the small tier is NO_BUILDING or HOSTED almost everywhere, with THREE licensed upgrades.** NO_BUILDING: the wayside shrine (§2: a cross 0.86–2.37 m on a socket stone; the Bildstock; the niche), the access row (§3: a 2–5 km walk — historically 0.75–3 miles and "a few yards to twenty miles"), the churchyard (§6: an acre with a bank/wall, a cross, a lychgate), the grove (§10: a ring, an enclosure, an avenue). HOSTED: the musician (§9: the nave on feast days, the hall's gallery, the alehouse, and a dated WEST GALLERY 1700–1850), the healer (§11: the patient's house, the priest's house, the cunning woman's cottage, the monastery's infirmary — a MOBILE institution whose host can DIE at the Dissolution), the school (§12: the nave, the porch chamber, the chancel chapel, the churchyard house, the guildhall's upper floor — "structural or documentary evidence for any special buildings ... so far lacking"), the library (§14: a chest, then an armarium, then a room over the walk — "their books were kept in a chest in the strong room"), the university before c. 1320 (§13: rented halls and the parish church's Congregation House), the archive (§16: a chest at four of five owners), the anchorite (§15: a sealed cell on the chancel wall, `approach: NONE`). The three upgrades are LICENSED EVENTS, not prosperity: the chapel of ease by a bishop's licence with a RIGHTS vector that accretes (font, graveyard — §3); the household oratory by a licence to a HOUSEHOLD (§3); the endowed schoolroom / library / college / cathedral by a FOUNDATION or a SEE (§12, §14, §13, §8). OWN BUILDING at every tier only for the parish church (§4) and the priest's house (§5 — which is a DWELLING with a study, not a faith parti, and whose tithe barn is a second lot that exists only at the RECTORY tenure).

**C4 — settled against the engine: deity rank licenses NOTHING structural; `religiousCenter` is wrong at two tiers.** No fetched source scales a shrine, church, cathedral or monastery by the rank of its dedication; the cathedral is licensed by a SEE event at any town size (St Davids 1,751 people), the monastery by an ENDOWMENT (Nuneaton's £253 net; Rievaulx's 6,000 acres), the college and library by a FOUNDATION (Wykeham, Bodley, the Malatesta, Philip II), the chapel of ease by a LICENCE, the pilgrimage grade by an INCOME ("sums which poured into the cathedral treasury"; Becket's ampullae). The one thing deity rank could plausibly map to is the SHRINE-STATION count (the pilgrimage grade of §8) — PLAUSIBLE, unsourced. `exclusiveGroup: religiousCenter` is correct at village (one church) and wrong at city (a cathedral city has friaries, a nunnery and 20–108 parishes: Chester, Norwich, London — §7/§8) and at town where the friary and the parish churches coexist (correct by the catalog's REQ placement). DESIGN §3's "tenure/endowment" has NO engine field (§Σ) — and this tranche found it load-bearing SEVEN times (the rectory's barn, the chantry's school, the cathedral's see, the monastery's count, the college's court, the library's room, the charnel's chaplains).

**C5 — settled: learning below the metropolis lives in the FAITH buildings' CELLS and the DWELLING's study.** The village's literacy is the priest (L762) with a STUDY closet in a hall house (§5); the town's school is hosted in the church/porch/chapel/guildhall until an endowment builds a 38 × 18 ft brick room (Ewelme 1437 — §12); the city's university is hosted in burgage halls and a church annex until c. 1320, then owns a court of chambers-on-staircases with study closets 3½–5 × 4½–6 ft (§13 — LOCAL W&C, measured) and a library room over a walk with a bay pitch of 4–7½ ft by fittings system (§14 — LOCAL Clark/Streeter, measured); the scriptorium is a row of carrels on the walk, never a room (§16); the archive is ONE cell type (`CLOSET{MUNIMENT}`) hosted in five owners' buildings (§16); the observatory is a patron's one-off (§15). The catalog has NO row for any of these below city (§0 observation) — three rows would fill it (song/grammar school, parish/chapter library, university), all HOSTED by default.

**C6 — settled: the beyond members add four circulation primitives and two storage primitives, and no theology.** The GRID floor with no axis (P1), the SUNKEN ENTRY down steps from the street (P2), the EXTERNAL ambulatory (P4), the SHUTTER-WALL that merges two cells (P5); the doorless HATCH-ONLY text store (P2) and the SACRISTY-AS-APSE (P3). Every one is a FUNCTION the §452/§453 vocabulary can spell with an attribute; none needs a word of doctrine.

**What the spine looks like, in one table (the grammar's reading order).**

| Rung | Faith | Learning | Verdict (§462) | Measured anchor |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| thorp | shrine marker (§2) · the walk (§3) | — | NO_BUILDING | cross 0.86–2.37 m; walk 2–5 km |
| hamlet | shrine-house option · oratory option · chapel of ease option (§2–3) | — | NO_BUILDING → HOSTED/OWN by licence | Menacuddle 2.74 × 3.45 m; Wethersfield chapel 34 × 17½ ft |
| village | parish church (§4) · priest's house (§5) · churchyard (§6) · musician/healer hosted (§9, §11) · grove (§10) · anchorhold (§15) | the priest's study; the school in the nave/porch | OWN (church, house) · NO_BUILDING (yard, grove) · HOSTED (rest) | nave 4.4–7.5 m; chancel arch 1.6 m; house 16 × 6.7 m; yard 1 acre; cell 2.0 × 1.3 m |
| town | 2–5 churches (§4) · monastery or friary (§7) · charnel (§6) · church house (§6) | grammar school hosted → endowed room (§12); parish library over the porch (§14) | OWN (church, precinct) · HOSTED (school, library) | cloister walk 4.6 m; garth 42 m; schoolroom 38 × 18 ft; library 16 × 14 ft |
| city | 10–30 churches · cathedral + close (§8) · several orders (§7) · sage's street (§15) | college court (§13); chapter library (§14); university schools (§13) | OWN + PRECINCT | quad 177½ × 125½ ft; chapter house 58–60 ft octagon; library 20 × 56 ft; study 4 × 5 ft |
| metropolis | 50–100+ churches · great cathedral (§8) · 5–10 monasteries (§7) | academy (§13) · great library (§14) · observatory (§15) | OWN + PRECINCT | nave 16–23 m wide; library 150–212 ft; theatre 7.56 × 6.92 m top tier; observatory 22–48 m |

**The three findings the grammar must not lose.** (1) The SCREEN — a wall with a passage inside a through-room, measured at 1.6 m and scaling to a cell with a stair in it — is the tranche's one new circulation primitive that the addendum could not type. (2) The library/charnel/vestry/study are all `sizeFrom: host` cells: the tranche's storage cells are DERIVED from their hosts (the walk's width, the aisle's bay, the chamber's corner), not licensed independently. (3) The ENDOWMENT/LICENCE event stream is the missing Faith supply: every upgrade from NO_BUILDING to OWN BUILDING in this tranche is a dated EVENT with a named donor or authority, and the engine holds no such input.

## §2 · FAMILY A — the wayside shrine: Wayside shrine (thorp L42 "Simple prayer marker. No resident clergy."; hamlet L285 "Simple prayer location. No clergy."; both `exclusiveGroup: 'religiousCenter'`)

*Provenance: sub-lane ABC (subagent of TC-R-INST-3, killed by the chair's §465 stop at ~06:15Z after 92 tool calls; its last note: "Research is complete (two final rounds added only incremental detail)"). No final report was written; the parent (this lane) wrote this section from the sub-lane's fetched evidence and search digests, recovered from its transcript on 2026-08-23. Labels: SUB-LANE CONFIRMED = page fetched by the sub-lane 2026-08-23; CONFIRMED-digest = search digest only; LOCAL = a PDF text the sub-lane saved and the parent read.*

**(a) Analogue.** The catalog's two rows describe a FIXTURE, not a building, and the historical register agrees at the thorp grade and splits at the hamlet grade. Three analogues, in ascending enclosure: (1) the **wayside cross / pillar** — the English and Cornish cross (a granite shaft with a wheel head set in a socketed base stone, at road junctions and on moorland routes), the German **Bildstock** ("a usually roadside pillar made of wood or stone that bears a sculptural or painted votive or devotional image": base, shaft, and a topped housing with a niche for a figure; sited "at roadsides, at road crossings, and field boundaries", as pilgrimage-route markers and accident memorials; the tradition "developed significantly from the 14th century onward"; regional types: the Alpine "sturdy, squat tabernacle pillars with very high, large pitched roofs", Franconian, the Wachau Breitpfeiler; related Wegkreuz, Marterl, Pestsäulen, Heiligenhäuschen — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [de.wikipedia Bildstock](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bildstock)), and the Italian **edicola votiva** (capitello / santella / tabernacolo / maestà / pilone by region: "two columns supporting a small pediment or gable framing a niche"; freestanding, wall-mounted, or merely painted; sited at "places of border, road intersections, and mountain passes" and on house façades; ex-votos against "famine or pestilence"; a station for community prayer and the Rogazioni processions; fittings = the image, candle provision — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [it.wikipedia Edicola votiva](https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edicola_votiva)); (2) the **open niche-chapel / Wegkapelle** ("kleine Kapellen, oft an Wegkreuzungen oder Abzweigungen"; may be an "offene Kapelle" (open niche) or enclosed; sometimes a "roofed or enclosed wayside cross"; also Stationskapellen on pilgrimage routes; the boundary with the Bildstock "ist fließend" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [de.wikipedia Wegkapelle](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wegkapelle)); (3) the **shrine-house over a spring** — the Cornish holy-well house: Menacuddle (late 15th c., granite blocks, a roof of granite slabs on three ribs, pointed-arched entrances in both north and south walls — CONFIRMED-digest, [Historic England 1019163](https://historicengland.org.uk/listing/the-list/list-entry/1019163)); Dupath (c. 1510, by the canons of a priory; single-cell granite ashlar, "steeply pitched corbelled roof, built from courses of granite slabs", a circular trough beside it, corner pinnacles, a small bell turret over the entrance; healing and possibly baptismal use — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Dupath Well](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dupath_Well)); and the Madron well-chapel, which is already a CHAPEL (a single cell with altar stone, stone seats and a chancel; the entrance is in the north wall "instead of the normal, west of centre" because a stream runs through the building — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Madron Well](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madron_Well_and_Madron_Well_Chapel)). The Breton **calvaire** is the ceiling of the open-air form: a stepped base platform carrying the cross group, carved with scenes, a focal point for pilgrimages and the pardon festivals, "sometimes functioning as part of an outdoor pulpit or throne"; oldest surviving 1450–1460 at Tronoën (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Calvary (sculpture)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvary_(sculpture))); Guimiliau's (1588) has 37 scenes on an octagonal base and sits in an enclos with a triumphal arch, a 17th-c. ossuary and a porch of 1606–17 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Guimiliau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guimiliau_Parish_close)) — i.e., at the ceiling the shrine is the churchyard's fixture set, family E's. Siting: the junction (Oundle's chapel "at the junction of three roads", in being by 1324 when a felon took sanctuary there — LOCAL, Swailes, *Lost churches of Northamptonshire*, Appendix 2), the crossing, the field boundary, the spring, the house front, the pass. Wear and ruin: the shaft goes and the socket stone stays — "most wayside crosses have either a simple socketed base or show no evidence for a separate base at all" (CONFIRMED-digest, HE scheduling text) — and chapel stone is re-used for a manor house (Hanging Houghton), a road (Nutcote, 1720) or a windmill site (LOCAL, Swailes Appendix 2). Era: the cross from the early medieval (Waterpit Down "early medieval decorated and inscribed"); the Bildstock from the 14th c.; the well-house c. 1480–1510; the calvaire 1450+.

**(b) Measured.** Cornish wayside crosses (scheduled-monument texts, CONFIRMED-digest, fetched via search 2026-08-23): Paul, Penzance — 1.35 m overall, shaft 0.38 m high × 0.39 m wide × 0.19 m thick, wheel head in a square base ([ancientmonuments.uk 108537](https://ancientmonuments.uk/108537-medieval-wayside-cross-at-paul-penzance)); Waterpit Down — 2.37 m overall, shaft 2.09 m, 0.69 × 0.27 m at the base tapering to 0.45 × 0.17 m ([HE 1007966](https://historicengland.org.uk/listing/the-list/list-entry/1007966)); Trevenning — 0.86 m overall ([HE 1007965](https://historicengland.org.uk/listing/the-list/list-entry/1007965)); Tregaminion — 1.31 m ([HE 1010854](https://historicengland.org.uk/listing/the-list/list-entry/1010854)); a monastic cross base at Malham with a socket 0.31 × 0.20 m, 0.18 m deep ([HE 1008776](https://historicengland.org.uk/listing/the-list/list-entry/1008776)). Well-houses: Menacuddle 2.3 m high × 2.74 m wide × 3.45 m long (≈ 9.5 m² external — CONFIRMED-digest, HE 1019163); Madron well-chapel internal 20 × 10 ft (6.1 × 3.0 m ≈ 18 m²), walls 2 ft thick and 8–9 ft high, altar stone 5 ft 10 in × 2 ft 7 in with a depression for a portable mensa (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Wikipedia). Lübeck's Chapel of Indulgences (1310) — 12 m long, 8 m deep, 2 m high (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia St Mary's Lübeck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Mary%27s_Church,_L%C3%BCbeck); a number audit flags "2 m high" as suspect — a chapel 12 × 8 m with a 2 m ceiling is implausible; probably a transcription slip for a vault or a different dimension; P1c to re-check). No measured figures found for the Bildstock height, the Wegkapelle floor area, or Dupath's plan (searched: the three Wikipedia pages above; HE 1383722/1019163 returned Cloudflare 403 to the sub-lane's fetch and curl).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The shrine is a fixture at one end and a chapel at the other, and the boundary is declared "fluid" by the German inventory vocabulary itself (Wegkapelle vs Bildstock) — the typed proposal must therefore carry a SIZE rule, not a name. (ii) The holy-well house is a CELL by floor area (Menacuddle ≈ 9.5 m²) yet has no congregational floor — it is a roofed fixture one walks through (two opposed doors), so the §453 "too small to be a cell is a fixture" rule cuts the other way here: a cell-sized roofed thing with no occupiable function. (iii) The catalog's "no clergy" holds for the cross and the niche but NOT for the hermitage-chapel, which is the English analogue of the roadside shrine WITH a resident: bequests "to Sir Thomas the hermit in St Andrew's chapel" at Little Harrowden (1517) and a "chapel or hermitage" south of Harringworth church (LOCAL, Swailes Appendix 2) — a one-person faith cell (family N's anchorhold is its enclosed cousin). (iv) The negation search ("wayside cross dimensions", "holy well well-house interior dimensions") returned measured figures only for Cornwall; the English lowland wayside cross is mostly a base stone today — the survival bias is toward granite. Null result: no source gave a plan for a freestanding English shrine-house that was not a well-house.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** NONE as a cell. The shrine sits ON a circulation feature of the settlement — `EXTERIOR_WALK{ROAD | GROUND}` (the CIRC addendum's new class) — at its junction or crossing; the calvaire's stepped base is a `STAIR_HALL{EXTERNAL}`-shaped fixture (a few steps) used as an outdoor pulpit. The well-house with two opposed doors (Menacuddle: north and south) is a walk-THROUGH fixture — a `CROSS_PASSAGE{NOTIONAL}` at fixture scale; Madron's north door (forced by the stream) shows the door position is licensed by the water, not by orientation.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** None. The niche is the store (the image; candle provision — edicola). The well-house's trough is a fixture.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: NO_BUILDING` for both catalog rows, with an OPTIONAL micro-cell upgrade at hamlet: `SHRINE_HOUSE` (a single roofed cell ≤ ~10 m² external, the `TEMPLE_CELLA` family's floor rung — one cell, one focus, no congregational floor). `fixtures[]` (liturgical FUNCTION types): `MARKER_CROSS{head: WHEEL|LATIN|PLAIN; shaft_h: 0.4–2.1 m; socket_base}`, `IMAGE_NICHE{support: PILLAR|WALL|FREESTANDING; roofed: bool}` (the Bildstock/edicola/niche), `IMAGE_TABLET` (Marterl), `SPRING_TROUGH`, `CANDLE_SHELF`, `STEPPED_BASE{steps: 1–3}` (the calvaire's outdoor pulpit), `BELL_TURRET` (Dupath — a one-bell fixture). Wear/fossil states: `COMPLETE → SHAFT_ONLY → BASE_ONLY → STONE_REUSED` (the HE survival pattern; Swailes' re-use cases). Structural buckets: none (`MONOLITH`; the well-house is `SLAB_ROOF` corbelled granite). Licensing: tier thorp/hamlet; culture weight decides FORM (English: cross; German/Alpine: Bildstock/Wegkapelle; Italian: edicola; Breton: calvaire), prosperity decides carving grade; deity rank does NOT change the form (a cult shrine and a major-deity marker are the same fixture set — the sub-lane found no source that scales the wayside marker by the rank of what it marks). `exclusiveGroup: religiousCenter` is respected: a thorp's one "religious centre" can be a post. HOME: NO TYPED HOME — the engine's `faith` template is nave + sanctuary (+ vestry), a building; a fixture-on-ground needs the NO_BUILDING mechanism (ODQ §462). VERDICT: thorp **NO_BUILDING**; hamlet **NO_BUILDING** (default) or **OWN MICRO-BUILDING** (`SHRINE_HOUSE`) where a spring or a pilgrimage route licenses it; never HOSTED (a shrine hosted in a hall is family B's oratory).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) The NO_BUILDING class must support a fixture that sits on a SETTLEMENT circulation feature (a road junction), not on a lot — today every catalog entry is a lot-holder. (2) A fossil-state enum on a FIXTURE (not only on rooms) — the DW law-5 fossil applies to a cross base. (3) The hermit's chapel shows that "no clergy" is a catalog STATE, not a building property: the same shrine with a resident becomes a one-person faith cell (family N) — the engine's `Priest (resident)` REQ at village (family D) is the next rung.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** The English register is poor in freestanding roadside shrines and rich in crosses (churchyard and wayside, mostly bases now); the continental register inverts this — the Bildstock and Wegkapelle thicken every Alpine and Franconian road, the edicola every Italian wall and crossroads, the Breton enclos makes the shrine a churchyard gate-group (arch, calvaire, ossuary, porch). Beyond the register: the roadside spirit-house / the shrine-niche at the town gate / the votive pillar are the same three rungs (marker, niche, shrine-house) and need no new parti — `TEMPLE_CELLA` at its floor covers the shrine-house everywhere.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** None (the Druid Circle is family I).

## §3 · FAMILY B — access to the parish church and the chapel of ease: Access to parish church (thorp L50 "Walk 2-5km to village church for services."; hamlet L293 REQ "Travel to village church. 2-5km distance typical.", `exclusiveGroup: 'religiousCenter'`)

*Provenance: sub-lane ABC evidence (see §2 header); the parent read the Dymond chapel-of-ease paper (LOCAL, `RINST3-ABC-dymond-chapel-of-ease.txt`, 14 pp., saved by the sub-lane from richardiii.net) and the Swailes Northamptonshire list (LOCAL).*

**(a) Analogue.** The catalog row is a NO_BUILDING by its own text: the institution is a WALK. The historical register has three states for a settlement without a parish church of its own, and the sub-lane's evidence dates each: (1) **the walk** — the hamlet's dead "had to be carried one and a half miles uphill to the large two-acre churchyard" at the mother church (Nayland → Stoke, before 1429 — LOCAL, Dymond, *The Chapel-of-Ease: Symbol of Local Identity and Ambition*, p. 207); Loxwood's people faced a "3-mile journey" through "dense woods and marshy land" to Wisborough Green (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Loxwood History](https://loxwoodhistory.co.uk/history-of-church-cottage/)); Longthorpe's chapel was taken down c. 1270 and rebuilt because "the poor state of the roads prevented parishioners from attending it"; Perio's chapel stood "about three-quarters of a mile from the church at Southwick"; Great Oxendon had a chapel "as the church was some distance from the houses" (LOCAL, Swailes Appendix 2). (2) **the hosted altar** — the manorial oratory: dispensations "for private chapels, oratories or portable altars may be found from 1279 in the published calendars of patent rolls", and "nearly every gentry family had a chapel in their manor-house" (CONFIRMED-digest, [buildinghistory.org chapels](https://www.buildinghistory.org/buildings/chapels.shtml); [OUP Lordship and Faith ch.](https://academic.oup.com/book/5957/chapter/149294164)); Ascote's chapel "existed in the manor house" under Henry VIII; Fotheringhay had "two chapels in the castle" (LOCAL, Swailes). (3) **the chapel of ease** — "Chapels abounded in medieval England and may have outnumbered parish churches"; they were "created for the 'ease' or convenience of parishioners who lived distant from a parish church, or for those within a town who sought more choice"; "the distance between such a chapel and its mother church varied from a few yards to twenty miles or more" (LOCAL, Dymond p. 203). The licence (recorded in bishops' registers) "assiduously protected the status of the mother church": tithes still went to the incumbent, attendance at the parish church on major festivals was usually expected, and baptism and burial "frequently" stayed with the mother church; privileges then accreted — "the right to baptise, which meant installing a font", "the right of burial and consecrate a graveyard" (LOCAL, Dymond p. 204). The Act of 1547 dissolving chantries "exempted those chapels 'ordained for the ease of the people dwelling distant from the parish church'" (LOCAL, Dymond p. 204, citing Statutes of the Realm IV pt 1, p. 28). Three licences in hand: Loxwood 1404 (eight petitioners to the bishop; "Masse and other Divine Services" allowed; burials reserved to the mother church though baptisms and marriages were held at Loxwood; the villagers to "find and sustain their own chaplain"; the first oratory of c. 1404 was converted into the chaplain's house when a larger chapel was built c. 1447 — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Loxwood History); Nayland 1429 (a royal mortmain licence and the lord's grant, one day apart, of "half an acre of land lying around the chapel" as a graveyard — "half the normal God's Acre" — to two elected wardens of a "populous hamlet"; the petition claimed corpses "remained at the hamlet unburied for six days and more" in winter, which Dymond tests against the actual road — a 150-ft rise — and finds "exaggerated" — LOCAL, Dymond pp. 207–208 and Appendix); Gravesend 1497 (the vicar-general's licence citing "magnam distanciam"; the chapel had been built by communal lay action 1452–75 from legacies of 3s to £10, supervised by an archdeacon, a gentleman, a butcher and a yeoman; plan "a nave and a north aisle containing a side altar"; porch by 1530; a tower planned in the 1530s–40s "never materialized"; baptism, marriage and burial explicitly withheld — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Kent Archaeology, Arch. Cant. 119](https://www.kentarchaeology.org.uk/journal/119/orthodox-parish-religion-and-chapels-ease-late-mediaeval-england-case-st-georges)). The continental legal form is the **Filialkirche**: subordinate to the Pfarrkirche, lacking independent legal status and (historically) burial and baptismal rights; a priest may be assigned; and — the key for the grammar — the classification says "nothing about the size or equipment of the building" ("nichts ... über die Größe oder Ausstattung des Gebäudes"): a filial church can be larger than its mother (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [de.wikipedia Filialkirche](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filialkirche)). England's largest chapel of ease is King's Lynn's St Nicholas; Rochester's St Margaret went chapel (1108) → parish church (1488) → chapel (1953) (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Chapel of ease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapel_of_ease)). Decline and re-use: a 14th-c. chapel at Wethersfield "converted into a cottage and divided into two storeys" early in the 17th c., its piscina still in the south wall (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [RCHME Essex i, Wethersfield](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/rchme/essex/vol1/pp332-341)); Lynch (c. 1530, single cell, wagon roof) "used as a store for the nearby farm" then returned to use in the 1880s (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Lynch Chapel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynch_Chapel_of_Ease)); Colchester's St Anne's "in 1748 the plain rectangular building was used as a barn"; St Helen's (a "small, single celled, rectangular building") went chantry → grammar-school grant 1539 (unused) → Quaker meeting house 1683 → National school 1830 → chapter house 1886 → museum store 1946 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [VCH Essex ix, Ancient chapels](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/essex/vol9/pp336-338)); and the inverse, a tithe barn turned chapel of ease in 1859 with "the entry in one wagon porch with the sanctuary in the opposite porch" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [greatbarns.org.uk Hamlet Church](http://www.greatbarns.org.uk/hamlet_church.html)). Northamptonshire's lost list carries chapels at deserted villages (Burghley 1289, deserted by 1450; Kingsthorpe 1398; Little Oxendon licensed 1398; Falcutt present 1536, gone by 1655) and "No trace remains of a former chapel of ease" at Elmington (LOCAL, Swailes).

**(b) Measured.** Distances (the only figures the catalog row needs): 1.5 miles (Nayland–Stoke, uphill 150 ft — LOCAL, Dymond); 3 miles (Loxwood — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED); 0.75 mile (Perio — LOCAL, Swailes); "a few yards to twenty miles or more" (the whole range — LOCAL, Dymond). Chapel sizes: Wethersfield 34 × 17½ ft (10.4 × 5.3 m ≈ 55 m²; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, RCHME); Madron 20 × 10 ft internal (§2); graveyard: Nayland's half-acre vs Stoke's two acres (LOCAL, Dymond). The Exeter 1287 synodal canons (9 and 12) are referenced by the search digest as governing chapels, but the DISTANCE rule the sub-lane looked for ("two miles"/"leagues") was NOT FOUND in any fetched text (searched: "medieval canon law chapel of ease distance ... Exeter 1287"; the Councils and Synods edition is not online) — the catalog's "2–5 km" is a designer's figure with no canonical threshold behind it, but it sits inside the documented range.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) Dymond's own test of the Nayland petition is the counterexample to taking licences at face value: the "impassable in winter" claim was "exaggerated" — licences record ambition and identity as much as distance; a grammar that builds a chapel whenever the walk exceeds N km is over-determined. (ii) The chapel of ease may be the LARGER building (King's Lynn; the Filialkirche rule) — size does not encode status; the catalog's tier order (access at thorp/hamlet, church at village) is a STATUS order. (iii) The negation search ("chapel of ease licensed never built / no trace") returned only a 19th-c. American case (Albany 1848) for "never built" — NULL for the medieval register; what it did return is the opposite failure: chapels that EXISTED and vanished with their hamlets (the Northamptonshire list), i.e., the hamlet-with-chapel is the fragile state and the hamlet-that-walks is the durable one. (iv) The hosted altar is a class of its own: the oratory licence is a licence to a HOUSEHOLD, not a place, and portable altars travel — the engine's one-lot-one-institution model cannot hold it.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** The access row is itself a circulation item of the SETTLEMENT: `EXTERIOR_WALK{ROAD}` of 2–5 km (the CIRC addendum's §4.1 class), with a dated `killedBy` when a chapel is licensed. The chapel of ease, once built, carries family C's floor circulation (THROUGH_ROOM porch → nave → focus; Gravesend added a `LOBBY{PORCH}` by 1530 and never got its tower). The hosted altar inherits the host's circulation (§4.3 item 9: `hostedIn`-inherited) — an oratory off the manor's chamber or at the hall's dais end.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** None for the walk. The chapel of ease's storage is the parish chest at fixture scale (it has no vestry in any fetched example: Wethersfield, Lynch, Guyhirn, Gravesend's "nave and north aisle"); the rights-accretion adds a FONT (a fixture) and a GRAVEYARD (family E), never a store.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: NO_BUILDING` (thorp L50, hamlet L293) with two licensed upgrades: `HOSTED{host: MANOR_HOUSE | HALL; cell: ORATORY}` (the household chapel: a small room or a screened bay with an altar, licensed per household; `rights: []`) and `OWN BUILDING: CHAPEL_OF_EASE` = family C's `BASILICAN` floor (two-cell or single-cell, one bell, porch optional) carrying a RIGHTS field the parish church lacks: `rights ⊆ {MASS, BAPTISM(font), MARRIAGE, BURIAL(graveyard)}` accreting over time, and `chaplain: SELF_SUSTAINED | MOTHER_SERVED`. `fixtures[]`: `ALTAR_FOCUS`, `SIDE_ALTAR` (Gravesend's north aisle), `BELL{1}`, `FONT` (only with BAPTISM), `PISCINA` (the fossil that identifies a converted chapel — Wethersfield), `CLOSE_SET_BENCHES` + `PULPIT_FOCUS` (the 1660 Guyhirn meeting-house grade: "close-set oak pews which prevented the congregation kneeling", "a raised pulpit" as the focal point, hat pegs — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Guyhirn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guyhirn_Chapel_of_Ease)). Structural: `SINGLE_CELL`, `SLATE|THATCH`, `WAGON_ROOF`. Fossil states (law 5): `CHAPEL → COTTAGE(floor inserted) | BARN | FARM_STORE | SCHOOL | MEETING_HOUSE | MUSEUM`, and the reverse `BARN → CHAPEL`. Licensing: tier thorp/hamlet; the walk is the DEFAULT; the chapel is licensed by (distance × population × a lay-initiative/prosperity input × era ≥ 13th c.), never by deity rank; culture: continental Filialkirche = same thing with a resident priest more often. HOME: NO TYPED HOME for NO_BUILDING and for HOSTED (ODQ §462); the chapel itself is `INTERIOR_KINDS.faith` at tier 0–1 (nave + sanctuary, no vestry). VERDICT: thorp **NO_BUILDING**; hamlet **NO_BUILDING** (REQ row — the walk) with `HOSTED` (oratory) and `OWN BUILDING` (chapel of ease) as dated, licensed upgrades.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) A RIGHTS vector on a faith building, distinct from prosperity: fixtures (font, graveyard) are licensed by rights, and rights are granted by events (a licence with a date) — DESIGN §3's Faith-row "tenure" is partly THIS. (2) A settlement-level circulation item (the road to the mother church) that a building's minting KILLS — the first `killedBy` that is not a stack or a wall. (3) The hosted oratory needs the `hostedIn` pointer on a HOUSEHOLD institution (family B of R-INST-1, the lord's agent's house), not on a faith lot.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** English: the chapel of ease is a lay-funded, bishop-licensed building whose rights accrete; the continental Filialkirche is the same legal shape with size declared irrelevant, and the Alpine Wegkapelle at the crossing is the chapel's micro-floor (family A). Beyond: the dependent prayer-hall / the neighbourhood oratory off a mother-temple is the same relation — a RIGHTS field (who may bury, who may initiate) and a `motherOf` pointer cover it without a new parti.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** None.

---

## §4 · FAMILY C — the parish church ladder: Parish church (village L747 REQ "Center of village life. Stone construction. Mandatory tithes.") · Parish churches (2-5) (town L1228 REQ "Multiple parishes within town.") · Parish churches (10-30) (city L1777 REQ "One per neighborhood.") · Parish churches (50-100+) (metropolis L2298 "Hundreds of parish churches across all districts.")

*Provenance: sub-lane ABC evidence; the parent read Schofield 1994 (LOCAL, `RINST3-ABC-schofield-clean.txt`, the 122-page LAMAS paper the sub-lane downloaded from colat.org.uk: "Saxon and medieval parish churches in the City of London: a review", Trans LAMAS 45 (1994) 23–146, with a 51-church gazetteer) and the RCHME Essex entries the sub-lane fetched.*

**(a) Analogue.** The parish church is ONE parti — an oriented axial sequence (entrance → congregational floor → screen → focus) — at four rungs the evidence dates: **(1) the single/two-cell floor** (nave + chancel, one bell, no tower, porch or vestry): Escomb (c. 670–675; two-cell, "narrow, tall nave", chancel arch 5 ft 3 in wide — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Escomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escomb_Church), citing Taylor & Taylor 1965); Bradford-on-Avon (porticus, 10th/11th c.; later "a combined school (nave) and cottage (chancel) ... both on more than one storey", rediscovered 1856 — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia St Laurence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Laurence%27s_Church,_Bradford-on-Avon)); Barfreston (c. 1170–85; nave and chancel only, "a small wooden turret was taken down", "only one bell", plan "a parallelogram not a rectangle" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Kent Archaeology Barfreston](https://www.kentarchaeology.org.uk/notes/st-nicholas-church-barfreston)); Heath Chapel (mid-12th c.; two-bay nave + two-bay chancel; "No tower, bellcote, porch, or vestry"; tub font; later five box pews and a two-decker pulpit — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Heath Chapel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heath_Chapel); HE 1383722 returned Cloudflare 403); Kilpeck (three-cell nave/chancel/rib-vaulted apse, bays 3/2/(1+3), double bell-cote, a west gallery of the 16th–19th c. — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [CRSBI Kilpeck](https://www.crsbi.ac.uk/view-item?i=11497)); and in London the c. 1080 St Benet Sherehog, "a rectangular stone church, of only one chamber", with doors north and south (LOCAL, Schofield gazetteer 25). **(2) the aisled rung** (an arcade pierces one or both nave walls; a west tower; a porch): the Essex village churches below; in London "By 1400, a nave and two aisles was becoming the norm", with whole rebuildings 1400–1532 and one-aisle churches where the site forbade the second (St Ethelburga; All Hallows London Wall, whose nave's north wall WAS the City wall, south aisle only, 1528; St Martin Outwich on a corner where a north aisle "would have encroached into Threadneedle Street") — LOCAL, Schofield pp. 47–48. Chapels accrete at the east end: "the fashion for chapels at the east end"; a neighbour gave part of his house to enlarge a chapel "on condition that he could have a window into the chapel from his house" (1337), and in 1544 a vestry "lay under another neighbour's house" (LOCAL, Schofield p. 80); Lavenham's Branch (c. 1500) and Spring (c. 1525) chapels flank the chancel behind parclose screens; Long Melford's Clopton chantry (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Wikipedia pages below). **(3) the ceiling** — the wool church: Long Melford (1467–97, nine-bay nave, 74 windows, a detached-in-plan Lady Chapel "with a central sanctuary surrounded by a pillared ambulatory" that served as a school 1670–early 18th c. — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Long Melford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Trinity_Church,_Long_Melford)); Lavenham (1485–1525; the east vestry of 1440 is "the only other remaining part of the previous church"; tower 1486–95 — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Lavenham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Peter_and_St_Paul%27s_Church,_Lavenham)). **(4) the metropolitan parish church** at continental scale: Lübeck's St Mary (1265–1352; "a three-aisled basilica with side chapels, an ambulatory with radiating chapels"; nine larger and ten smaller sepulchral chapels; the Burgomasters' chapel 1289 — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Wikipedia) and Landshut's St Martin (1389–1500, brick hall church, the tower 130.6 m — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia St Martin Landshut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Martin%27s_Church,_Landshut)). Fixtures as FUNCTION types, dated: the SCREEN with a loft ("a feature of the church by the 1340s" in London; the earliest parochial example mid-13th c.; the loft reached by "a narrow rood stair set into the piers supporting the chancel arch" or "the normal external turret"; roods out 1547, back, out again; lofts ordered down under Elizabeth, screens allowed to stay — LOCAL, Schofield p. 62; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Rood screen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rood_screen)); SEATING (standing congregation with the old and infirm on wall benches → movable benches 13th–14th c. → "blocks of fixed benches ... normally present in the naves of English parish churches by the end of the 15th century"; named pews with doors at St Michael Cornhill from 1457; separate men's and women's areas; seating in the chancel for prominent persons "accepted by 13th-century church statutes" — LOCAL, Schofield p. 63; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [buildingconservation.com sacred-secular](https://www.buildingconservation.com/articles/sacred-secular/sacred-secular.html)); the CHEST (1166: money trunks by the altar with "three locks and three keys"; 1287 Synod of Exeter: chests "for the storage of books and vestments" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [buildingconservation.com church chests](https://www.buildingconservation.com/articles/church-chests/church-chests.html)); the FONT (11th–12th-c. forms; none survives intact in the City — LOCAL, Schofield p. 63); BELLS in a tower "expressly to hang bells" (the City's towers "must have been built to serve as belfries"; St Martin Ludgate 1425: a belfry-tower on common soil "28ft by 24ft", containing "two gates for the mayor and commonalty to pass through" — LOCAL, Schofield pp. 56–58). Secular use of the nave is "the exception and not the rule" (church ales, a market in one church, courts — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, buildingconservation.com); but SCHOOLS were routinely hosted: "by the end of the medieval period a number of churches had schools attached to them; but structural or documentary evidence for any special buildings or arrangements for education is so far lacking. Presumably much of the business of the school took place within the church itself" — schools set up 1447–8 and 1456–7 in seven City parishes, a girls' school at St Helen's by 1459, and St Mary at Hill in 1523–4 "used a chamber in the adjacent Inn of the Abbot of Waltham as a parish school"; St Peter Cornhill "later acquired a library built by the executors of Sir John Crosby" (LOCAL, Schofield pp. 74–75 — the HOSTED finding for family K). Siting and the urban plot: London churches expanded over adjacent property (St Nicholas Cole Abbey 1377; St Mary at Hill 1500–1), stood over vaults "let out to secular occupation", built buttresses onto the common way only "providing that the common way was not narrowed" (1451), and the parish built tenements on its own churchyard (LOCAL, Schofield pp. 48–50, 80). Entrance: "the entrance through the south side of the tower is a London tradition or fashion. This is in contrast to the normal entrance in churches in Norwich or York, which is into the nave" (LOCAL, Schofield p. 58). Prosperity/wear and decline: 17th-c. Peterborough-diocese surveys — a vestry "decayed and ruinated", floors with holes "deep enough to bury a child in", a chancel "full of starlings, owls and jackdaws", a spire and part of a tower fallen (1657); stone from a redundant steeple taken "to repair the west bridge and the town walls" (1549) and from a chapel "to repair the Town Hall as needed" (1631) (LOCAL, Swailes pp. 6–8); the truncation modes — chancel kept as a family burial place while the nave goes (Rugeley; Ireby) or the choir demolished after the Dissolution while the nave stays (Fotheringhay) (CONFIRMED-digest, the chancel-demolition search); London gave up 11 ft of a church's east end to widen Gracechurch Street after the Fire (LOCAL, Schofield gazetteer). The count ladder (for the catalog's bands): London "over 100" / 108 parishes at the Fire; Winchester 57 by the late 13th c.; Canterbury 22 by c. 1200, "large in number and small in size"; York 35 by 1200 + 10 in the 13th c., rationalised 40 → 25; Lincoln 46 → 9 in 1549; Bristol 18 (LOCAL, Schofield pp. 37–38, 40); Norwich 56 in the late 13th c., 58 within the walls (CONFIRMED-digest, [the-past.com Norwich](https://the-past.com/feature/full-of-faith-new-insights-into-the-medieval-parish-churches-of-norwich/)); Northamptonshire "about 300 parish churches" c. 1500, 47 lost (LOCAL, Swailes preface).

**(b) Measured.** Nave internal widths (the grammar's one load-bearing figure): Escomb 14 ft 6 in (4.4 m) × 43 ft 6 in long, walls 2 ft 4 in thick and ~23 ft high; chancel 10 ft square; chancel arch 5 ft 3 in (1.6 m) wide, apex 15 ft (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED + PARENT-VERIFIED 2026-08-23 verbatim, Wikipedia/Taylor & Taylor) · Bradford-on-Avon nave 7.5 m × "a little over 4 m", ~8 m high inside (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED) · Barfreston nave 24–24½ × 17 ft, chancel 17 × 14 ft, walls 2–2½ ft (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Kent Arch.) · St Benet Sherehog 9 × 5 m (30 × 15 ft), c. 1080 · St Pancras Soper Lane nave 19 ft 4 in wide, walls ~3 ft, apsidal chancel · St John Watling St nave 27 × 17 ft, chancel ~10 ft 3 in wide · All Hallows Lombard St early church 66 × 22 ft overall with "no cross wall" · St Ethelburga nave+chancel undivided 55 ft 3 in × 17 ft · St Gabriel Fenchurch 76 ft long, walls 5–6 ft · St Mildred Bread St nave ~21 ft wide, aisles ~12 and ~15 ft, tower ~24 ft square · St Martin Outwich nave + south aisle 67 × 37 ft · St Anne & St Agnes 65 ft 6 in × 51 ft, nave and two aisles, tower flanked by aisles · St Mary Aldermanbury 74 × 44 ft, nave/chancel/aisles all five bays · St Martin Ludgate tower 28 × 24 ft (1425) (all LOCAL, Schofield gazetteer entries 6, 17, 24, 25, 38, 40, 47, 62, 65, 98 and p. 56). RCHME Essex village churches (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, British History Online): Corringham — chancel 28 × 13½ ft, nave 31 × 17½ ft, north chapel 14½ × 13½, north aisle 10¾ ft wide, west tower 13¾ × 14¾ ft (late 11th c.; modern north vestry) ([vol4/pp25-27](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/rchme/essex/vol4/pp25-27)); Terling — chancel 34½ × 20, nave 47½ × 24½ (four bays), south aisle 15½ wide, tower 17½ × 16½ (19th-c. vestry) ([vol2/pp227-230](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/rchme/essex/vol2/pp227-230)); Great Waltham — chancel 33½ × 27, nave 69 × 32 (c. 1100), south aisle 14 ft, tower 17 ft square, nave roof of four hammer-beam bays, "about thirty oak seats of mid 15th-century date", north vestry 1890 ([vol2/pp104-112](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/rchme/essex/vol2/pp104-112)); Wethersfield — nave 50 × 20½, chancel 38 × 21, vestry modern ([vol1/pp332-341](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/rchme/essex/vol1/pp332-341)); Braintree nave 52 × 22; Hornchurch nave 53½ × 18½ (CONFIRMED-digest, the RCHME search). Ceiling: Long Melford total 245 ft (75 m) with the Lady Chapel; nave 152 ft 6 in long, 41 ft 6 in high, nine bays; aisles 135 ft 4 in long, 24 ft high; 74 windows (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED); Lavenham 156 ft (48 m) long, tower 138 ft (42 m), tenor 21 cwt (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED); Lübeck 102 m long, nave 70 m, central vault 38.5 m, aisle vaults 20.5 m, towers 124.95/124.75 m (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED); Landshut tower 130.6 m (alt. 133.0 m) (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Counter-parti ceilings: the Gesù 75 × 35 m, "a single nave without aisles ... a series of identical interconnecting chapels behind arched openings", 1568–84, "the faithful to clearly hear the words of the sermon" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Gesù](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_Ges%C3%B9)); Sant'Andrea Mantua — single barrel-vaulted nave, "multiple chapels branch off the nave instead of lining it with aisles — because the colonnades would block the view", dome 80 m high / 25 m diameter, 1472–1790, a campanile of 1414 inherited from a monastery (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [en](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_Sant%27Andrea,_Mantua) + [it](https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_di_Sant%27Andrea_(Mantova)) Wikipedia). Chests: St Weonards ~2.6 m × 0.61 × 0.58 m; Eckington 0.87 × 0.54 × 0.52 m (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, buildingconservation.com). Derived widths ladder (parent arithmetic from the above): two-cell naves 4.0–5.3 m (Bradford, Escomb, Barfreston, St Pancras, St John Watling St); village aisled naves 5.3–7.5 m (Corringham 17½ ft → Terling 24½ ft); the big village/small town nave ~9.75 m (Great Waltham 32 ft); the urban nave 6.4 m + aisles 3.7–4.6 m (St Mildred); screen passage = the chancel arch at the floor: 1.6 m (Escomb). No measured rood-stair width, rood-loft width, or parvise floor area was found (searched: "parvise room over the porch" — functions only).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) **The vestry.** One blog-grade source says medieval parish churches "didn't have vestries ... at least they were uncommon outside of cathedrals and abbeys", vestments being kept in a chest and the priest vesting in the sanctuary (CONFIRMED-digest, modernmedievalism.blogspot.com — a weak source); Schofield's London evidence says the opposite for the urban/rich grade — a vestry in being by 1428 at the south-east corner of one church, another "in 1453", one bequeathed as an adjacent stone house, one under a neighbour's house (LOCAL, Schofield pp. 80, 122, 124); Lavenham's east vestry of 1440 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). All four RCHME Essex village vestries are modern (1874–1890). VERDICT: vestry = a prosperity × tier × era cell — urban and wool-church 15th c.; village mostly post-1800; the floor state is the CHEST IN THE CHANCEL (1287) — the §453 fixture-not-cell rule holds at the floor. (ii) **Pews.** Schofield (citing Nairn & Pevsner) says "Thirteenth-century pews survive at ... Dunsfold"; the buildingconservation article reports dendro on the Dunsfold benches "a felling date of 1409–1441" — CONTESTED; the measured source wins: fixed benches are 15th c. (iii) **The timber church.** Greensted's nave of split oak logs (51 planks; dendro revised 1995 to 1053 +10–55) is "an exceptional survivor" — "There were probably many similar churches ... but all of them were rebuilt in stone" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Greensted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greensted_Church); CONFIRMED-digest, britainexpress): the catalog's "Stone construction" is the SURVIVING register, not the built one — a timber floor rung exists and is an era/prosperity state (family P's STAVE). (iv) **One-aisle churches** are a SITE effect, not a prosperity effect (London). (v) **The aisle-less big church** is a deliberate parti (Gesù, Sant'Andrea — the preaching nave with chapels) — aisles are not a prosperity rung on the continent after 1470. (vi) The count ladder breaks the catalog's population bands: Norwich (~58) and Winchester (57) would be "city" by the engine's 10,000+ rule yet carried metropolis-band counts; the count tracks the pre-1200 parish-formation era, not population (LOCAL, Schofield: "The proliferation of churches in London and other towns at this general period"). (vii) Negation search on "no vestry" found the blog; on "chancel demolished / nave used as barn" found the truncation modes — not null.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** Floor: ONE `THROUGH_ROOM` sequence on an oriented axis — `LOBBY{PORCH}` (or the tower's ground stage as the lobby: the London `LOBBY{TOWER_PORCH}` sub-form — the entrance "through the south side of the tower" fossilised by the Fire, the tower passage doubling as a public gate at St Martin Ludgate) → nave (the congregational floor, a through-room) → the SCREEN (a `wall-with-passage` whose passage is the chancel arch: 1.6 m at Escomb; the screen's doors at Corringham's 14th-c. screen "with middle doorway") → chancel (focus). The aisles are NOT circulation cells — they are the nave's congregational extension (seating "in an aisle", 1431); the one processional ring in the family is Long Melford's Lady Chapel "pillared ambulatory" — `GALLERY{AMBULATORY; enclosure ENCLOSED; level GRADE; ring}` (the addendum's `CLOISTER_WALK` is its open cousin; family G has the cathedral version measured). Vertical: `STAIR_HALL{VICE}` ×2 at the aisled rung — the rood stair (in a pier or an external turret; "narrow", unmeasured) to `GALLERY{GALLERY_OVER}` = the rood loft (a singing gallery; "occasionally house an altar"), and the tower vice (ringing chamber, belfry); the parvise stair (a turret vice to the porch chamber). The west gallery (Kilpeck 16th–19th c.; the post-1600 musicians' gallery) is a second `GALLERY_OVER` at the entrance end — a dated `mintedBy` (era ≥ 1600) fixture. Undercroft joints: "the stair descending from the south aisle" to a 15th-c. crypt (St Dionis); small east-end crypts "entered from outside the church" (St Bride; All Hallows Barking) — the undercity joint is an EXTERNAL door or an aisle stair, never the chancel floor in the parish register (LOCAL, Schofield pp. 50–51). Metropolis grade: Lübeck's ambulatory with radiating chapels is a cathedral circulation (family G) carried by a parish church.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** Floor: FIXTURES only — the parish CHEST in the chancel (books, vestments, plate; three locks), the AUMBRY and PISCINA as niche-fixtures, the tower ground stage as `STORE`. Aisled/urban rung: `CLOSET{VESTRY}` (`lightReq: ANY`; size ~10–20 m² — no measured parish vestry found; adjacency `REQUIRED_NEAR(chancel)`, typically north or south-east; fixtures press/chest/table; era: 15th c. urban, 19th c. village) — and its two odd London forms, `CLOSET{VESTRY; hostedIn: NEIGHBOUR_HOUSE}` (a vestry UNDER another man's house, 1544; an adjacent stone house bequeathed AS the vestry) — a storage cell whose envelope is another building's. `CLOSET{TREASURE/PLATE}` = the PORCH-CHAMBER module (the parvise: priest's lodging → school → library → record room; Grantham's chained library over the porch — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Church porch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_porch); the addendum's `LOBBY{PORCH}` with chamber over and a vice) or the tower chamber (a chest "hidden in the belfry" at Foy). `CLOSET{MUNIMENT}` = the parish chest again (registers from 1538 — a fixture, not a cell, at every parish grade). `CELLAR/UNDERCROFT`: rare — "several churches stood above a vault or vaults let out to secular occupation" (the addendum's `SEPARATELY_LET` sub-form applies to CHURCHES: All Hallows Honey Lane, All Hallows the Less, St Mary Colechurch; the c. 1270 two-bay crypt at St Olave Hart Street with an entrance "directly to the open air") — and the CHARNEL: "a chapel called a charnel" before 1496; "As many as seven parish churches had charnels, the earliest dating from 1457" (LOCAL, Schofield pp. 50–51) — family E carries it. Family-wide adjacency law: storage cells accrete at the EAST end beside the chancel (vestry, chapel crypts), never at the west (the west is entrance/tower) — except the porch chamber.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: BASILICAN` (the oriented axial church) with rungs `rung ∈ {CELL_2 (nave+chancel, ≤ 5.3 m nave), AISLED_1 (one aisle — a SITE state), AISLED_2 (+west tower, porch, chapels), WOOL (clerestory, nine bays, chantry/guild chapels, Lady Chapel), METROPOLITAN (ambulatory + radiating chapels, 19 chapels — Lübeck)}`; alternates at the metropolis: `HALL` (the Hallenkirche — family P) and `PREACHING_NAVE` (single nave with side chapels instead of aisles — the Gesù/Sant'Andrea parti; era ≥ 1470, continental; a new sub-parti under BASILICAN because the axis and focus are unchanged and only the aisles are replaced by chapels). `functions[]`: NAVE (congregational floor; seating states STANDING → MOVABLE_BENCHES → FIXED_BLOCKS(≥ c. 1450) → NAMED_PEWS(1457+) → BOX_PEWS(17th c.)), CHANCEL (focus), SCREEN{loft: bool; stair: PIER|TURRET}, PORCH{chamber_over: bool}, TOWER{bells: 1–8; stages: 1–3; entrance: bool}, AISLE{n: 0–2}, CHAPEL{kind: CHANTRY|GUILD|LADY|BURGOMASTER; screen: PARCLOSE}, VESTRY (≥ AISLED_2 urban / era ≥ 1800 village), GALLERY_WEST (era ≥ 1600), CRYPT_JOINT{door: EXTERNAL|AISLE_STAIR}. `fixtures[]`: ALTAR_FOCUS, SIDE_ALTAR, FONT{form: TUB|OCTAGONAL}, PISCINA, AUMBRY, CHEST{locks: 1–3; L 0.9–2.6 m}, ROOD_GROUP (era ≤ 1547; fossil: the loft stair), PULPIT{decker: 1–3; era ≥ 1600 as focus}, LECTERN/READING_DESK, BELL_FRAME, WALL_BENCH (the floor's only seat), HAT_PEGS (1660 meeting-house grade), BATTLEMENTS (the London/civic fashion, 15th c.). Structural buckets: `orientedAxis`, `stoneWalls{0.6–1.8 m}` (Escomb 0.7 m → St Gabriel 1.5–1.8 m), `timberWalls` (the Greensted rung), `towerLoad`, `arcade{bays: 2–9}`, `clerestory`, `hammerBeam`, `vaulted` (Kilpeck's apse only at the parish grade; continental brick vaults at the metropolis), `chapelAccretion`. Licensing: tier village+ (REQ); rung by prosperity × era (CELL_2 floor any era; AISLED_2 ≥ 13th c.; WOOL 15th c. + a cloth/merchant prosperity input; METROPOLITAN by tier); culture decides TOWER vs CAMPANILE (family P) and ENTRANCE (London tower-porch vs Norwich/York nave door — a regional fashion, not a rule); deity rank (major/minor/cult) licenses NOTHING structural that the sub-lane could find — a cult's building is the same parti at a lower prosperity; `exclusiveGroup: religiousCenter` at village is correct (one church); at town/city the count band is the engine's input and the historical count is an ERA function (see (c) vi). HOME: `INTERIOR_KINDS.faith` = nave + sanctuary (+ vestry at tier ≥ 2) — the two-cell floor IS the template; NO TYPED HOME for porch, tower, aisle, chapel, screen/loft, rood stair, gallery, crypt joint, or the hosted school/library (the `learning` kind exists but not as a cell of a `faith` interior). VERDICT: OWN BUILDING at every tier (the village row is REQ and the evidence agrees: the parish church is the one building every village has); the chapel of ease (family B) is this parti's floor with a rights vector.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) The SCREEN is a wall-with-passage INSIDE a through-room — the §452 classes have no "partition with a passage" primitive; the addendum's pulpitum question (family G) is the same gap: propose `SCREEN{passage_w}` as a fixture-class joint that does not split the cell for reachability but does split it for VISIBILITY (the congregation "see the focus through the screen" — §6-bis). (2) Accretion by donors: chapels are minted by EVENTS (a will, a guild, a neighbour's bequest) not by prosperity alone — DESIGN §3's Faith-row "rooted temples accrete chapels" needs an endowment EVENT stream, which the engine lacks (the §Σ engine gap). (3) The hosted SCHOOL in the nave and the chamber over the porch as the school/library are the typed homes for family K at village/town — `hostedIn: PARISH_CHURCH{cell: NAVE | PORCH_CHAMBER}`. (4) A SITE-forced asymmetry (one aisle) is a plot-shape rule the grammar can already express if the lot is narrow — but the CITY-WALL-as-nave-wall case needs `joint.target: OTHER_BUILDING` (addendum §4.3 item 1). (5) Count per tier is an ERA prior, not a population prior — flag for the owner (the catalog's bands are fine as bounded possibilities; they must not be read as "one per 500 souls").

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** English: the aisled two-cell with west tower and porch, chapels accreting east; the tower is the parish's one tall element and its bells its one machine. Continental: the hall church (Landshut; the Low Countries) equalises nave and aisles under one roof and kills the clerestory; the Italian basilica detaches its bell into a campanile (Mantua's 1414 monastic tower re-used) and its font into a baptistery (a separate CENTRALIZED building — family P); the French parish church borrows the chevet; the Iberian single nave carries side chapels between internal buttresses; after 1470 the Italian preaching nave replaces aisles with chapels for sight and sound (Gesù: the acoustics argument is stated by the source). The Baltic brick parish church (Lübeck) is a cathedral-scale parti with a burgomasters' chapel — civic faith. Beyond the register: the congregational hall with an orientation wall (family P's MOSQUE_COURT / SYNAGOGUE_HALL) and the cella-with-forecourt (TEMPLE_CELLA) are the two non-axial-through-room alternatives; the BASILICAN through-room is the one parti where the congregation WALKS the axis.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** None.

## §5 · FAMILY D — the priest's house: Priest (resident) (village L762 REQ "Performs sacraments, collects tithes. Often only literate person.")

*Provenance: sub-lane DEHJ (killed at ~06:15Z after 85 tool calls; last note: "Final searches: the remaining measured priest's houses, a Pfarrhaus dated example, and the negation searches for E, H and J" — i.e., research essentially complete, no report). Historic England list entries returned Cloudflare 403 to the sub-lane's WebFetch and curl; it recovered the SAME list texts via britishlistedbuildings.co.uk (saved as `RINST3-DEHJ-BLB-*.html`), which reproduces the NHLE description verbatim — labelled SUB-LANE CONFIRMED (BLB mirror of NHLE <id>).*

**(a) Analogue.** The catalog's REQ row names a PERSON; the building is the parsonage (rectory/vicarage/priest's house), and the evidence gives it three states. (1) **Hosted in the church** — "In the Middle Ages, the parish priest could lodge in a room over the church porch, but by the 12th century a separate parsonage house was more usual" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [buildinghistory.org parsonages](https://www.buildinghistory.org/buildings/parsonages.shtml)); Grantham's porch chamber "once provided lodging for the priest" (CONFIRMED-digest). (2) **Own house on the glebe** — the parsonage "occupied glebe land, likely granted to the church during Saxon or Norman periods, potentially maintaining the same site for centuries despite rebuilding"; surviving medieval examples "reflected a standard of living comparable with lords of the manor and prosperous yeomen, though these survivors likely represented better-built rather than typical specimens" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, buildinghistory.org). The measured survivors are all HALL HOUSES of the ordinary domestic parti (DWR1A's hall house / Wealden), not a faith type: Muchelney (first mentioned 1308; "cross passage layout, with solid walls to cross-passage"; a west room, a centre room (the hall, originally open "from floor to roof", ceiled in the 16th c.), a parlour behind a part-stone part-wattle partition, a late-15th-c. "solar type" transomed window; raised crucks; "said to be 'ruinous' in 1608; it was used by the vicar or curate until c1840, when the house was used as a cellar and later as a school, and in late C19 was rented by a farmer" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, BLB mirror of NHLE 1056574, citing Pantin 1957 and VCH Somerset III); Alfriston Clergy House (dendro felling 1399–1407; "a central communal hall open to the rafters", "the priests rooms, comprising of a living room and a bedroom with lavatory (garderobe) attached" at the east, "two floors of humbler service rooms" at the west, "four bays ... two staircases"; built "for the priest of St Andrew's Church by Michelham Priory"; a floor "inserted in the hall c.1550" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [National Trust history](https://www.nationaltrust.org.uk/visit/sussex/alfriston-clergy-house/the-history-of-alfriston-clergy-house); Wikipedia calls it "a farmer's dwelling" originally — CONTESTED, see (c)); the Treasurer's House, Martock (a T-plan: early-13th-c. cross-wing, the hall "apparently added 1293-94" with a 4-bay arched-collar roof and "no fireplace", a late-15th/early-16th-c. DETACHED kitchen "which has no direct link to the house" with a "very wide but shallow fireplace" and a smoke-blackened south bay; the rector was the cathedral treasurer from 1227–8 — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, BLB mirror of NHLE 1225764); the Priest's House, Easton on the Hill (early 16th c., dendro 1485–99; "Rectangular, one-unit, plan. 2 storeys", a "shallow stair turret" with a "stone spiral stair", two 16th-c. fireplaces; probably built by the parson of 1456–95; later "a stable", now a museum — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, BLB mirror of NHLE 1225369); West Hoathly (five bays: solar wing two bays, hall two bays, buttery and pantry one bay; built "for the Priory of St Pancras in Lewes as an estate office" — a priory's estate house, NOT a priest's, despite the name — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Priest_House,_West_Hoathly)); Congresbury's vicarage range built 1440–70 by a bishop's executors, porch 1465 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Vicarage,_Congresbury)). (3) **The glebe farmstead** — the post-1571 terriers describe the parsonage as a FARM: 1607 "a stone parsonage house with 4 bays and 10 rooms, featuring a hall, kitchen, buttery, 4 chambers, and 3 cocklofts, along with 2 barns (one of 5 bays and one of 4 bays) and a hovel of 3 bays"; 1634 Halse "a hall, parlour, and kitchen, with chambers over parlour and kitchen. Beside the house stood a milk-house, dovecot, barn, and stable, and nearby a herb garden" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [VCH Somerset v, Halse](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/som/vol5/pp73-81)); 1686 "the dwelling house containing about 12 bays ... the tithe barn containing 8 bays ... hay barne, stables, granary, malt-house ... the old brew-house"; 1703 a brick parsonage with "entry, kitchen, dishhouse, pantry, hall, little and great parlour (with cellars beneath both parlours), two staircases, five chambers and six garrets" (CONFIRMED-digest, the glebe-terrier search). Nonington's terriers split the two institutions: "le parsonage" was "messuage or barn" with one acre (1533) and "one barne; one barne yard" (1615), leased to a layman in 1679, while "le vicaredge" was "one dwelling house and kytchin with a garden or orchard" (1615), "opposite the south side of the churchyard wall" (1744), and by 1833 a "small vicarage house and about 40 perches of garden" whose inventory held "three bells, silver chalice, surplices, linens, prayer books" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [nonington.org.uk](https://nonington.org.uk/le-parsonage-and-le-vicaredge/)). The TITHE BARN is the row's "collects tithes" made into a building: Great Coxwell (c. 1230, for Beaulieu Abbey; "Cruciform in plan, 152ft 2ins x 43ft 10ins x 48ft to the ridge"; seven bays, nave and aisles on 7-ft stone piers; the west transept "was originally the double storied office of the monastic official" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, BLB mirror of NHLE 1183045); Bradford-on-Avon (51 × 9 m, 14 bays, c. 1332, for Shaftesbury Abbey; "two large porches on the northern side to allow entry for loaded carts. Opposite them are smaller porches to allow the empty carts to leave" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tithe_Barn,_Bradford-on-Avon), citing Hughes 1985). Continental: the Pfarrhaus — "before the Reformation, priests often lived with housekeepers in church-provided quarters"; rural Pfarrhäuser kept a "strict separation between the residence and agricultural outbuildings"; the Pfarrhof held the barn and the Zehntscheune (tithe barn); the Protestant Pfarrhaus added the Amtsstube (study/office) when the pastor's household became a family (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [de.wikipedia Pfarrhaus](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pfarrhaus)); Engerhafe (East Frisia): a 13th-c. cellar and well, timbers dendro-dated c. 1535, "a hall with fireplace in the western section", core ~11 m long, gable 6.90 m wide, a 3.80 m extension, "originally had three single-room stories accessible from the narrow end" — a Steinhaus/Niederungsburg, i.e., a tower-house parsonage (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [de.wikipedia Pfarrhaus (Engerhafe)](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pfarrhaus_(Engerhafe))); the French presbytère with its servante and, in Quebec from 1664, "salles des habitants" for assembly and adjoining tithe barns (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [fr.wikipedia Presbytère](https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyt%C3%A8re)). Literacy/learning: the catalog's "often only literate person" is matched by the Pfarrhaus's Amtsstube and by the CIRC addendum's R-INST-1 finding that the scribe is "the priest's assistant" (L710) — the STUDY/closet is the priest's house's one non-domestic cell. Decline: "ruinous" 1608; cellar → school → farm let (Muchelney); stable (Easton); "many parsonages showed deterioration, evidenced by comments in bishops' visitations" by the early 18th c.; the parsonage barn "conveyed to trustees for use as a village hall in 1929" (Halse).

**(b) Measured.** Muchelney external 51 × 22 ft (16 × 6.7 m), four bays (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Wikipedia + NHLE); Engerhafe core ~11 m × 6.90 m gable, + 3.80 m (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED); Alfriston four bays incl. a two-bay open hall (dims NOT FOUND — the NT page has none); Great Coxwell 152 ft 2 in × 43 ft 10 in × 48 ft ridge, 7 bays, 7-ft piers, east transept ~14 × 4 ft, the west three times that (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, NHLE); Bradford-on-Avon 51 × 9 m, 14 bays, ~100 tons of stone tiles (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED); terrier bay counts: house 4 bays / 12 bays; barns 5 + 4 bays / 8 bays (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED / CONFIRMED-digest); glebe 1–2 acres beside the house (Halse 1571 "2 a."; Nonington 1 acre); Easton one-unit plan, 2 storeys (dims NOT FOUND; NHLE gives none). Pantin 1957 (Medieval Archaeology 1, 118–146) — the type-study with plans — was NOT retrievable (ADS returned Cloudflare/404 to two routes; the 14 KB XML is a catalogue record only); its figures are queued for P1c.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) Alfriston is called "a farmer's dwelling" built "by a yeoman farmer in 1350" by Wikipedia/a search digest and "built for the priest ... by Michelham Priory" by the National Trust's own history page — CONTESTED; the dendro (1399–1407) refutes the 1350 date either way; the building is a Wealden of the ordinary kind, so the verdict stands whichever patron is right: the priest's house is a HOUSE, not a faith parti. (ii) West Hoathly's "Priest House" never housed a priest (an estate office) — names lie. (iii) The REQ row has no building at the thorp/hamlet grades by definition (no resident priest there); at village the historical floor is a HOSTED lodging (porch chamber) or a cottage indistinguishable from the peasant's — Muchelney and Martock are the CEILING (a rich abbey's and a cathedral treasurer's houses), as buildinghistory.org warns. (iv) The parsonage splits into TWO institutions where the rectory is appropriated: the rector's barn (tithe) and the vicar's house — Nonington's "le parsonage" was a BARN leased to a layman; the engine's single row conflates them. (v) Negation search (the sub-lane's last round, "priest lived in church chamber over porch vestry priest's room absentee rector") confirmed the porch-lodging floor; no source for a priest living in the VESTRY was found.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** The house's: `CROSS_PASSAGE{HALL}` with "solid walls to cross-passage" (Muchelney — a walled passage, not a screens passage), the Wealden's two staircases (Alfriston: `STAIR_HALL{WINDER}` ×2, one each end), Easton's `STAIR_HALL{VICE; STAIR_TURRET}` on a one-unit plan (the smallest house with a vice the tranche found), Martock's "screen/gallery all covered in" (a `GALLERY_OVER` fossil) and its DETACHED kitchen (a `joint.refused` between kitchen and house — the CIRC addendum's §4.3 item 4); Engerhafe's "three single-room stories accessible from the narrow end" (a stacked `THROUGH_ROOM` tower, entry by the gable). The barn: two opposed porches per bay-pair = `CROSS_PASSAGE{ENTRY}` ×2 (cart in, empty cart out — Bradford-on-Avon's "large" and "smaller" porches are an asymmetric pair: a cart-width in-door and a narrower out-door); Great Coxwell's transept office with "steps up" = a `STAIR_HALL{EXTERNAL}`-class stair to the reeve's chamber.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** The house: `BUTTERY` + `PANTRY` pair (West Hoathly's southern bay; the 1607 terrier's buttery), `STUDY_CLOSET` (the Amtsstube; the "only literate person"'s cell — lightReq ANY, ≤ 10 m², fixtures desk/press/the parish books before the 1538 registers went to the church chest), `CELLAR` (1703 "cellars beneath both parlours"; Engerhafe's 13th-c. cellar + well), `ATTIC{COCKLOFT}` (1607 "3 cocklofts"; 1703 "six garrets"), the detached `KITCHEN` (Martock) with its own `STORE`; the farmstead: `STORE{TITHE_BARN}` (a nave-and-aisles barn 46–51 m long with a reeve's office — the biggest STORE in the village register), `DAIRY{MILK_HOUSE}` (Halse 1634), `STILL_ROOM`-class `BREW_HOUSE` and `MALT_HOUSE` (1686), `GRANARY`, dovecot, stable. Adjacency: the tithe barn sits on the GLEBE beside the house (REQUIRED_NEAR(house) at the rectory grade; at the appropriated grade the barn belongs to another institution — the monastery or the lay lessee — and the vicar's house has NO barn).

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: HOUSE` (DWR1A's hall house / Wealden / one-unit / tower-house — NOT a faith parti) with a faith-specific fixture set; rungs: `HOSTED{host: PARISH_CHURCH; cell: PORCH_CHAMBER}` (pre-12th-c. and the poverty floor at any era) → `COTTAGE` (unmeasured; PLAUSIBLE floor) → `HALL_HOUSE{bays: 4–5; open hall; cross passage; solar; service}` (the 14th–15th-c. measured survivors) → `GLEBE_FARMSTEAD{house 4–12 bays; tithe barn 5–14 bays; outbuildings}` (the 1571+ terrier state) → `TOWER_HOUSE` (continental/Frisian: Engerhafe). `functions[]`: HALL, CHAMBER/SOLAR, PARLOUR, STUDY (the learning cell), SERVICE (buttery/pantry), KITCHEN{attached|detached}, CELLAR, COCKLOFT; out: TITHE_BARN, BREW_HOUSE, MALT_HOUSE, DAIRY, DOVECOT, STABLE, GRANARY, HERB_GARDEN. `fixtures[]`: HEARTH (Martock's hall had NONE — an open hall without a fireplace is a wear/era state), GARDEROBE (Alfriston), PRESS/DESK (the study), the parish's own movables kept in the house (bells, chalice, surplices, books — Nonington 1833: a `CLOSET{TREASURE}` function hosted in the vicarage, not the church), BARN_PORCH{in: cart-width; out: narrow}. Structural: `timberFrame{crownPost}` / `stoneRubble`, `raisedCruck` (Muchelney), `thatch|stoneSlate|Collyweston`, `aisledBarn{stonePiers}`. Licensing: tier village (REQ person); house grade by prosperity × tenure (RECTORY vs VICARAGE — the appropriation state decides whether the tithe barn is the priest's: a TENURE field, DESIGN §3's missing Faith-row input); era (hall house ≤ 1550, then floored); culture (Pfarrhof separation; the Amtsstube post-Reformation; the Frisian Steinhaus); deity rank: no effect found. HOME: `ROOM_KINDS.hall/chamber/kitchen/cellar/store/study` all exist; the engine's `faith` template does NOT include a dwelling — the priest's house is a `dwelling` interior with a `study` (the `learning` kind's room) — NO TYPED HOME for the tithe barn's cart-passage pair or the detached-kitchen refusal. VERDICT: OWN BUILDING at village (a dwelling on the glebe, `hostedIn: NONE`), with `HOSTED{PARISH_CHURCH.PORCH_CHAMBER}` as the floor/poverty state; the TITHE BARN is a second lot on the glebe, present only at the RECTORY tenure.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) A faith ROLE (priest) whose building is a DOMESTIC parti — the engine must let `institutionNature: faith` select a `dwelling` interior with one learning cell (the study); the present `faith` template (nave/sanctuary/vestry) is wrong for this row. (2) TENURE (rector vs vicar; appropriated or not) decides a second LOT (the tithe barn) — the first concrete field for DESIGN §3's Faith-row "tenure/endowment" that the §Σ engine gap names. (3) The tithe barn is the village's largest STORE and belongs to the faith institution — the grammar's store ladder (R-INST-2) needs the aisled barn with opposed cart porches as a `STORE{TITHE_BARN}` sub-form.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** English: the parsonage is a hall house on the glebe with a barn, split by appropriation into rectory-barn and vicarage-house; continental: the Pfarrhof is a FARM COURT with a strict house/outbuilding separation, the Frisian parsonage is a stone tower, the French presbytère keeps a public hall (Quebec's salle des habitants); after the Reformation the Protestant Pfarrhaus adds the study-office that the English parsonage only acquires in the 18th-c. terriers. Beyond: the temple-keeper's house beside the precinct and the prayer-house's attached lodging are the same parti — a DWELLING with a study, on the institution's land.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** None.
