## §6 · FAMILY E — the churchyard: Graveyard (village L769 REQ "Consecrated ground around church.")

*Provenance: sub-lane DEHJ evidence; the parent read the two charnel primaries the sub-lane saved — Barnwell, Craig-Atkins, Crangle & Hadley 2023, "Medieval Charnel Houses: Resurrecting Lost Medieval Rites", JBAA (CC-BY; LOCAL `RINST3-DEHJ-charnel-whiterose.txt`) and Craig-Atkins et al., the Rothwell charnel chapel paper (LOCAL `RINST3-DEHJ-rothwell-craigatkins.txt`) — and the Villamagna cemetery chapter (Fenwick, LOCAL `RINST3-DEHJ-ucl-medieval-cemetery.txt`).*

**(a) Analogue.** The churchyard is a NO_BUILDING institution with a fixture set and, at the town grade, one building (the charnel house) and one surface joint to the undercity (the crypt stair). Enclosure: "Many churchyards date from Medieval times and may be the oldest piece of enclosed land in a parish. The encircling wall will date from that moment of enclosure"; "Originally, churchyards did not contain monuments ... other people were buried with no permanent marker"; "the ground inside is often considerably higher as a result of all the burials"; "In Medieval times the churchyard was a useful open space where archery practice, markets and fairs took place as well as games and festivals. A stone preaching cross might have been the only built structure apart from the wall"; "In the 15th century lychgates became frequent, sometimes with a coffin stone in the middle"; crosses "lost their tops during the Reformation and some were subsequently given a new use by the addition of a sundial"; permanent memorials "started to become fashionable" in the 18th c. (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Caring for God's Acre, Built Heritage](https://www.caringforgodsacre.org.uk/resources/built-heritage/)). Size: Penn's churchyard "approximates one customary medieval acre", the church "in the middle of the N-S axis of the churchyard", the present church of 1177 "on the highest spot of ground closest to the manor house" after an earlier wooden church where "roads and footpaths" converged on common land (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [pennchurch.uk no. 37](https://www.pennchurch.uk/no-37-the-siting-of-the-church-and-churchyard/)); Stoke-by-Nayland's "large two-acre churchyard" vs Nayland's licensed half-acre, "half the normal God's Acre" (LOCAL, Dymond — family B); a four-acre churchyard that shrank to two (CONFIRMED-digest). Boundary forms: a wall "of local stone rubble" in stone country; earthen enclosures "later replaced by modern walls"; ditches 180–600 mm deep; burials found "beyond the churchyard boundary, indicating that the graveyard had contracted" (CONFIRMED-digest, the enclosure searches). The lychgate: "four or six upright wooden posts in a rectangular shape" under a pitched roof, "recessed benches for mourners or a coffin-stool beneath"; the sheltered point "at which the coffin was set down at a funeral to await the clergyman's arrival"; the first part of the service read there; the 1549 Prayer Book's requirement that the priest meet the corpse at the churchyard entrance "encouraged the provision of lychgates"; lych-gates "existed in England in the 7th century, but comparatively few early ones survive because they were almost always of wood" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Lychgate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lychgate); CONFIRMED-digest, Britannica). Measured survivors: Boughton Monchelsea — "C16 or earlier. Timber framed ... 2 timber-framed bays. Gabled. 3 trusses ... Moulded octagonal crown-post ... Side walls open above midrails, boarded below, with continuous seat. Ground-cills on stone plinths" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, BLB mirror of NHLE 1068606; Wikipedia dates it 1470); Beckenham claimed 13th c. (roof "essentially 700 years old", side walls and foundations renewed 1924 — CONFIRMED-digest); the Westhead lychgate's interior "partitioned by a shoulder-high coffin rest" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, NHLE 1210371 — 1890s); Brampton Abbotts (1931: benches down each side, a stone coffin rest — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, NHLE 1464172, useful only as the fixture list). Zoning: the south side favoured; Gilbert White's 1789 note that parishioners avoided the north side; "some historians have argued that there is no evidence"; at Penn "two important graves lined with 14th century Penn tiles were found immediately outside the north porch door in 1967" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, pennchurch.uk) — CONTESTED; at Villamagna (Lazio), excavated: after 1297 "a substantial cemetery appeared in front of the church ... bounded on four sides by a cemetery wall creating a firmly enclosed yard (8 x 10 m)"; walled multi-burial family tombs "abut the still partially standing narthex walls"; 15 north–south adult burials "about ten metres west of the monastery wall", shallow, "excluded from the main cemetery for some reason"; 470 individuals from 383 graves, "the majority ... inside the church, or to its west" (LOCAL, Fenwick, *The Medieval Cemetery*, Villamagna). THE CHARNEL HOUSE (the tranche's undercity joint): "two main forms: separate structures in churchyards or rooms within the body of a church, with each type found at both major churches and parish ones. There was usually a chapel above the charnel, and in most cases evidence for visual access to the charnel, which was visible through windows in the lower room, which was typically semi-subterranean. The dimensions of charnel houses vary and have little independent significance, for they were usually determined by the size of the chapel, aisle or porch beneath which they were constructed" (LOCAL, Barnwell et al. 2023, p. 3); "the majority of charnel chapels ... were built in the mid-13th century to the mid-14th century"; access by "permanent staircases and doorways to allow the public, as well as those depositing charnel, to enter"; "at least one window, usually on the south or east side"; at the Reformation "either emptied and repurposed, dismantled entirely, or simply shored up with earth on the exterior" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Rothwell project, history](https://rothwellcharnelchapel.sites.sheffield.ac.uk/research/history-of-charnel-chapels)). Parish examples: Rothwell — a room 9 × 4.5 m of two rib-vaulted bays under the south aisle, late 13th c.; "two large, splayed openings, one placed central to each bay, apparently previously barred, with large external light wells providing both light for the room and visual access to it from ground level"; a piscina in the aisle above marks the altar over the charnel; "a now blocked slot behind the vaulting at the east end of the crypt rose to the floor of the aisle in front of the altar" (LOCAL, Barnwell p. 9); today reached by "a staircase of 17 steps ... descends through 180° clockwise to the charnel house from the west wall of the south porch", post-medieval, six treads re-using brass-indent slabs; a GPR void in the south aisle marks the original internal stair (LOCAL, Craig-Atkins et al., p. 11); the bones = an MNI "in the low thousands" (c. 2,500 per Garland 1988) (LOCAL, ibid.). Oundle 5.8 × 3.2 m under the south transept (entrance "always ... external"); Irthlingborough 4.8 × 4 m; Brackley 4.8 m square (octopartite vault on a central column); Kingsthorpe 4.8 × 4.6; Towcester 5 m square; All Saints Northampton ~6.7 × 6.9; Bosham c. 5 × 3.8 (two quadripartite bays, two windows "just above ground level"); Sandwich 4.5 × 7 m four bays under the south aisle's east end, "accessed from within the church"; Fotheringhay 3.50 × 5.70 × 2.2 m high under the 15th-c. north porch, c. 30 individuals, "a stone staircase provided access from the small room on the west side of the porch", filled in the early 19th c. (LOCAL, Barnwell pp. 9–10, 14); London: seven parish charnels, the earliest 1457, Sir Hugh Brice's "chappel called the Charnel" 1485 (LOCAL, Schofield; Barnwell table); Hythe's charnel (c. 4,000 individuals) "now stacked in the ambulatory beneath the chancel ... in what was originally an open passageway"; St Gregory Pottergate, Norwich: "a 14th-century passageway runs beneath the sanctuary, providing external access to a crypt ... 'which was a charnel'" (LOCAL, Barnwell pp. 7, 14). Freestanding (the town/cathedral grade): Norwich Carnary 1316, four bays c. 18 × 8 m, first-floor chapel, "circular cusped windows, which provided a view of the charnel"; Bury St Edmunds 17.95 × 6.7 m (c. 1300, two chaplains); Ely ≥ 10 × 6 m; St Paul's London two-storey with a Lady chapel above (Stow: "more than one thousand cart loads" of bones carted to Finsbury Fields); Exeter ≥ 12 × 6.5 m externally, crypt c. 3 m deep, stairs from the chapel's west end; Worcester c. 17.1 × 6.7 m, two vaulted bays; St Mary Spital 11 × 5.6 m, six vaulted bays; Bodmin's churchyard chapel (licensed 1377) over a vaulted crypt 12.2 × 4.2 m, "commonly called the Bone-house"; Barnstaple's two-storey chantry chapel with an unglazed lower window (dendro 1317–43) (LOCAL, Barnwell pp. 3–6); and the CONTINENTAL timber lean-to charnel against a church's west wall (Lucerne chronicle, 1513) — "an ephemeral charnel house in a churchyard is likely to have passed unremarked" (LOCAL, Barnwell p. 7). Continental charnel forms: Paris Innocents' arcaded charniers along the cemetery walls (14th–15th c.; closed 1780, ~2 million burials from 22 parishes — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Innocents%27_Cemetery)); the Breton ossuaire d'attache "built against church walls, often featuring multiple arcaded openings", "narrow bays at eye level (up to twelve)" for skull boxes, peaking 15th–17th c., and the freestanding funerary chapel (Saint-Thégonnec 1676–82, two-storey, crypt with an Entombment — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [fr.wikipedia Ossuaire](https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ossuaire); [fr.wikipedia Saint-Thégonnec](https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enclos_paroissial_de_Saint-Th%C3%A9gonnec)); Sedlec (c. 1400: "a Gothic church ... with a vaulted upper level and a lower chapel" as the ossuary; 40,000–70,000 skeletons — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedlec_Ossuary)); the Breton enclos as a WHOLE: wall + triumphal gate (Saint-Thégonnec 1587–89, four pillars, two staircases up to the arch) + calvary (1610, ~40 figures on one platform) + ossuary, "calvary positioned south, gate entrance to south-southeast, and ossuary to the southwest of the church" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). The CHURCH HOUSE (the churchyard's one secular building): "the medieval equivalent of the church hall", built for church ales "leaving the church for worship only" (early 1500s); Crowcombe c. 1515 — ground floor "to brew ale and bake bread", upper floor for "feasting, and dancing"; Chew Magna c. 1510 — 2 storeys, an EXTERNAL STAIR on two octagonal piers to two upper doors, three ground-floor doors, a "very large lintel across whole east wall with stone jambs for fireplace, possible smoke chamber above", an 11-bay arch-braced roof; later the school (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, BLB mirror of NHLE 1135935; CONFIRMED-digest, churchhousecrowcombe.org.uk).

**(b) Measured.** Churchyard: one customary acre (Penn, ≈ 4,047 m²; the church on the N–S axis); two acres (Stoke); half an acre (Nayland); four → two acres (digest); Villamagna's walled yard 8 × 10 m (LOCAL). Boundary ditch 0.18–0.60 m deep (digest). Lychgate: two bays, three trusses (Boughton Monchelsea); plan dims NOT FOUND for any medieval lychgate (the NHLE texts give none; searched "lychgate dimensions ... Historic England"). Charnel rooms (LOCAL, Barnwell/Craig-Atkins): Rothwell 9 × 4.5 m (2 bays), MNI ~2,500; Oundle 5.8 × 3.2; Irthlingborough 4.8 × 4; Brackley 4.8 sq; Kingsthorpe 4.8 × 4.6; Towcester 5 sq; Northampton 6.7 × 6.9; Bosham 5 × 3.8; Sandwich 4.5 × 7; Fotheringhay 3.5 × 5.7 × 2.2 h; Durham's "4 m in length by 2.3 m in breadth"; freestanding Norwich 18 × 8, Bury 17.95 × 6.7, Ely ≥ 10 × 6, Exeter ≥ 12 × 6.5 (crypt 3 m deep), Worcester 17.1 × 6.7, St Mary Spital 11 × 5.6, Bodmin 12.2 × 4.2; charnel stacked "up to 0.5 m in depth" against walls (St Mary Spital); Rothwell's stair 17 steps through 180° (post-medieval). Church house: Chew Magna 11-bay roof (dims NOT FOUND). Great Coxwell/Bradford barns → family D.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) North-side avoidance: folklore (White 1789) vs "no evidence" historians vs Penn's two tiled graves at the north porch — CONTESTED; the engine should NOT zone the churchyard by side; it may zone by STATUS (Villamagna's walled family tombs against the church vs the shallow excluded group outside the wall — excavated). (ii) The lychgate is a 15th-c.+ structure in the SURVIVING record but "existed in England in the 7th century" in timber — the floor is a timber gate that leaves no trace. (iii) The charnel's dimensions "have little independent significance" (Barnwell) — they are the chapel's above; the grammar must derive the charnel cell from its host (aisle/porch/chapel), not size it independently. (iv) The freestanding charnel house is a CATHEDRAL/town-monastery grade building (Norwich, Bury, Ely, Exeter, Worcester, St Paul's) with chaplains and endowments — at village it is a room under the aisle; at the smallest it is a timber lean-to (continental, 1513). (v) Negation: the sub-lane's "unenclosed churchyard" search found earthen enclosures and contracted boundaries — the WALL is not the floor; the bank/ditch/hedge is. (vi) The church house is post-1500 and English-southwestern in the fetched evidence (Somerset ×2) — a regional, dated sub-type; the sub-lane found no medieval (pre-1500) purpose-built church house.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** The churchyard IS `EXTERIOR_WALK{GROUND}` (the addendum's class): the lych way ends at the `LOBBY{LYCHGATE}` — a roofed two-bay vestibule on the boundary with benches and a coffin rest (a `LOBBY` sub-form the addendum lacks: OPEN-SIDED, on the ENCLOSURE line, with a FIXTURE that blocks the passage shoulder-high (Westhead) or sits mid-floor (the coffin stone)); the path to the south porch; the processional ring round the church (the Breton calvary/gate/ossuary are stations on it). The charnel joint: `STAIR_HALL{VICE|WINDER; direction DOWN}` from the aisle (Rothwell's original; Narborough's "winding staircase from the south-west corner of the Lady Chapel"; Upchurch's "circular staircase"; Newcastle's "staircase along western wall") or from the PORCH's side room (Fotheringhay, Rothwell now), or an EXTERNAL door (Oundle; Beverley "accessed externally from the churchyard"; the Norwich chancel passageway "providing external access to a crypt") — three joint forms for ONE undercity component; the freestanding charnel house has its own stair from the chapel's west end down c. 3 m (Exeter). The church house: `STAIR_HALL{EXTERNAL}` on piers to the upper hall (Chew Magna) — the R-INST-1 guildhall idiom at parish scale. The Breton gate: two staircases up to the arch (Saint-Thégonnec) — a `LOBBY{BARBICAN}`-shaped ceremonial gate with stairs (the addendum's barbican, inverted in meaning).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `CELLAR/UNDERCROFT{CHARNEL}` — the tranche's named storage cell: semi-subterranean (floor c. 1 m below the chancel's — St Peter's Leicester; c. 3 m deep — Exeter), vaulted (2 bays typical; quadripartite/octopartite), `lightReq: GROUND_LEVEL_SPLAYS` (barred, unglazed openings at external ground level, south or east — a NEW lightReq value: light and VISIBILITY from outside), size 15–40 m² under an aisle/porch, 60–140 m² freestanding, adjacency `REQUIRED_BELOW(CHAPEL/ALTAR)` (the piscina above marks it) and `REQUIRED_NEAR(CHURCHYARD)` (it is fed by grave-digging), fixtures: stacked long bones in rows with a walking passage between (Worcester "two rows ... leaving a passage"), skull bands, a slot to the altar above, a wall painting; states: `IN_USE → EMPTIED → BLOCKED/EARTHED (Reformation) → REDISCOVERED → RESTACKED (1843, 1911)`; the Breton `ossuaire d'attache` is an ABOVE-ground `STORE{BONE_SHELF}` against the church wall with eye-level bays — a storage FIXTURE-wall, not a cell. The churchyard's other stores: the sexton's tool store (NOT FOUND — the search for sextons' and hearse houses was null), the church house's ground-floor `BREW_HOUSE/BAKEHOUSE` with a smoke chamber (Chew Magna), and the parish's bier under the lychgate roof (a fixture).

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: NO_BUILDING` (the REQ row: consecrated ground) with a fixture set and three optional structures. `fixtures[]`: `ENCLOSURE{form: BANK_DITCH | HEDGE | WALL{rubble}; area: 0.5 | 1 | 2 | 4 acres}`, `PREACHING_CROSS{state: COMPLETE | HEADLESS | SUNDIAL_HEAD}` (the Reformation fossil), `LYCH_WAY`, `COFFIN_REST{stone | stool | shoulder-high partition}`, `BIER`, `GRAVE_MARKERS{none (≤ 17th c.) | headstones (18th c.+) | walled tombs (Italian)}`, `GROUND_RISE` (the raised interior — a wear grade: the older the yard, the higher), `FAMILY_TOMB_WALLED` (continental), `EXCLUDED_GROUP_OUTSIDE_WALL` (a status zone). Structures: `LYCHGATE{bays: 1–2; posts: 4–6; benches; roof: thatch|tile; era ≥ 15th c. surviving, timber earlier}` = `LOBBY{LYCHGATE}`; `CHARNEL{form: UNDER_AISLE | UNDER_PORCH | UNDER_CHANCEL | FREESTANDING_2STOREY | LEAN_TO(timber) | ATTACHED_SHELF(Breton); joint: AISLE_STAIR | PORCH_STAIR | EXTERNAL_DOOR | CHANCEL_PASSAGE}` — the undercity component's SURFACE JOINT is the only part this tranche types; `CHURCH_HOUSE{2 storeys; external stair; brew/bake below; hall above; era 1500–1650; region SW England}`; at the ceiling the Breton `ENCLOS{wall, triumphal gate with stairs, calvary, ossuary chapel}`. Structural: `vaulted{2 bays}` (charnel), `timberFrame{crownPost}` (lychgate), `archBraced{11 bays}` (church house). Licensing: tier village (REQ); charnel by era (mid-13th–mid-14th c. construction; blocked after 1547) × prosperity (an endowment with chaplains at the freestanding grade — again DESIGN §3's Faith-row endowment input) × burial RIGHTS (family B: a chapel without burial right has no yard and no charnel); lychgate by era; church house by era + region; deity rank: none. HOME: NO TYPED HOME for the churchyard (no lot, no interior), the lychgate, the charnel joint, or the church house; `ROOM_KINDS.cellar` is the nearest for the charnel cell. VERDICT: **NO_BUILDING** (the yard) with OWN MICRO-STRUCTURES (lychgate; charnel room as a component of the church's undercroft; the church house as a separate small building at the 16th-c. SW-English grade).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) A `lightReq` value the §453 set lacks — `GROUND_LEVEL_SPLAY` (light AND visibility from outside into a below-grade store: the charnel's defining attribute; also Bosham/Rothwell's light wells). (2) The charnel is sized by its HOST cell (derived, not licensed) — a `sizeFrom: host` rule. (3) The lychgate is a LOBBY on an ENCLOSURE, not on a building — the addendum's `LOBBY` sub-forms assume a wall of a building; the validator's reachability (S9) must treat the churchyard enclosure as a cell boundary with exactly one licensed roofed crossing. (4) The undercity joint has THREE forms (aisle stair, porch stair, external door) — `joint.target: UNDERCITY` needs a `via` attribute.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** English: the walled acre with a cross and a south porch, the charnel under the aisle, the lychgate from the 15th c., headstones from the 18th; Breton: the enclos makes the yard a designed precinct (gate with stairs, calvary, ossuary with skull boxes at eye level); French urban: arcaded charniers round the cemetery walls (Innocents) — a GALLERY{ARCADE} that is a bone store; Central European: the two-storey Karner/Beinhaus with the chapel over (Hallstatt, Sedlec) — the freestanding form as the village norm, not the cathedral exception; Italian (excavated): walled multi-burial family tombs against the church and a small walled yard (8 × 10 m) in FRONT of the church, burials inside the church the majority — the English "around the church" is one culture's siting. Beyond: the ossuary-with-view and the gate-with-rest are universal fixtures; the cremation-ground / the tower-of-silence / the family vault-field are NO_BUILDING variants with different fixture sets and the same enclosure law.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** None (the undercity's crypt conventions are the UNDERCITY train's; this family hands over at the stair).

## §6-bis · THE INFIRMARY-HALL-WITH-CHAPEL JOINT (a parent note FOR R-INST-4; the five hospital/almshouse/foundling rows are EXCLUDED to that tranche, but the building is a church-type parti and R-INST-4 should not have to rediscover it)

**The finding.** The medieval hospital, almshouse and infirmary is ONE building type in the faith register: a long aisled hall on an east–west axis with a chapel at the east end under the same roof, separated by a screen — the sick lie in the hall and see the focus through the screen. This is the monastic infirmary's parti (family F's infirmary complex) exported to the town. St Mary's Hospital, Chichester (completed by 1292): "six massive oak constructed bays, with a kingpost roof over 40' high", "chapel at the east end", the hall and chapel "all under one roof", a carved oak screen between them (CONFIRMED — [St Mary's Almshouses: Buildings](https://www.stmarysalmshouses.org.uk/about/buildings), fetched 2026-08-23; [Britain Express](https://www.britainexpress.com/counties/westsussex/properties/st-marys-hospital-almshouses.htm), fetched 2026-08-23: "accommodation for 13 residents", 40-ft roof, chapel with 13th-century misericords). A search digest of the Sussex Express piece gives the hall as "25 metres wide and 58 metres long when first built" and the chapel "14 metres long" (CONFIRMED-digest only — [Sussex Express](https://www.sussexexpress.co.uk/heritage-and-retro/retro/chichesters-remarkable-surviving-example-of-a-12th-century-hospital-3514998); ⚠ number audit: 25 m is very wide for a timber aisled hall of six bays — it may be an overall width including aisles, or a transcription slip; P1c should re-measure from the Historic England entry 1026704, which returned 403/Cloudflare to three fetch routes this lane). Decline sequence (CONFIRMED, both fetched pages): the open hall was "converted during the 17th century into eight single bedroom flats" with four inserted chimneys for back-to-back units; "two western bays were sadly demolished" at the end of the 19th century; five apartments after mid-20th-century works; four after 2003. That is the DW law 5 fossil sequence for this parti in one building: open hall → subdivided cells with inserted stacks → truncation → re-merger.

**What R-INST-4 inherits from here.** Parti `INFIRMARY_HALL_CHAPEL` (a `BASILICAN`-family sub-parti: aisled hall + screen + east chapel, single roof; the wards may be the aisles with the central vessel as the walk — the Beaune/Tonnerre continental form has the beds in the aisles against the walls and the chapel visible down the axis — PLAUSIBLE here; sub-lane F reports the monastic infirmary measured). Circulation: the hall IS a THROUGH_ROOM with the screen as the wall-with-passage; the later cottages mint a lost-circulation fossil. Storage: the chapel's aumbry and the warden's chest; the almshouse's later per-dwelling pantries are an 17th-c. grade. The foundling home's "wheel in the wall" (catalog L1770) is an ABLUTION/FONT-class threshold fixture in reverse — a turning box in the street wall — and has no typed home; R-INST-4 should propose it.

## §7 · FAMILY F — the claustral plan: Monastery or friary (town L1235 "Religious community. May operate hospital/school.", `exclusiveGroup: 'religiousCenter'`) · Multiple monasteries (city L1755 "Different religious orders.", `religiousCenter`) · Major monasteries (5-10) (metropolis L2314 "Five to ten major monastic houses: scholarly, contemplative, and charitable functions at scale.")

*Provenance: sub-lane F (killed at ~06:16Z after 94 tool calls; its last three WebSearches were REFUSED — "this session has used its web search budget (200 of 200)" at 06:14:48Z — so the lane's measured cloister-walk search never ran). The parent read the Witham Priory report (LOCAL, `RINST3-F-witham-priory-sanhs.txt`, Barlow/Reid/Burrow, SANHS 1990) and the Cessford Cambridge Austin-friary paper (LOCAL, `RINST3-F-cessford-cambridge-austin-friary.txt`, Antiquaries Journal 2023; ⚠ pypdf dropped every DIGIT from this text — its figures are unrecoverable from disk and are queued).*

**(a) Analogue.** ONE parti — the CLAUSTRAL plan: a church on one side of a square garth ringed by a covered walk, with the three other ranges holding (east) sacristy / chapter house / parlour-slype / day room with the dormitory OVER and the night stair into the transept, (south) warming house / refectory / kitchen, (west) the cellarer's range with the lay brothers or guests over — and outside the cloister the infirmary, abbot's lodging, guest hall, gatehouse and the walled precinct. The plan is the same from the 9th-c. ideal to the 16th-c. survivors: the Plan of St Gall (820–830; five parchments 113 × 78 cm; "approximately 40 ground plans"; church c. 91.44 m apse to apse, nave 12 m, aisles 6 m; "meant to house about 110 monks, 115 lay visitors, and 150 craftsmen and agricultural workers"; "never actually built" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Plan of St Gall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plan_of_Saint_Gall)); the Benedictine type-plan (east range sacristy/treasury/chapter/stair/scriptorium; south refectory + kitchen at the corner; west storehouses and pantries with the lay dormitory over; guest rooms, infirmary, work areas outside; "covered gallery or passageway on all four sides" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Carlin, Plan of a Benedictine Monastery](https://sites.uwm.edu/carlin/plan-of-a-benedictine-monastery/)); Durham (VCH, measured below: cloister, dorter over a 12-bay sub-vault, chapter house, frater over a 3-aisled sub-vault, the octagonal great kitchen 1366–71, prior's solar, rere-dorter, the slype with the library OVER it 1414–15, a gatehouse, and the infirmary's "lying house" prison — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [VCH Durham iii](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/durham/vol3/pp123-136)); Gloucester (the 13th-c. refectory "the length of the whole of the north side of the cloister" on "a vaulted undercroft with a central row of piers"; the new dormitory 1303–13 "aligned east-west, perhaps so that it could be longer"; the infirmary "an aisled hall of six bays" with a chapel "probably at the east end" off a small cloister; the abbot's first lodging "a tower c. 35 ft square" on the west side, relocated to the precinct wall in the 14th c.; three gates, one called the "lich gate" in 1223; the precinct 13 acres — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [VCH Glos iv](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/glos/vol4/pp275-288)). VARIANTS the evidence dates: (1) **Cistercian** — lay brothers' west range (Fountains' cellarium "over 300 ft" with the lay brothers' dormitory over; Rievaulx's range of 1135–42 "the earliest surviving Cistercian building in Europe"; Maulbronn's church divided by "a choir wall" between monks and lay brothers), the remote siting and huge precincts (Fountains 70 acres within an 11-ft wall; Rievaulx 37 ha; Poblet's third wall 608 m round, 11 m high, 2 m thick, 1367–82), the water engineering (Rievaulx diverted its river twice; Maulbronn "some 20 ponds", a stream "diverted to flow under the monastery to form its sewerage"; Alcobaça's 3.2 km conduit at 0.25 % slope; the Canterbury waterworks plan c. 1160–70 in the Eadwine Psalter) and industry (Fontenay's "forge" 53.3 × 13.5 m, 30 m south of the complex; Rievaulx's Laskill blast furnace) — all SUB-LANE CONFIRMED (Wikipedia pages for [Fountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fountains_Abbey), [Rievaulx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rievaulx_Abbey) + [EH description](https://www.english-heritage.org.uk/visit/places/rievaulx-abbey/history-and-stories/history/description/), [Maulbronn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maulbronn_Monastery), [Fontenay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fontenay_Abbey), [Alcobaça pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosteiro_de_Alcoba%C3%A7a), [Poblet es](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasterio_de_Poblet), [Eadwine Psalter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eadwine_Psalter)). (2) **Carthusian** — the great cloister of individual two-storey CELLS each with a garden, a water tower in the garth piping to every cell, a SMALL church: Mount Grace (1398; "15 cells in the east, west and north ranges of the Great Cloister, and cells for the prior and sacrist in the south range"; the church "initially a rectangular building of four bays, with a cross-passage at its centre", the tower "added above the cross-passage" in the 1420s; "an octagonal water tower in the Great Cloister piped water to each of the monks' cells"; a lesser cloister of six lay brothers' cells; a guest house of "four guest cells on the ground floor, with a suite of rooms on the first and second floors" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [EH description](https://www.english-heritage.org.uk/visit/places/mount-grace-priory/history-and-stories/history/description/)); Witham (1178–9, the first English charterhouse: the cloister alley located with its outer arcade wall and "the inner wall forming the exterior walls of the cells"; a 'cell' identified "by its apparent similarity in size to the examples at Hinton Charterhouse, and the presence of a probable washing place feature or 'sink'"; chapter house and book store "identified from their somewhat similar form and location to the 13th-century example at Hinton"; "Carthusian churches are not consistently located on the north side of the Great Cloister" — LOCAL, Barlow/Reid/Burrow 1990, pp. 176–177); London Charterhouse (1371, on a Black Death plague pit; 25 monks "each occupying their own small building and garden" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Charterhouse)); Certosa di Pavia (great cloister c. 125 × 100 m; 12 monks at first — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Certosa_di_Pavia)). (3) **Friary** (the catalog's "or friary") — the preaching church with a WALKING PLACE: Norwich Blackfriars (rebuilt 1440–70; "an aisled nave of seven bays ... divided by a cross passage from a chancel of five bays"; the passage "a walkway leading directly from the exterior to the cloister", "surmounted by a tower" (1462, collapsed 1712); "paved space south of the nave was an open preaching yard"; the original church 265 ft long; dorter and chapter house east, frater west, kitchens north — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Andrew%27s_and_Blackfriars%27_Hall,_Norwich) + [Norfolk HER MNF194](http://www.heritage.norfolk.gov.uk/record-details?MNF194-St-Andrew%27s-and-Blackfriars%27-Halls-Norwich=)); "a friary had less space and therefore a less formal layout than a monastery" and "strict enclosure ... was not a factor in planning their sites" (CONFIRMED-digest, Buckinghamshire HER theme); Cambridge Austin friars — a cloister built in several stages after c. 1300, the west range "an internal width of around seven metres", a possible SECOND cloister ("the Regensburg or Ratisbon Constitutions ... insist on the need for two cloisters so that different tasks could be kept apart, and with the second restricted to members of the order and subject to perpetual silence"; "a spatial hierarchy of privacy": church open to all, first cloister to secular clergy and select laity, second to the order only — at London it held "the kitchen, bakehouse, infirmary, dining room and library"; at Clare instead "a small court of timber framed buildings ... which included the infirmary, a dining room and latrine"); the 1538 particulars: cloister with dorter and frater, kitchen, "le priors lodging" with its own two-storey kitchen annexe, a second lodging, "le Covent Garden", a dovecote, an ortus for food and medicinal plants, six stables, a precinct wall with two gates; lead: only ~35 sq yd of lead roofing — "most roofs were of tile or slate" (LOCAL, Cessford 2023); Chester's three friaries: Grey Friars' precinct 7 acres and church "c. 60 m long, with north and south aisles, a transept, and a tower with a spire"; Black Friars 5½ acres, nave "c. 30 m long with seven bays"; White Friars' precinct "almost 80 m from north to south and 120 m from east to west" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [VCH Ches v pt 2](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/ches/vol5/pt2/pp240-246)); Santa Croce (1294; 115 m long, timber roof "without vaulting, reflecting Franciscan austerity", sixteen chapels, the Pazzi chapel AS the chapter house, a second inner cloister 1453 — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Croce,_Florence)). (4) **Nunnery** — the cloister often NORTH of the church ("a reversal of the standard monastic plan"; the nuns' choir "could take on the dimensions of a small church", the abbess's seat at the west end — CONFIRMED-digest, the Gilchrist search; Chester's nunnery kept its cloister SOUTH — VCH); Nuneaton's 93 nuns (1234) → 27 (1539) (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuneaton_Priory)); Watton's Gilbertine double house, 70 canons + 70 nuns (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watton_Priory)); Lacock's cloister "on three sides" with sacristy, chapter house and warming house under the dormitory (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lacock_Abbey)). (5) **The small cell / alien priory** — Isleham (c. 1100; a nave-chancel-apse church 30 m long, nave "about 8 m by 14 m", 6 m high; monks left 1254; "converted to a barn after the Reformation" with a cart door — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isleham_Priory_Church)); Craswall "smaller than some parish churches, with a square chapter house" (CONFIRMED-digest); cells "of two or three monks established to manage manors" (CONFIRMED-digest, the alien-priory search). (6) **The stacked plan** — Mont-Saint-Michel's Merveille (1211–28): "six salles réparties sur trois étages (40 m de hauteur)": cellar and almonry below, guests' and knights' halls in the middle, refectory and cloister on TOP — "the local geography and tight island space determined the compact, vertically-stacked arrangement" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [en](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mont-Saint-Michel_Abbey) + [fr](https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbaye_du_Mont-Saint-Michel) Wikipedia). Prosperity and decline by COUNT: Rievaulx 140 monks + 500 lay brothers under Aelred → 14 + 3 by 1381 → abbot + 21 monks with 102 lay employees in 72 buildings at 1538; Cluny "never numbered more than 60"; Cleeve 26 (1297) → 16 (1536); Le Thoronet ≤ 25 (13th c.) → 4 (1433); Sénanque "about a dozen" (1509); Alcobaça "almost 1,000" by 1300 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, the pages above). Ruin and re-use (DW law 5 at its richest): the church demolished and the claustral ranges made a house (Lacock — "retained the cloisters and the medieval basement largely unaltered while constructing another story above"; Newstead; Forde 1649–58 with "the small loggia"; London Charterhouse 1545 → Sutton's Hospital 1611 for 80 pensioners + 40 boys); the church bought by the town as its parish church "for the value of its bells and lead roof", £453 (Tewkesbury); the friary nave made "a fayer and large halle" by the mayor (Norwich 1540–44); the priory church made a barn (Isleham); the site made a wireworks (Tintern 1568), a paper mill (Fontenay 1791), a glassworks then a prison to 2023 (Clairvaux), a Protestant seminary (Maulbronn 1556), stone quarried to 1813–23 (Cluny: 8–10 % survives); Bavaria dissolved "around 160 monasteries" in 1802–03 with 4,500 religious pensioned, churches "converted into a parish church" (CONFIRMED-digest, the Säkularisation search); Fountains Hall (1597) built of abbey stone; Gloucester's infirmary fragments became "the Babylon" housing "choristers, almspeople, and poor widows".

**(b) Measured.** Cloister: Durham garth "approximately 145 ft square", alleys "about 15 ft wide", eleven bays per alley, the earlier cloister "about 115 ft square" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED + PARENT-VERIFIED 2026-08-23 verbatim, VCH Durham — the only measured WALK WIDTH the lane found: 4.6 m; the CIRC addendum's Rochester 2.5 → ~4 m brackets it); Rievaulx "about 42 metres (140 feet) square" (EH; PARENT-VERIFIED 2026-08-23); Fontenay 36 × 38 m; Le Thoronet "about thirty meters on a side", "an elongated trapezoid", once with a second storey of galleries; Alcobaça's Cloister of Silence perimeter 203 m (≈ 51 m a side), average height 5 m; Certosa di Pavia great cloister c. 125 × 100 m; Canterbury 8 bays a side + corners = 36 bays (1394–1414; carrels glazed c. 1470; lavatorium basins in bays 22–23); Gloucester south walk 10 bays × 2 carrels = 20 carrels, a separately vaulted lavatorium in the north walk opposite the refectory stair; Witham: cloister-arcade buttresses c. 0.60 m square at 2.21 m face-to-face (LOCAL), floor tiles in situ 0.3 m down. Church: St Gall 91.44 m (nave 12, aisles 6); Cluny III 187 m, vault 33 m; Fountains 300 ft, 11-bay aisles, Huby's tower 160 ft; Tintern 228 ft, transept 150 ft, six-bay nave, four-bay presbytery; Alcobaça 106 m × 22 m (central nave 17 m), 20 m high, transept 52 m; Fontenay nave 66 × 8 m, transept 19 m; Le Thoronet 40 × 20 m, tower > 30 m; Norwich Blackfriars 265 ft; Chester Grey Friars 60 m, Black Friars nave 30 m/7 bays; Santa Croce 115 m; Isleham 30 m, nave 8 × 14 × 6 m; Mount Grace church four bays + two. Ranges: Durham dorter sub-vault "about 194 ft. long and 39 ft. wide internally ... vaulted in twelve bays" (PARENT-VERIFIED 2026-08-23), 15 ft to the crown, the dorter over with a 41-ft roof span (1398–1404), chapter house 78 ft 6 in × 34 ft 6 in (vault 44 ft), frater "about 106 ft long" × 32 ft over a 3-aisled sub-vault 7 ft 6 in high, great kitchen octagon 36 ft 8 in across with a 14-ft lantern, prior's solar 62 × 22 ft, rere-dorter ~68 × 30 ft, slype "about 5 ft" deep [sic — probably wide], first dorter sub-vault 38 × 23 ft; Alcobaça dormitory 66.5 × 21.5–17.5 m (~1,300 m²), refectory 29 × 21.5 m (~620 m²), chapter house 17.5 m square "200 monges", kitchen 29 × 6.5 × 18 m high with a 3 × 8 m hearth 25 m high; Fountains infirmary hall 171 × 69 ft, its chapel 46½ × 23 ft, kitchen 50 × 38 ft; Tintern infirmary 107 × 54 ft "in cubicles"; Cleeve rere-dorter 19.7 m; Westminster chapter house an octagon of 60 ft diagonals (1246–55), "tiered seating for up to eighty monks", the Commons met there in the 14th c., a records store 1540–1863; Le Thoronet sacristy 2 m high × 3 m wide × 4 m long and armarium 3 × 3 m (the smallest two storage cells measured in this tranche); Cambridge west range 7 m internal; Gloucester abbot's tower c. 35 ft square; Mount Grace guest house 4 cells + 2 upper floors; the Merveille 40 m high, 6 halls on 3 floors. Precincts: Fountains 70 acres, wall 11 ft; Rievaulx 37 ha; Gloucester 13 acres; Chester friaries 7 / 5½ acres / 80 × 120 m; Poblet 18 ha, wall 608 m × 11 m × 2 m; Witham's precinct field "some 320 m long and 180 m" (LOCAL). NOT FOUND (the cap bit): any measured English cloister-walk width other than Durham; the Mount Grace cell plan in figures (Coppack's monograph not online); Westminster's dorter and garth; the Cessford figures (digits lost in extraction).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The plan is not universal: Glastonbury had "no west range" (the abbots' palace south-west of the cloister instead — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Glastonbury Abbey Archaeology](https://research.reading.ac.uk/glastonburyabbeyarchaeology/digital/the-cloister-c-1150s/monastic-life/)); Sénanque's "liturgical east end faces north, as the narrow and secluded valley offered no space" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A9nanque_Abbey)); Carthusian churches sit on any side of the great cloister (Witham LOCAL); nunneries flip the cloister north; Le Thoronet's cloister is a trapezoid; the Merveille stacks the cloister on the ROOF. The grammar's claustral parti must carry `church_side ∈ {N, S, E, W}`, `west_range: bool`, `orientation: FREE` under site pressure. (ii) The friary cloister is SIMPLER than its church ("the cloister design and style emphasise simplicity" vs a church that was "flamboyant" — LOCAL, Cessford) and may be two cloisters or a timber court — the friary is a PREACHING HALL with domestic ranges, not a monastery with a smaller garth. (iii) The negation searches ("friary no cloister"; "monastery no chapter house / no night stair"; "small priory two ranges") returned no documented cloister-less friary and no chapter-house-less monastery — but DID return the two-or-three-monk manorial CELL, which has neither: the floor of this family is a CHURCH + A HOUSE (Isleham: one church, now a barn), and Craswall's priory is "smaller than some parish churches". (iv) Lead: the Cambridge friary's roofs were mostly tile/slate (a prosperity fossil — lead is the abbey grade). (v) Counts refute any "monastery = N monks" prior: 4 to 1,000 at the same parti.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** THE CLOISTER WALK = `GALLERY{CLOISTER_WALK; enclosure OPEN_ONE_SIDE (arcaded) → ENCLOSED (glazed c. 1470 Canterbury; fan-vaulted 1351–1412 Gloucester); level GRADE; ring}`: width 4.6 m (Durham), 11 bays a side (Durham) / 8 + corners (Canterbury) / 10 (Gloucester south), garth 30–51 m a side at the abbey grade, 125 × 100 m at the Carthusian ceiling; it is simultaneously the house's CORRIDOR (every range opens off it), its study hall (carrels: 20 on Gloucester's south walk, two per bay; Durham's wainscotted carrels "three per window" in the north alley; Canterbury's cubicles c. 1470; Glastonbury's 15th-c. "individual booths" in the north walk), its wash-place (the lavatorium in the walk opposite the refectory door — Durham, Gloucester, Canterbury's circular two-storey lavatorium tower, Maulbronn's fountain house) and its processional ring — FOUR functions on one cell, which is why the addendum's GALLERY class needs a `functions[]` list, not one role. The NIGHT STAIR = `STAIR_HALL{direction DOWN from the dorter into the transept}` (Hexham's survives at the foot of a blocked doorway; Maulbronn: "a staircase on the east side of the cloister leads to the monks' dormitory" = the DAY stair) — width NOT FOUND. The SLYPE = `CORRIDOR{CONNECTING}` between chapter house and transept/infirmary ("about 5 ft" at Durham; Gloucester's "passage from the cloister to the former Infirmary"), with the library OVER it (Durham 1414–15) — the first measured medieval CORRIDOR in this tranche, and a corridor BY PROGRAM (the addendum's §3 C1: the cell is early, the plan's licence is late). The friary's WALKING PLACE = `CROSS_PASSAGE{HALL}` THROUGH the church between nave and choir, from the street to the cloister, with the tower over it (Norwich; Mount Grace's Carthusian church has the same cross-passage with the tower added over it in the 1420s) — a second family member of the addendum's GATE-with-room-over. The Carthusian cell = a private two-storey house off the walk with a food hatch (PLAUSIBLE — the hatch search was refused) and a garden; the water tower in the garth = `VERTICAL{SHAFT}` + piped `CHUTE`. The infirmary = `THROUGH_ROOM` aisled hall with the chapel on the axis (§6-bis; Fountains 171 × 69 ft; Gloucester six bays; Tintern's cubicles). The stacked plan (Merveille) = `STAIR_HALL` as the house's spine, three floors. Gates: two per precinct (Cambridge), three (Gloucester), a drawbridge gate and five towers (Maulbronn), an 11-ft wall (Fountains) — `LOBBY{GATE}` + `EXTERIOR_WALK{COURT_RING}` (inner court, outer court — Mount Grace's Inner Court with the guest house and service ranges).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** The SACRISTY = `CLOSET{SACRISTY}` off the transept/east walk (Le Thoronet 2 × 3 × 4 m; Lacock's under the dormitory; Tintern's "book room and sacristy" c. 1300) — `lightReq: ANY`, adjacency `REQUIRED_NEAR(CHOIR)`, fixtures press/chest/aumbry/piscina; the ARMARIUM / BOOK ROOM = `CLOSET{ARMARIUM}` a recess or cell in the east walk beside the chapter house (Le Thoronet 3 × 3 m; Tintern's book room; Witham's "Book Store" beside the chapter house — family M/O: the library's floor state is THIS closet); the LIBRARY over the slype (Durham 1414–15) = the first-floor library cell; the TREASURY (Gloucester: "the library sits above the treasury"); the CELLARIUM = `STORE{CELLARIUM}` — the west range's ground floor, vaulted, 300 ft at Fountains with the lay brothers over, = the addendum's `STORE{CELL_BELOW_LODGING}` at abbey scale; the REFECTORY UNDERCROFT = `CELLAR/UNDERCROFT` (Durham's 3-aisled 7 ft 6 in vault; Gloucester's "central row of piers"; the Merveille's "wine cellar with thick robust walls"); the KITCHEN as a DETACHED or corner MACHINE (Durham's octagon with a 14-ft lantern; Alcobaça's 18-m-high kitchen with a 3 × 8 m hearth and a conduit through it — `lightReq: LANTERN`, `FIRE_ISOLATED`); the WARMING HOUSE (calefactory — the one heated room; Fontenay's caldarium; Sénanque's "small calefactory"); the GRANARY / Fruchtkasten (Maulbronn, 13th c., rebuilt 1580) and the TITHE BARN (Le Thoronet lists one) in the outer court; the dovecote, stables (six stalls), the ortus; the infirmary's own kitchen (Fountains 50 × 38 ft); the prior's lodging's "two-storey kitchen annexe, an adjacent storeroom and a garden" (Cambridge 1538 — a HOUSE inside the precinct with its own service set); the charnel (family E) and the guest house's cells. Adjacency laws the evidence states: lavatorium OPPOSITE the refectory door (three houses); kitchen at the refectory's corner (type plan; Durham); library OVER the slype / treasury; cellarium UNDER the lay brothers; sacristy BETWEEN transept and chapter house; the second cloister's functions = the private set (kitchen, bakehouse, infirmary, dining, library — London Austin friars).

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: CLAUSTRAL{order_variant ∈ {BENEDICTINE, CISTERCIAN(lay-brothers' west range, remote, industrial), CARTHUSIAN(great cloister of cells), MENDICANT(preaching nave + walking place + one or two small cloisters, urban plot), NUNNERY(cloister N, nuns' choir), CELL(church + house), STACKED(Merveille)}; church_side; west_range: bool; garth_side_m: 30–51 (125 × 100 Carthusian ceiling)}`. `functions[]` (floor → ceiling): CHURCH (with CHOIR_SCREEN/PULPITUM — family G; lay/monks division), CLOISTER_WALK, CHAPTER_HOUSE{form: RECT 2-bay vaulted (Durham 78 × 34 ft) | SQUARE (Alcobaça 17.5 m, Craswall) | OCTAGON (Westminster 60 ft, 80 seats) | APSIDAL; seats: tiered}, SACRISTY, ARMARIUM, SLYPE, DAY_ROOM, DORTER{over east range; length 66–194 ft/m; cubicles (Thoronet: "a monk slept in front of each window")}, RERE_DORTER, NIGHT_STAIR, DAY_STAIR, WARMING_HOUSE, REFECTORY{over undercroft; reader's pulpit}, KITCHEN{octagon|corner|detached}, CELLARIUM, LAY_BROTHERS_RANGE, LAVATORIUM{in-walk | tower}, CARRELS{n}, LIBRARY{over slype}, TREASURY, INFIRMARY_HALL{+chapel, +kitchen, +little cloister}, ABBOT'S/PRIOR'S LODGING{tower | house with own kitchen}, GUEST_HOUSE{cells + suites}, ALMONRY, GATEHOUSE{1–3}, PRECINCT_WALL{+towers}, WATER_SYSTEM{conduit, water tower, culverted stream}, OUTER_COURT{barn, granary, mill, bakehouse, brewhouse, smithy, cooperage, dovecote, stables, ponds, forge}, PREACHING_YARD (mendicant), CELL_HOUSES{n: 12–25; 2 storeys; garden; hatch} (Carthusian), SECOND_CLOISTER. `fixtures[]`: ALTAR_FOCUS ×n (13 altars at Chester's nunnery), CHOIR_STALLS, CHAPTER_BENCH (tiered; Cambridge's bench foundations "widened"), READING_PULPIT (refectory), LAVER_BASINS, CARREL_DESK, BOOK_PRESS, CONDUIT_HEAD, CHAPTER_DOOR (Westminster's 1050s door), FLOOR_TILES (in situ at Witham 0.3 m down — a ruin-state fixture). Structural buckets: `vaulted{rib|fan}`, `undercroft`, `stackedRanges`, `precinctWalled{height 3.4 m}`, `waterEngineered`, `leadRoof | tileRoof` (a prosperity bucket), `tallSpan{41 ft roof}`, `octagonLantern`. Licensing: tier town+ (one house), city (several orders — the catalog's "Different religious orders" is the friary + monastery + nunnery mix the evidence shows in Chester: abbey + nunnery + three friaries), metropolis (5–10); order variant × culture (mendicant = urban, post-1220; Cistercian = remote; Carthusian = rare — nine successful English houses); prosperity by ENDOWMENT (the §Σ engine gap — Nuneaton's £253 net in 1535; Rievaulx's 6,000 acres; Certosa's 2,325 ha) with COUNT as the wear grade (4 → 1,000); era (the claustral plan is 9th c.–1540 in England, to 1803/1834 on the continent; the re-use states after). `exclusiveGroup: religiousCenter` is WRONG at city tier for this family (§Σ item 3): every English cathedral city also had friaries. HOME: `ROOM_KINDS.cells/quarters/hall/kitchen/cellar/store/reading/stacks/records` exist; NO TYPED HOME for the cloister walk, chapter house, slype, night stair, lavatorium, carrels, cellarium, infirmary hall, gatehouse, precinct, water system, or the cell-house; the `faith` template (nave/sanctuary/vestry) covers only the church. VERDICT: OWN BUILDING (a PRECINCT of buildings) at every tier it appears; the floor below town is the manorial CELL (church + house) and the HOSTED school/hospital inside the precinct (the catalog's own "May operate hospital/school" — families K and R-INST-4 inherit the claustral host).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) The claustral plan is the tranche's one COURT parti — the addendum's `EXTERIOR_WALK{COURT_RING}` with a roofed ring (`GALLERY{CLOISTER_WALK}`) that is corridor, study, wash-place and procession at once: the GALLERY class needs `functions[]`. (2) A PRECINCT is a settlement-within-a-settlement (walls, gates, courts, water, industry, a cemetery, a charnel) — the engine's one-lot-one-institution model cannot hold 72 buildings (Rievaulx 1538); this family forces the `PRECINCT` container that R-INST-1's castle and palace also need. (3) The slype is a measured CORRIDOR by program (12th c.) — the addendum's licence correction holds. (4) The cloister-of-cells (Carthusian) is a HOUSING parti — a ring of two-storey houses with gardens on a shared walk: the DWR1A unit-system yard at faith scale. (5) Re-use is the richest in the register: house / parish church / barn / hall / hospital / school / mill / prison / seminary — DW law 5 needs a RE-USE enum on the precinct, not just on rooms.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** English: dissolved 1536–40 in one stroke — every precinct is a ruin, a house or a parish church; the cathedral-priory (Durham, Gloucester, Canterbury) fused families F and G. Continental: the plan lives to 1803 (Bavaria), 1834 (Portugal), 1835 (Spain) and beyond; the Cistercian abbey is an industrial estate (Fontenay's forge, Maulbronn's ponds, Alcobaça's conduit); the Italian friary carries the city's art (Santa Croce's sixteen chapels); the charterhouse grows to 125 × 100 m (Pavia); the Merveille stacks the plan vertically on a rock; the nunnery flips it. Beyond: the monastery-with-cells (lavra), the court-madrasa (family L), the temple precinct with dormitories and a kitchen are the same `PRECINCT + COURT_RING + CELLS` parti — `CLAUSTRAL` covers them with the church swapped for the focus building.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** None (the "scholarly" function at metropolis → families K/L/M/O by the hosted pointer; magical orders → R-INST-5).

## §8 · FAMILY G — the cathedral and its close: Cathedral (10,000+ only) (city L1747 "Bishop's seat. Requires 10,000+ population minimum. Defines major city status.", `exclusiveGroup: 'religiousCenter'`) · Great cathedral (metropolis L2306 "Metropolitan cathedral. Seat of the highest regional religious authority. Pilgrimage destination.")

*Provenance: sub-lane G (killed at ~06:16Z after 80 tool calls; its last search was REFUSED by the 200-cap; Mapping Gothic France's building pages are JavaScript-rendered and returned "Loading…" shells to every fetch — the French measured-plan source of record is UNREACHED). The parent read Bond, *English Cathedrals Illustrated* (1899; LOCAL `RINST3-G-bond-english-cathedrals.txt`, Gutenberg #63739 via the pglaf mirror) for the passages cited LOCAL.*

**(a) Analogue.** The cathedral is family C's BASILICAN parti with FOUR additions the evidence dates: (1) the ENCLOSED CHOIR with its screen — "a pulpitum is a massive screen that divides the choir from the nave and ambulatory ... a central passage, leading immediately into the choir stalls ... an internal stair within the thickness of the pulpitum" to "a broad upper platform, which commonly supports the cathedral organ"; the rood screen "one bay to the west of the pulpitum" with "the main nave altar for the use of lay worshippers ... set against its western face"; pulpita at York, Canterbury, Exeter, Southwell, Glasgow; "subsidiary altars either side of their central passages"; removed at Ely (1770) and Amiens (1755) (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Pulpitum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulpitum)); Chichester's screen of 1508–36 "occupying the whole of the crossing, and containing chantries"; Exeter's "built on the choir-screen and separating choir from nave" — the Puritans' "vast whitewashed wall" that made two churches of it, Independents in the nave and Presbyterians in the choir (LOCAL, Bond pp. 63–64, 100); stalls: Lincoln 92 misericords (1370s), Norwich and New College 62, Wells' chapter house 51 stalls, Amiens 120 (1508–19), Cologne 84 (CONFIRMED-digest; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Wikipedia Amiens/Cologne/Wells). (2) the AMBULATORY and shrine circuit — "Saint-worship increased; pilgrimages increased; pilgrims came in thousands and tens of thousands. They could not be accommodated in the crypts as before: room had to be found on the floor of the choir for shrines transferred from the crypt; and aisles had to be constructed round the shrines, that there might be a free passage, and no dangerous block in the stream of pilgrims" (LOCAL, Bond p. viii); the ambulatory = "the processional way around the east end of a cathedral ... behind the high altar", French 11th c., English by the 13th (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Ambulatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambulatory)); Saint-Denis 1140–44 "double ambulatory with five radiating chapels" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Saint-Denis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_Saint-Denis)); Amiens' seven radiating chapels; Santiago's ambulatory with five chapels and "galleries above the side aisles around the church"; Toledo's double ambulatory; Rochester's "Pilgrim Steps ... so worn by medieval feet that before 1897 they had to have wooden treads added" and its "Pilgrim Door" in the north transept; Durham's shrine "behind the high altar" and the Chapel of the Nine Altars "in which many monks could say the Daily Office simultaneously"; Chichester's shrine "ordered to be destroyed in 1538"; Canterbury's Trinity Chapel (1184) for the translated tomb; Ely's Octagon (after the 1322 crossing collapse) and its Lady Chapel 100 × 46 ft (all SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, the Wikipedia cathedral pages cited in (b)). (3) the CHAPTER HOUSE — "rectangular, tending towards the square, but octagonal and other near-circular plans are an English speciality"; "seating around, often built into, all the walls ... with the central space left open"; larger ones "designed with vestibules for attendants and those waiting to be called" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Chapter house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapter_house)); "secular canons had as a rule no cloister, they preferred a polygonal chapter-house, as at Lincoln, Beverley, Lichfield, Salisbury, Wells, Elgin, Southwell, York" (CONFIRMED-digest, Ditchfield/Bond); Bristol's oblong chapter house "71 by 25 feet" for six canons (LOCAL, Bond p. 3); Westminster's octagon 60 ft, 80 monks, the Commons' meeting place in the 14th c., a State records store 1540–1863 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [westminster-abbey.org](https://www.westminster-abbey.org/history/explore-our-history/chapter-house)). (4) the CLOSE — a walled precinct of houses: "Not being served by monks, they [the secular cathedrals] required none of the monastic buildings"; a secular cathedral "sometimes have a cloister, but this was added more as an ornament" (CONFIRMED-digest); Chichester's Tudor "irregular three-sided cloister ... in a quite abnormal position encircling the south transept. The object of it was to provide a covered way to the cathedral for the Canons, as well as for the Vicars, whose Close is hard by" (LOCAL, Bond p. 100); Wells' Vicars' Close — "about 140 metres long", 42 houses originally (22 east + 20 west), each "approximately 6.1 by 4 metres (20 by 13 ft)" per floor (PARENT-VERIFIED 2026-08-23 verbatim: "a ground floor hall of approximately 6.1 by 4 metres ... and an upper floor of the same size"; 22 east + 20 west; the hall "completed in 1348"; chapel "built between 1424 and 1430"; the Chain Gate "abutted to the hall in 1459"), the hall at the south end (1348) over a gateway, "a chapel and library at the north end" (1424–30), the Chain Gate (1459) bridging to the chapter-house stair, the street narrowed 2.7 m at the far end for false perspective (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Vicars' Close](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicars%27_Close,_Wells)); Lichfield's close "licensed to wall the Close in stone and crenellate it" (1299) with corner and interval towers, a ditch on three sides, a gate "with two towers and a portcullis" (by 1376), three Civil War sieges (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Cathedral Close, Lichfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_Close,_Lichfield)); the Bishop's Palace at Wells — begun c. 1210, a five-bay aisled great hall 1275–92, "5 metres (16 ft) crenellated walls, a moat and a drawbridge", a three-storey gatehouse 1341, 14 acres, the moat fed at 180 L/s (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Bishop's Palace, Wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishop%27s_Palace,_Wells)); Lincoln's Galilee porch "as a state-entrance for the bishop from his palace over the way" (LOCAL, Bond); Durham's Galilee "occupies the position of a porch", with the great west door "blocked ... by an altar" and a sanctuary bell in its tower (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Durham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durham_Cathedral); LOCAL, Bond p. 63); Salisbury's close "Britain's largest" (80 acres) with a detached bell tower "about 320 feet northwest of the main building" demolished 1792; Norwich's close 85 acres, "a tenth of the total area of the city", gates rebuilt after the 1272 riots; Canterbury's necessarium 145 × 25 ft with 55 seats and a kitchen 47 ft square (the monastic cathedral's claustral set = family F). The MONASTIC vs SECULAR split: "Of the 17 late-Saxon/early-medieval foundations 9 were monastic & 8 were secular"; "Monastic cathedrals were an English phenomenon ... very rare outside of England" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, LOCAL `RINST3-G-monastic-secular-cathedrals.txt`, Chisholm 2019 citing Clifton-Taylor 1967). The library and school: Hereford's chained library "located over the cloister", ~1,500 chained books, 1611 presses (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Hereford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hereford_Cathedral)); Wells' library 1424–30 over the east cloister walk; Rochester's chapter room "now serves as the cathedral library"; Exeter's 36 Anglo-Saxon charters; Wren's library in Lincoln's cloister (1674). Prosperity ceiling: Salisbury "70,000 tons of stone, 3,000 tons of timber and 450 tons of lead", spire 404 ft by 1330 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Salisbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salisbury_Cathedral)). Decline and ruin: towers and spires FALL — Ely's crossing 1322 (from digging the Lady Chapel's foundations), Lincoln's spire 1548, Norwich's 1362 and 1463 ("in flames it collapsed through the nave roof"), Chichester's 1861 ("the rubble cores of the columns ... had become dust"), Hereford's west tower 1786, Beauvais 1284 and 1573, Ribe's tower 1283 ("into the church and unto the streets"), Bourges' north tower 1506, Cologne's crane left 400 years; the nave as a market ("Paul's Walk" — a 1381–1404 decree against selling "wares, as if it were a public market"), as a cavalry stable (1640s–50s), as a wall-divided double church (Exeter), as a quarry (Carlisle 1646: "the nave was thus reduced to just two bays in length" for castle stone); St Davids' palace lead stripped 1536, "beyond repair" by 1678 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, the pages cited). Siting ceiling: the cathedral CITY can be a VILLAGE — St Davids 1,751 people (2021), St Asaph 3,485; the catalog's "10,000+" is a status threshold the English register does not observe (the see, not the town, makes the cathedral).

**(b) Measured.** English (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Wikipedia pages, fetched 2026-08-23 — P1c re-verifies any figure the grammar consumes): Salisbury 442 ft long, spire 404 ft (332 steps to 225 ft), cloister "the largest in England" (CONFIRMED-digest: "58 meters by 190 feet square" — a garbled unit pair; read as ≈ 58 m square), chapter house 58 ft across and 52 ft high (1270–79), close 80 acres · Old St Paul's 586 × 100 ft (290 ft across the transepts), spire 489 ft (Hooke measured ~264 ft in 1664 — CONTESTED), chapter house octagonal c. 1332 · Wells 126.5 m long, nave 67 ft (20 m) high "notably low", west front 100 × 147 ft with 300 figures, chapter house octagon with 51 stalls and 32 ribs, 1275–1310 · Lincoln 147 m long, 24 m wide, central tower 271 ft (1307–11), spire "reputedly 525 ft" (lost 1548), decagonal chapter house with 20 ribs · Canterbury 525 × 154 ft, Bell Harry 235 ft, Ernulf's east arm 198 ft (1096–1126), Lanfranc's nave of nine bays · York 524.5 × 222 ft, tower 235 ft, choir 102 ft high, east window 77 × 32 ft, nave 45 ft broad and NOT vaulted in stone ("no English architect ever dared a vault 77 ft wide ... the York people did not even venture to vault a nave 45 ft broad"; Gerona's 73-ft span — LOCAL, Bond p. 57), octagonal chapter house with no central column · Durham 469 ft interior, 1093–1133 · Ely 164 m, nave > 75 m, west tower 66 m, Octagon 23 m wide × 52 m high (43 m to the boss), Lady Chapel 100 × 46 ft · Norwich 461 × 177 ft, nave 14 bays, spire 315 ft, close 85 acres · Hereford 342 ft exterior / 326 interior, nave 73 ft wide × 64 ft high · Chichester 408 × 157 ft, 61 ft high, spire 277 ft, double aisles, a detached campanile (one of two in England) · Carlisle 239 × 141 ft, 72 ft high, tower 110 ft, east window 51 × 26 ft, nave cut to two bays 1646 · Rochester: the 604 church 42 × 28 ft with walls 2 ft 4 in (LOCAL, Bond p. 198: "an apse, but neither aisles nor transepts"), Gundulf's transepts "120 feet long, but only 14 feet wide", Gundulf tower 65 ft · Ripon's aisleless nave 40 ft broad; Bristol's chapter house 71 × 25 ft; Winchester's "original" Saxon east limb 23 ft square, transept 88 ft (LOCAL, Bond) · St Albans nave 292 ft, total 520 ft inside, Winchester longer by 2 yards (LOCAL, Bond p. 237) · Exeter's vault "about 96 m (315 ft)" unbroken, bishop's throne 18 m (1312–16), minstrels' gallery c. 1360 · Westminster chapter house 60 ft octagon; Vicars' Close 140 m, houses 6.1 × 4 m; Wells palace walls 5 m. Continental: Chartres 130 m long, nave 16.4 m wide × 37 m high, 10,875 m², towers 105/113 m · Amiens 145 × 70 m, vault 42.30 m, flèche 112.70 m, 200,000 m³, labyrinth 240 m · Reims 149.17 m, towers 81 m (120 planned), bourdon 10 t · Beauvais choir 48.5 m (47.5) under vault, 72.5 × 67.2 m, nave 16 m wide, tower 153 m (1569–73), "the planned nave was never constructed" · Bourges 118 × 41 m, nave 37 m, inner aisle 21.3 m, outer 8.6 m, 6,200 m², NO transepts, lower church 6 m deep · Saint-Denis 108 m, nave 29 m, narthex 34 × 20 m · Cologne 144.58 × 86.25 m, nave 43.35 m, aisles 18 m, towers 157 m, 7,914 m², 10,000 m² of glass, 84 misericords · Milan 158.6 × 92 m, nave ~45 m, columns 24.5 m, five aisles on 40 pillars, "40,000" capacity, 135 spires · Seville 135 × 100 m (Gothic part 126 × 76), nave 36–42 m, Giralda 104.5 m on a 13-m base, the mosque court kept as the Patio de los Naranjos · Toledo 120 × 59 × 44.5 m, five naves, double ambulatory · Santiago 97 × 70 m, 22 m high (75 max), nave 11 bays, transept 6, 5 radiating chapels · Córdoba 180 × 130 m, ~850 columns, minaret 47 m · Aachen octagon 14.46 m diameter, 31 m high, in a 16-sided two-storey circuit; Gothic choir 25 × 13 × 32 m with > 1,000 m² of glass · Frauenkirche Munich 109 × 40 m, nave 37 m, "20,000 standing people" in a town of 13,000 (a hall church; cathedral only from 1821) · Ribe's tower 62 m (1333), rebuilt 10 m lower after 1594 · Pisa baptistery 34.13 m diameter, 54.86 m high (1152–1363); Florence baptistery octagon 37.0 × 32.5 × 39 m. The sub-lane's NOT FOUND: any triforium-passage width (the search returned "a narrow passage in the thickness of the wall" only — the addendum's `CORRIDOR{MURAL}` width stays open), any ambulatory width, any pulpitum passage width, Salisbury's cloister-walk width, the Mapping Gothic France figures.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The catalog's "10,000+ only": St Davids and St Asaph are cathedral "cities" of 1,751 and 3,485 today and were villages medievally; Ribe, Ripon, Southwell, Wells are small towns — the cathedral is licensed by the SEE (a polity event), not by population; conversely London's 40–80k had ONE cathedral and 108 parishes. (ii) "No transepts" (Bourges, Sens, Senlis, Vézelay) and "no cloister" (the nine secular cathedrals "as a rule") — the cruciform-with-cloister image is the MONASTIC cathedral, "an English phenomenon". (iii) The spire figures are antiquarian: Old St Paul's 489 vs Hooke's 264 ft; Lincoln's 525 "some consider this doubtful" — the grammar takes RANGES for lost spires. (iv) Chartres' nave 16.4 m and Beauvais' 16 m vs York's 45 ft (13.7 m) unvaulted and Wells' 67-ft-low nave: the English nave is LOW and LONG, the French HIGH and SHORT (Bond's whole argument: Winchester longer than St Albans by 2 yards; Amiens 42 m high) — a CULTURE rule on proportion. (v) The hall church (Munich) and the mosque-hall (Córdoba, Seville's court) are cathedrals by later designation — the parti does not follow the status. (vi) Negation: the "cathedrals without transepts" and "secular cathedrals without cloister" searches both returned positive lists — the counterexamples are real and dated.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** The cathedral adds to family C's axis: the PULPITUM = a `wall-with-passage` thick enough to hold a `STAIR_HALL{VICE; internal}` to a `GALLERY{GALLERY_OVER; loft}` that carries the organ — the addendum's "screen that is a wall with a passage" question is answered by the evidence: it is a CELL (it has a stair inside it), not a fixture — propose `SCREEN_CELL{passage_w; stair: INTERNAL|TURRET; loft: ORGAN|ROOD|SINGING}`; with the rood screen one bay west and the nave altar against it, the cathedral's axis has TWO screens in series (the lay altar, then the monks'/canons' choir) — a `THROUGH_ROOM` with two gated thresholds. The AMBULATORY = `GALLERY{AMBULATORY; ENCLOSED; GRADE; ring}` round the choir behind the high altar with radiating chapels on its outer side (5 at Saint-Denis/Santiago, 7 at Amiens; double at Toledo, Bourges, Saint-Denis) — its engine purpose is STATED by Bond: a pilgrim stream that must not block the choir; the English alternative is the retrochoir/Lady Chapel extension (Durham's Nine Altars) with Pilgrim Steps (a `STAIR_HALL` worn to wooden treads). The TRIFORIUM/TRIBUNE = `GALLERY{level UPPER; enclosure OPEN_ONE_SIDE}` over the aisles — "wide and spacious" early (Santiago's galleries "around the church"; the Aachen two-storey circuit with the throne on the upper level) → "shallow, within the thickness of an inner wall ... not wide enough to walk along" later (= the addendum's `CORRIDOR{MURAL}` degenerating into a blind arcade — a dated wear of the class itself); "occupied by persons who came to witness various ceremonies" — a spectator gallery. The CRYPT = the undercity component whose surface joint is the choir-aisle stair (Bourges' lower church 6 m deep; Canterbury's; Old St Paul's St Faith's as a PARISH CHURCH in the crypt — a hosted institution below grade). The GALILEE = `LOBBY{PORCH}` at cathedral scale — a western chapel-porch (Durham) or a transept state-entrance for the bishop (Lincoln). The CLOSE = `EXTERIOR_WALK{COURT_RING}` walled and gated (Lichfield's portcullis; Norwich's two gates; Wells' Bishop's Eye 1450 and the Chain Gate 1459 — a BRIDGE gallery from the Vicars' hall over the street to the chapter-house stair = the addendum's `CORRIDOR{ELEVATED/BRIDGE; joint.target: OTHER_BUILDING}` in the faith register); Chichester's three-sided Tudor cloister is a `GALLERY{CLOISTER_WALK}` built PURELY as a covered way — the cloister's corridor function with no claustral program (Bond: "The object of it was to provide a covered way"). The Vicars' Close = a `STREET` of 42 identical houses closed by a hall-gate and a chapel — the DWR1A unit-system yard as a faith street. Stairs: the tower vice (332 steps to 225 ft at Salisbury); the chapter-house stair (Wells, from the north transept up to the chapter house and on through the Chain Gate).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** The TREASURY = `CLOSET{TREASURE}` in a barred tower room (Canterbury's "barred Treasury" by St Andrew's Tower; Winchester's west aisle of the south transept "walled off ... to serve as a treasury" by Henry de Blois; York's treasury and sacristy of the Curvilinear period; Gloucester's treasury UNDER the library; Westminster's Pyx Chamber off the east cloister "behind double doors with multiple locks" for the coinage standards — the addendum's `CLOSET{MUNIMENT}` bundle hosted in a church) — `lightReq: BARRED`, `approach: SINGLE`, two doors, keys divided (LOCAL, Bond pp. 38, 72, 265, 268; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Pyx Chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyx_Chamber)). The SACRISTY/VESTRY = `CLOSET{SACRISTY}` beside the choir, enlarged with "an upper story and buttresses" at Chichester (LOCAL, Bond p. 100), a "choir boys' vestry" as a second cell (Bond), the vestry with a "show-case" (post-medieval display state). The LIBRARY = a first-floor long room OVER the cloister walk (Hereford, Wells 1424–30, Lincoln 1674) or over the slype (Durham, family F) or IN the chapter house (Rochester) — `lightReq: SIDE_WINDOWS`, chained presses (1611 at Hereford: 1,500 books) — the library's cell-state is family M's `LIBRARY_ROOM{STALL|WALL}`; the cathedral school/song school hosted in the close (family K). The MUNIMENTS: Westminster's chapter house as "a repository for State records until 1863" — the faith building as the state's archive (R-INST-1's muniment room at national scale). The STABLE in the nave (a wear state, not a store). The close's houses carry their own domestic stores (DWR1A); the bishop's palace its buttery/kitchen/cellar (R-INST-1's palace family). Adjacency: treasury ADJACENT to the transept tower / over a porch; sacristy BETWEEN choir and chapter house; library OVER a walk (never at grade — damp and theft); the chapter house's vestibule as the records' antechamber.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: BASILICAN{rung: CATHEDRAL}` = family C's parti + `{CHOIR_ENCLOSED, PULPITUM(SCREEN_CELL), ROOD_SCREEN, AMBULATORY{chapels: 3–7; double: bool} | RETROCHOIR+LADY_CHAPEL (English), TRIFORIUM{walkable: bool}, CRYPT{joint: AISLE_STAIR}, CHAPTER_HOUSE{form: OCTAGON(58–60 ft)|DECAGON|OBLONG(71 × 25 ft)|SQUARE; stalls 51–80; central pier: bool; vestibule}, GALILEE, TOWER{crossing|west ×2; height 110–271 ft}, SPIRE{stone|timber-lead; 277–404 ft; lost: bool}, DETACHED_CAMPANILE (Chichester, Salisbury; the Italian norm)}`; alternates: `HALL` (Munich), `HYPOSTYLE_COURT` (Córdoba/Seville — family P's MOSQUE_COURT inherited by a cathedral), `CENTRALIZED` (Aachen — family P). The CLOSE as a `PRECINCT{wall: crenellated (licence 1299) | moat (Wells); gates 2–4 with portcullis; area 13–85 acres; members: BISHOP'S_PALACE (R-INST-1 N), DEANERY, CANONS' HOUSES, VICARS' CLOSE{houses 42; hall-gate; chapel+library}, SCHOOL (K), LIBRARY (M), CHARNEL (E), CLOISTER (ornamental at the secular grade), BELL_TOWER (detached), CEMETERY}`; at the MONASTIC grade the close IS family F's precinct (cathedral-priory). `functions[]`: NAVE (public; market/gossip/stable as wear states), CHOIR (canons/monks; stalls 51–120), PRESBYTERY, SHRINE_STATION{steps; ring}, LADY_CHAPEL{30 × 14 m}, NINE_ALTARS, CHANTRY_CHAPELS{n}, SACRISTY{+choir-boys' vestry}, TREASURY, LIBRARY{over walk}, CHAPTER_HOUSE, CLOISTER{covered way}, GALILEE, CRYPT_JOINT, TOWER_VICE, TRACING_FLOOR (the masons' loft — York, Wells; unmeasured), SANCTUARY_RING (Durham's knocker + Galilee bell — a legal fixture set). `fixtures[]`: BISHOP'S_THRONE (Exeter 18 m), HIGH_ALTAR, SHRINE{translated from crypt}, STALLS{misericords 62–120}, ORGAN (on the pulpitum), LABYRINTH (240 m), GREAT_WINDOW{77 × 32 ft}, ROSE, RELIQUARY (the Three Kings' shrine; St Erkenwald's rebuilt by three goldsmiths in a year), FRITHSTOOL/KNOCKER, CLOCK (1484), IRON_CHAIN (Amiens 1498 — a structural fixture), CRANE (Cologne: a construction fixture left 400 years — a "building in progress" state). Structural buckets: `vaulted{stone; span ≤ 45 ft England / 73 ft Gerona; height 20–48.5 m}`, `timberCeiling` (York's nave), `flyingButtress`, `towerLoad{6,397 tons}`, `spireLoad{collapse events}`, `glassWall{10,000 m²}`, `doubleAisle`, `octagonLantern`, `iron-chained`. Licensing: tier city (the catalog's 10,000+ is a STATUS rule — the evidence says a SEE event licenses it at any size; flag for the owner), metropolis ("Great cathedral" = the pilgrimage/primatial grade: ambulatory + shrine + 7 chapels + 400-ft spire); culture decides proportion (English LOW-LONG with a crossing tower; French HIGH-SHORT with twin west towers and a chevet; German hall; Italian detached campanile and baptistery; Iberian five-nave with a mosque court); era (Romanesque 1075–1150 galleries; Gothic chevet 1140+; English octagon chapter houses 1220–1330; Perpendicular screens and towers 1330–1530); prosperity by ENDOWMENT + pilgrim income ("sums which poured into the cathedral treasury" — Bond); deity rank: the engine's `major/minor/cult` could license ONLY the shrine-station count (the pilgrimage grade) — PLAUSIBLE mapping, no source scales a cathedral by the rank of its dedication. `exclusiveGroup: religiousCenter` — the cathedral city ALSO holds friaries, a nunnery and 20–108 parishes (Chester, Norwich, London): the group is wrong here (§Σ). HOME: `INTERIOR_KINDS.faith` (nave/sanctuary/vestry) is the FLOOR of this parti; NO TYPED HOME for choir, pulpitum, ambulatory, triforium, chapter house, treasury, library, galilee, close, crypt joint, tower, or the Vicars' Close. VERDICT: OWN BUILDING + OWN PRECINCT at city and metropolis; never hosted.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) The SCREEN becomes a CELL (it has a stair inside it and a loft on top) — `SCREEN_CELL` joins the §452 set as a sub-form of `CROSS_PASSAGE` (a passage THROUGH a wall-thick cell) rather than a fixture; family C's parish screen is the fixture-grade of the same thing. (2) The ambulatory is a ring GALLERY with chapels on its outer face — the validator must allow a ring cell whose joints are all on one side. (3) The triforium shows a circulation class DEGENERATING by era (walkable gallery → unwalkable blind arcade): `walkable: bool` on GALLERY. (4) A precinct of houses with a bridge-gallery to the church (the Chain Gate) — `joint.target: OTHER_BUILDING` at first-floor level, in the faith register. (5) The cathedral's licence is a polity EVENT (the see), not population — the catalog's "10,000+" is a designer's proxy; the owner may keep it as a bounded default but the grammar should accept a `see: bool` override. (6) Pilgrimage is an INCOME and a CROWD input: shrine stations, ambulatory width, Pilgrim Steps wear — a DW law-3 decline-order driver the engine lacks.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** English: long, low, with a central tower, square east end (or retrochoir), a polygonal chapter house, a walled close of canons' houses; the monastic cathedral's cloister is unique to England. French: short, high, twin-towered west front, a chevet of radiating chapels round a double ambulatory, no chapter house to speak of, no close. German: the hall church at cathedral scale (Munich), the Carolingian centralized octagon with a throne gallery (Aachen). Italian: the basilica with detached campanile (Pisa's tower 55 m) and a separate CENTRALIZED baptistery (Pisa 34 m diameter; Florence 37 m octagon) — three buildings for one institution. Iberian: five naves on a mosque plan with the ablution court kept (Seville; Córdoba's 850 columns with a cathedral inserted into the hall in 1523). Beyond: the hypostyle hall + court (Córdoba), the centralized dome with galleries (Aachen's Byzantine model), and the basilica are the three cathedral-scale partis the closed enum needs — BASILICAN, CENTRALIZED, MOSQUE_COURT(HYPOSTYLE); the hall church is a BASILICAN attribute (`aisleHeight = naveHeight`).

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** None (the "highest regional religious authority" is the engine's `religious_authority` from deity rank — that is the one existing input this family's licence can read).
