## §9 · FAMILY H — the village musician (hosted): Village musician (village L755 "A resident singer, fiddler, or piper. Plays at festivals and weddings. Also a keeper of local history through song." — Religious shelf, `tags: ['religious']`)

*Provenance: sub-lane DEHJ evidence (its negation search for H ran in its last round).*

**(a) Analogue.** No building; four HOSTS, each dated. (1) The church: minstrels "played formally during ceremonial installations of high-ranking clergy (1309 at Canterbury, 1374 at Winchester Cathedral, 1432 at Maxstoke Priory)"; "in May 1297 the minstrel Walter Lund played his harp in reverence before the tomb of Saint Richard in Chichester Cathedral"; processions "to the music of fiddles, gitterns, psalteries and wind instruments" (Middelburg 1365); religious houses paid visiting minstrels (Durham's accounts 1278–1396); a minstrels' fraternity chartered at Beverley 1555 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [earlymusicmuse.com](https://earlymusicmuse.com/minstrels-and-the-medieval-church/)); clerical condemnation ("buffoons and harlots") is "clerical fundamentalism", not policy (ibid.). (2) The church's WEST GALLERY: "wooden galleries which in the 18th century were constructed at the west end of typical churches", where the band of "flutes, clarinets, bassoons, cellos, and violins" played 1700–c. 1850 facing the altar; "most west galleries were removed during restorations in the 19th century"; an 1895 Cornwall survey found "18 out of 219 churches still had a band"; the band was "difficult for a vicar to control", the organist manageable (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia West gallery music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_gallery_music)). (3) The hall's MINSTRELS' GALLERY: "a form of balcony, often inside the great hall of a castle or manor house", musicians "sometimes discreetly hidden"; 14th c. (Berkeley) and 15th c. (Desmond Hall); Exeter Cathedral's is the rare sacred one (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Minstrels' gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minstrels%27_gallery) — the CIRC addendum's `GALLERY_OVER` "the loft over the screens — music"). (4) The alehouse/tavern and the church house: "epic binge-sessions at an alehouse or tavern"; Cromwell's 1657 decree against "fiddlers or minstrels ... taken fiddeling, and making music, in any inn, alehouse or tavern" (CONFIRMED-digest); the church house's upper floor for "feasting, and dancing" (Crowcombe c. 1515 — family E); the churchyard's "games and festivals" (Caring for God's Acre). The catalog's "keeper of local history through song" has no building either — the oral archive is a PERSON (family O notes it).

**(b) Measured.** None for the musician; the west gallery and minstrels' gallery carry no dimensions in the fetched pages (searched: "minstrels' gallery ... dimensions if" — none). Counts: 18/219 Cornish churches with a band in 1895.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** The catalog shelves the musician as RELIGIOUS; the evidence puts the medieval musician in the church only on feast days and installations, in the hall and alehouse the rest of the time, and in a church GALLERY only 1700–1850 — the religious tag is an ERA-specific truth (the west-gallery century) and a festival truth otherwise. Negation (the sub-lane's "medieval minstrel ... where performed" search): no source gives the village musician a house of his own beyond a cottage.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** Inherited from the host (§4.3 item 9): `GALLERY{GALLERY_OVER; level UPPER}` at the church's west end (era 1700–1850) or over the hall's screens (14th c.+), reached by a `STAIR_HALL{LADDER|WINDER}`; the churchyard/ground for the festival (`EXTERIOR_WALK{GROUND}`).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** None; an instrument chest is a FIXTURE of the musician's dwelling.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: NONE_HOSTED`; `hostedIn ∈ {PARISH_CHURCH.NAVE (feast-day floor) | PARISH_CHURCH.GALLERY_WEST (era 1700–1850) | HALL.GALLERY_OVER | ALEHOUSE.HALL | CHURCH_HOUSE.UPPER_HALL | CHURCHYARD.GROUND}`; `fixtures[]`: `GALLERY_WEST{era ≥ 1700; killedBy: restoration 19th c.}` (minted on the host), `BAND_BENCH`, `INSTRUMENT_CHEST` (in the dwelling). Licensing: tier village; era decides the host (the gallery is a dated mint and a dated kill); prosperity: none; culture: the continental town's civic waits (a paid band — R-INST-1's watch/council territory) is the hosted-in-the-town-hall variant. HOME: NO TYPED HOME (no building; the `faith` template has no gallery). VERDICT: **NO_BUILDING / HOSTED** — never an own building; the musician's DWELLING is an ordinary cottage (DWR1A).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** A hosted institution that mints a FIXTURE on its host (the west gallery) in one era and is killed with it — the cleanest test case for `mintedBy/killedBy` on a hosted fixture (addendum §4.3 item 8).

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** Same everywhere: the band is hosted; the German Stadtpfeifer and the Italian civic trumpeters are the council's (R-INST-1), the church organ-loft (continental, earlier than the English west gallery) is the same `GALLERY_OVER` with a machine in it. Beyond: the temple musicians' platform / the drum tower are host fixtures, not buildings.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** None (bardic performance → R-INST-4's performance hall; the Bardic college → family L).

## §10 · FAMILY I — the open-air sanctuary / the grove: Druid Circle (village L812 "A circle of druids tied to the land ... Some circles tend city gardens or hidden urban groves." — Magic shelf, `arcane|religious`) · Elder Grove Council (town L1349 "A council of senior druids ... They may maintain a hidden grove beneath the streets ... Found in cities that have made peace with nature magic." — Magic shelf)

*Provenance: sub-lane IP (killed at ~06:16Z after 73 tool calls; its one Greensted search REFUSED by the cap; Speyer/Charenton/Koepelkerk/Burntisland fetches 404'd or were cut off at the kill). The FAITH half only; magical conventions → R-INST-5; the council's meeting → R-INST-1's family A (the moot).*

**(a) Analogue.** The open-air sanctuary is a NO_BUILDING faith parti with an ENCLOSURE and a focus, in four rungs the evidence measures. (1) **The grove** — "an open place set apart for worship, most often a sacred grove or clearing in woodland rather than a roofed building"; Lucan's Massilia grove is "literary conventions" with "limited historical accuracy" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Nemeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nemeton)); "Roman historian Tacitus reporting Germanic cult practices occurred exclusively in groves, not temples"; the Roman lucus by the Vestals' house "lingered until ... the Great Fire of Rome in 64 CE"; Spoleto's two stones (late 3rd c. BC) set "punishments for profaning woods"; Baltic groves noted 1075 (Adam of Bremen) and 1326 (Peter of Dusburg); "During the Northern Crusades ... conquering Christians commonly built churches on the sites of sacred groves" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Sacred grove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_grove)); Dodona: cult "during the Late Bronze Age" with "No buildings" until a small stone temple in the 4th c. BC and a wall round the tree c. 290 BC (family N). (2) **The enclosure** — the temenos: "usually surrounded by a wall, ditch, or line of stones"; the peribolos "often just a set of marker stones ... or a light fence", later "large stone walls with gateways or gatehouses"; "The earliest sanctuaries appear to have begun as a peribolos around a sacred grove, spring, cave, or other feature, with an altar but no temple or cult image" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Temenos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temenos)); the Celtic/Belgic sanctuary from the 3rd c. BC: "roughly square precincts bounded by banks and ditches, measuring approximately 80 square meters ... screened sacred ground from view and contained minimal built structures, primarily deep ritual shafts" (Gournay-sur-Aronde, Ribemont — whose "structure was constructed from over 2,000 human long bones") (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Nemeton); the Viereckschanze: Bopfingen "83 m x 73 m" with "a 9.5 x 10 m building" inside (2nd c. BC; the article "provides no discussion of interpretive disputes regarding function (cultic versus agricultural/domestic use)" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Viereckschanze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viereckschanze)); the Romano-Celtic temple's temenos "marked off by a wall, palisade and ditch" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Romano-Celtic temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romano-Celtic_temple)). (3) **The henge and the timber/stone circle** (the "circle" of the catalog's name — a MEASURED open-air parti, prehistoric, used here as a BOUNDED POSSIBILITY of form only): Goseck (c. 4900–4700 BC; "75 metres across" to the outer edge of a V-ditch up to 1.8 m deep with an external rampart; palisade posts 2.5 m (reconstructed, 1,675 oak poles); three gates N/SW/SE, the SW and SE "align with winter solstice sunrise and sunset" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Goseck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goseck_circle)); Woodhenge (2470–2000 BC; "six concentric oval rings of postholes", outer "about 43 by 40 metres", the whole 110 m with bank and ditch; ditch 2.4 m deep, up to 12 m wide; bank 10 m wide, 1 m high; 168 post-holes up to 2 m deep, posts "as high as 7.5 metres", "up to 5 tons"; one entrance "approximately to the midsummer sunrise" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Woodhenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodhenge)); The Sanctuary (four phases: 8 posts at 4.5 m + a centre post, c. 3000 BC → 8 posts at 6 m → 11.2 m → 33 posts at 21 m → 42 sarsens at 40 m; 162 post-holes; an entrance onto the 2.5-km Kennet Avenue — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia The Sanctuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sanctuary)); Durrington Walls (ditch 5.5 m deep, 7 m at the bottom and 18 m at the top, bank 30 m wide; the Southern Circle c. 2600 BC "oriented southeast towards the sunrise on the midwinter solstice", four concentric post rings; a later palisade of "around 300 huge trunks" 1.5 m across and 7 m high; a settlement of "up to 1,000 houses and perhaps 4,000 people" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Durrington Walls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrington_Walls)); Stonehenge (the enclosure "about 360 feet (110 m)" c. 3100 BC; the sarsen circle "approximately 98 feet (30 m)", stones 4.11 m high × 2.13 × 1.06 m, ~26 tons, lintels at 4.9 m, 2600–2400 BC; trilithons 6–7.3 m, 50 tons; the Avenue "two miles (3 km)" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Stonehenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonehenge)); Avebury (henge 347.4 m across, ditch ~11 m deep with a 4-m flat base and a 9-m bank; outer circle 331.6 m, ~98 sarsens 3.6–4.2 m high, > 40 tons; inner rings 98 and 108 m; a 5.5-m central monolith; avenues c. 2400 BC; the West Kennet Avenue "25m wide", 2.5 km, "around 100 pairs" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Avebury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avebury), [West Kennet Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Kennet_Avenue)). (4) **The roofed cella in the enclosure** (the grove's ceiling — family N/P): the Romano-Celtic cella 5.1–16 m with an ambulatory 8.5–22 m "likely accommodated the Celtic practice of circumambulation" (Maiden Castle 6 × 6 m cella; Caerwent 7.5 × 7 m; Pagans Hill octagonal with a well — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Romano-Celtic temple). DECLINE AND RE-USE (DW law 5 on a NO_BUILDING): Avebury's villagers from the early 1300s "began to demolish the monument by pulling down the large standing stones and burying them in ready-dug pits" (a barber-surgeon crushed c. 1320–25); 17th–18th c. "the majority of the standing stones ... were smashed up to be used as building material" by fire and cold water; "by 1939 only 9 remained standing"; a VILLAGE grew INSIDE the henge from the early medieval period and the National Trust demolished houses within it after WWII "as they fell vacant" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Avebury); the Kennet stones "pushed over and buried, or broken up ... between the 13th and 17th centuries"; churches built on grove sites (the Northern Crusades); the grove burnt (Rome 64) or cut for a siege (Massilia). The catalog's own text gives the URBAN rungs: "city gardens or hidden urban groves" (L812) and "a hidden grove beneath the streets" (L1349) — the latter is the UNDERCITY train's (a buried garth: the addendum's `CELLAR/UNDERCROFT` with a planted floor — no historical analogue in the evidence; the nearest is the Mithraeum "built partly underground, recalling the cave", "frequently constructed within or below an existing building" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Mithraeum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithraeum)).

**(b) Measured.** Enclosures: Celtic square precinct ~80 m² (a 9-m square — number audit: "80 square meters" is tiny beside Bopfingen's 83 × 73 m = 6,000 m²; the Wikipedia figure may be a slip for 80 m a side; report both, flag); Bopfingen 83 × 73 m + 9.5 × 10 m building; Goseck 75 m, ditch 1.8 m, palisade 2.5 m; Woodhenge 110 / 43 × 40 m, ditch 2.4 × 12 m, bank 10 × 1 m, posts 7.5 m; Sanctuary 4.5 → 40 m; Durrington ditch 5.5 × 7–18 m, bank 30 m, palisade posts 1.5 × 7 m; Stonehenge 110 / 30 m, uprights 4.11 m, lintels 4.9 m, trilithons 6–7.3 m; Avebury 347.4 / 331.6 / 98 / 108 m, ditch 11 m, bank 9 m, stones 3.6–4.2 m; Kennet Avenue 25 m × 2.5 km, ~100 pairs; Dodona's wall c. 290 BC (unmeasured); Romano-Celtic cella 5.1–16 m, ambulatory 8.5–22 m. NOT FOUND: any MEDIEVAL open-air sanctuary with a measured enclosure in the European register (the medieval grove is documentary only: 1075, 1326); the Greensted HE entry (403); the Kizhi enclosure wall 300 m with a 14.4 × 2.25 m gate is the one measured FAITH ENCLOSURE of the post-medieval register (family P).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The Viereckschanze's function is DISPUTED (cultic vs farmstead) and the Wikipedia stub "provides no discussion" — the enclosure-with-a-small-building is ambiguous between a grove sanctuary and a farm; the grammar must not read every square ditch as faith. (ii) The Celtic "80 square meters" is a suspect figure (audit above). (iii) Lucan's grove is a literary topos; Tacitus's "exclusively in groves" is an ethnographer's generalisation — the medieval grove is attested by its SUPPRESSION (churches built on it; groves named in crusading documents), not by its fabric. (iv) The catalog puts the Druid Circle at VILLAGE with `religious` + `arcane` tags and the council at TOWN — the historical grove is PRE-settlement (Durrington's 4,000 people round a timber circle; Avebury's village INSIDE the henge) and then SUPPRESSED — an era rule: the open-air sanctuary is a floor that the register's churches replace, and its catalog presence is the fantasy register's choice (legitimate — bounded possibility). (v) Negation: the sub-lane's grove searches were fetches of summaries; no search for "grove with a building" vs "grove without" was possible (cap) — the contested point stands on the fetched pages.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** `EXTERIOR_WALK{GROUND}` = the sanctuary floor; `EXTERIOR_WALK{RANGE/LINEAR}` = the AVENUE (Kennet 25 m × 2.5 km with ~100 stone pairs; Stonehenge's 3 km — the largest "processional axis" in the tranche and the only one that is a LANDSCAPE feature); the ENTRANCE on a solstice line (Goseck's gates, Woodhenge's single entrance, Durrington's SE orientation) = an ORIENTED `LOBBY{GATE}` in a bank/palisade — the sanctuary's "orientedAxis" is astronomical, like family N's meridian corridor; the ring of posts/stones = a `GALLERY{ring; OPEN_TWO_SIDES}` at fixture scale (the Sanctuary's six rings; the Romano-Celtic ambulatory for circumambulation = the cloister walk's pagan ancestor); the ditch-and-bank = the ENCLOSURE (family E's churchyard bank/ditch is its dated successor: 0.18–0.60 m deep vs 11 m at Avebury); the urban rungs: a walled garden (`EXTERIOR_WALK{COURT_RING}` — Uraniborg's geometric garden is the measured learning cousin) and the buried grove (UNDERCITY joint — a stair down from a court).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** None as cells; the ritual SHAFT (Gournay, Ribemont — "deep ritual shafts") is a `VERTICAL{SHAFT}` used as a DEPOSIT store (weapons, bones) — the addendum's class with a faith use; the Romano-Celtic temple's well (Pagans Hill); the henge's buried stones (Avebury's "ready-dug pits") are a fossil of suppression, not storage.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: NO_BUILDING{kind: GROVE (trees, a spring, a tree-wall — Dodona) | ENCLOSURE (bank+ditch | palisade | stone wall; 9–350 m; gates oriented) | CIRCLE (posts 4.5–43 m | stones 30–332 m; rings 1–6) | URBAN_GARDEN (walled court) | BURIED_GARTH (→ UNDERCITY)}` with an OPTIONAL cella (family N's `TEMPLE_CELLA` floor: 5–16 m square/octagonal with an ambulatory, or the Viereckschanze's 9.5 × 10 m building). `fixtures[]`: SACRED_TREE, SPRING, ALTAR (outside any building — the temenos rule), MARKER_STONES/PERIBOLOS, POST_RING{n; h 2.5–7.5 m}, STONE_RING{n; h 3.6–4.2 m; lintels}, CENTRE_POST/MONOLITH{5.5 m}, AVENUE{w 25 m; pairs}, RITUAL_SHAFT, BANK{h 1–9 m}, DITCH{d 1.8–11 m}, SOLSTICE_GATE, BONE_STRUCTURE (Ribemont). Fossil states: `STANDING → FALLEN → BURIED (pits) → SMASHED/REUSED → BUILT_OVER (church on the grove; village in the henge)`. Structural: `earthwork`, `timberPost{earthfast}`, `megalith`. Licensing: tier village (L812) / town (L1349) by the catalog; culture: the register's HISTORICAL grove is suppressed by 1100–1400 — so in `EUROPEAN_FANTASY_BASE` the circle is a DELIBERATE survival (the owner's fantasy setting licenses it; the grammar should carry `era: PRE_SETTLEMENT | SURVIVING | SUPPRESSED` as a state); prosperity: none (the grove is cheap; the henge is a 4,000-person project); deity rank: the catalog's own desc ("forested or isolated settlements") is a SITING rule, not a rank rule. HOME: NO TYPED HOME (no lot, no interior; the engine's `faith` template cannot render a ring of posts). VERDICT: **NO_BUILDING** at both tiers (a fixture set on an enclosure), with the council HOSTED in it (R-INST-1's family A moot-on-ground) and an OPTIONAL micro-cella; the "hidden grove beneath the streets" is an UNDERCITY component by pointer.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) The first NO_BUILDING with a SOLSTICE-ORIENTED entrance and an AVENUE kilometres long — `orientedAxis` must accept astronomical targets and `EXTERIOR_WALK{RANGE}` a length beyond the settlement's lot grid. (2) A faith site that a LATER faith building REPLACES on the same spot ("churches on the sites of sacred groves") — a `supersededBy` pointer between institutions across eras, which DW law 5 (fossils) does not yet carry for NO_BUILDING sites. (3) A village INSIDE the sanctuary (Avebury) — the enclosure as a settlement boundary: the addendum's `COURT_RING` at landscape scale.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** English: the henge and stone circle (Wessex) — measured, prehistoric, suppressed and quarried from the 14th c.; the medieval English grove is absent from the record. Continental: the Celtic square sanctuary with shafts (Picardy), the Viereckschanze (Bavaria), the Roman lucus with inscribed penalties (Spoleto), the Baltic groves suppressed by the Northern Crusades (the last living groves in the European register, 11th–14th c.), the solstice-gated ring ditch (Goseck, the oldest). Beyond: the temenos with an altar and no temple (Greece) and the shrine grove with a protected forest (the Kashima Shrine's 1,500 ha — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Sacred grove) — the grove persists as a living institution outside Europe; the grammar's `era: SURVIVING` state is the owner's "made peace with nature magic" (L1349) in historical dress.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** Faith half above (enclosure, ring, avenue, altar-outside, tree/spring, the suppression history). "Regulate the seasons", "mediate disputes with wild creatures", "ecological advisors", the hidden grove's magic → R-INST-5; the council's assembly → R-INST-1 family A (hosted on the ground).

## §11 · FAMILY J — the divine healer (hosted): Healer (divine, 1st level) (village L826 "Basic healing spells. Cure Wounds (10 GP)." — Magic shelf, `tags: ['divine','healing']`, `priorityCategory: 'religion'`)

*Provenance: sub-lane DEHJ evidence (its negation search for J ran in its last round). Magical conventions → R-INST-5; the infirmary building → R-INST-4 (§6-bis gives it the infirmary-hall-with-chapel parti). This family answers one question: WHERE does a village's faith-healer work, historically, and does it license a building.*

**(a) Analogue.** Four hosts, no building. (1) The PRIEST at the bedside: "Pope Innocent IV (d. 1254) recommended summoning a priest before a physician"; the priest "administered the Eucharist to the sick and dying, which was considered powerful medicine" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wellcome Collection](https://wellcomecollection.org/stories/health-and-the-medieval-church)) — the healer's workplace is the PATIENT'S house (a mobile institution, like R-INST-2's itinerants). (2) The MONASTERY infirmary: uroscopy from the 10th c., a medicinal herb garden, the Syon herbal (c. 1517, "over 700 herbal plants"); monks "managed trauma including fractures and lacerations but referred complex procedures like trephination to experienced laymen" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, Wellcome) — family F's infirmary range; the Lateran IV (1215) rule forbade clergy in major orders "the part of surgery that involved cautery and cutting", not medicine; Honorius III (1219) extended the study prohibition; "by the end of the Middle Ages, canon law still had not prohibited the clergy from practicing medicine" (CONFIRMED-digest, [Catholic Encyclopedia, Medicine and Canon Law](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/10142a.htm)). (3) The SHRINE and WELL: "Canterbury water" sold in ampullae; St Winefride's well-chapel (c. 1512–25: an upper chapel over a well crypt with a star-shaped basin "surrounded by stone columns once linked with traceried screens", an external stair at the west end to a gallery overlooking the crypt, a bathing pool with "triple immersion" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia St Winefride's Well](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Winefride%27s_Well)) — family A's shrine-house at its ceiling, a TWO-STOREY well-chapel. (4) The CUNNING FOLK at home: "cunning folk, wise women, and charmers" were the village's lay healers c. 1400–1700, practising in their own cottages and "during the night if requested"; "some clergy were cunning men"; "as many as several thousand working in England at any given time"; eight wise women in Whitby in 1816 (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Cunning folk in Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cunning_folk_in_Britain)). The PRIEST'S HOUSE (family D) with its herb garden (Halse 1634 "nearby a herb garden") is the only faith-owned cell the healer has at village.

**(b) Measured.** None (the well-chapel's dims were not on the fetched page; the herb garden is unmeasured).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** The row is on the MAGIC shelf with `priorityCategory: religion` — its historical host is split between clergy (sacramental), monastic infirmary (medical), shrine (pilgrim) and lay cunning folk (magical-medical, often at odds with the clergy) — the catalog's "divine" merges what the register keeps apart, and at village tier only the first and fourth exist. Negation (the sub-lane's "parish priest healer ... Lateran prohibition" search) found the prohibition is narrower than commonly stated — it does NOT forbid the priest healing. No source gives a village healer a purpose-built room.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** Inherited from the host: the patient's house; the priest's house; the well-chapel's external stair and gallery over the crypt (`STAIR_HALL{EXTERNAL}` + `GALLERY{GALLERY_OVER}` — at the pilgrimage-shrine ceiling only).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `STILL_ROOM`-class HERB store hosted in the priest's house or the cottage (a FIXTURE: press and drying rack — R-INST-2's apothecary holds the cell version); the monastery's is family F's.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: NONE_HOSTED`; `hostedIn ∈ {PATIENT_DWELLING (the floor — a MOBILE institution) | PRIEST_HOUSE.STUDY/HERB_GARDEN | COTTAGE (cunning folk) | SHRINE_HOUSE/WELL_CHAPEL (pilgrim grade) | MONASTERY.INFIRMARY (→ F) | HOSPITAL (→ R-INST-4)}`; `fixtures[]`: `HERB_GARDEN`, `HERB_PRESS`, `AMPULLA_STALL` (the shrine's sale fixture), `IMMERSION_POOL` (well-chapel). Licensing: tier village; the host by culture (continental: the monastery and the hospital earlier and denser; English: the cunning folk) and era (the cunning-folk floor is 1400–1850; the monastic host dies 1536–40 in England — a dated kill of a HOST). HOME: NO TYPED HOME (the `institutionFunction: heals` → `quarters` variant room exists for a BUILDING, and this row has none). VERDICT: **NO_BUILDING / HOSTED** at village (the healer is a person in someone else's rooms); the first OWN BUILDING for healing is R-INST-4's hospital at town.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** The engine's `institutionFunction: heals` appends a `quarters` room to a building — for this row there is no building to append to; `NONE_HOSTED` must carry the function on the HOST (a hosted `quarters` in the priest's house, or none). Also: a host can DIE (the monastery at the Dissolution) and the hosted institution must re-host — `hostedIn` needs a dated fallback.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** Continental: the monastic infirmary and the town hospital (Hôtel-Dieu) are denser and earlier, so the village healer re-hosts to the monastery more often; English: the cunning folk are the village floor for four centuries. Beyond: the temple's healing hall and the incubation shrine (sleep-by-the-focus) are the well-chapel's cousin — a `SHRINE_HOUSE` with an overnight cell, a fixture set, not a new parti.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** The faith half is the above; spell conventions, "1st level", the 10 GP fee → R-INST-5.

## §12 · FAMILY K — schools: NO CATALOG ENTRY at any tier (the song school, the reading/petty school, the chantry/guild school, the grammar school, the cathedral and almonry school; learning below city tier exists in the catalog only as the priest's literacy (L762) and the monastery's "May operate hospital/school" (L1235))

*Provenance: sub-lane KL (killed at ~06:16Z after 79 tool calls; three of its last searches REFUSED by the 200-cap). The parent read Willis & Clark, *Architectural History of the University of Cambridge* vol. 3 (1886; LOCAL `RINST3-KL-willisclark-v3.txt`, archive.org OCR) and vol. 1 (Eton; LOCAL `RINST3-KL-willisclark-v1.txt`).*

**(a) Analogue.** The school is HOSTED at the floor and a SCHOOLROOM at the ceiling; the evidence dates every rung. (1) **Hosted in the church** — "there were small informal schools held in the parish church, song schools at cathedrals, almonry schools attached to monasteries, chantry schools, guild schools, preparatory grammar schools, and full grammar schools. A local grammar school intended for day pupils did not need lodgings — all that was required was a school room, but there might also be a chapel" (CONFIRMED-digest, [Historic England, Education Buildings listing selection guide](https://historicengland.org.uk/images-books/publications/dlsg-education-buildings/) — the PDF itself returned Cloudflare 403 to fetch and curl); London's schools of 1447–8 and 1456–7 with "structural or documentary evidence for any special buildings or arrangements for education ... so far lacking. Presumably much of the business of the school took place within the church itself" (LOCAL, Schofield — family C); Oxfordshire's hosts: a school "in the churchyard next to the 'priest's chambers'" (Henley 1553), in the churchyard (Banbury; later a Civil War prison), "held in the chapel on the north side of the chancel. Above was a room used as a library" (Bicester c. 1669), "in the crypt of the chapel of the old Hospital of St. John" (Magdalen School 1479, a schoolhouse begun 1480), "the council chamber" (Nixon's 1659), "the Towne house" (Charlbury 1675), "a part of the abbey, probably the guest house" (Dorchester c. 1652); the CHANTRY school — "the chantry priest, was schoolmaster" (Chipping Norton 1547; Deddington, £6 a year, "After dissolution, the school came to an end"; Burford's guild "dyd builde a chapell of Our Lady annexed to the parish church ... and dyd fynde a prest to mynyster ther, and to teache chylderne frely") (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [VCH Oxon i, Schools](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/oxon/vol1/pp457-490)); the porch chamber as school (family C's parvise: "once used as the village school" — CONFIRMED-digest); the GUILDHALL's upper floor — Stratford's guildhall (1417–c. 1420; dendro 1410–35; ground floor "a main hall, with a stone floor", upper floor "two rooms, with spine beam") housed the grammar school from the 1560s (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Stratford Guildhall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratford-upon-Avon_Guildhall)); Long Melford's Lady Chapel as a school 1670–early 18th c. (family C). (2) **The purpose-built schoolroom** — Ewelme (1437, brick; "about 38 by 18 feet", "only a subsidiary position in a complex of buildings established for charitable purposes" (the almshouse); two heated floors, "one (perhaps the upper) probably originally providing dormitory accommodation, and the other the main teaching space"; a three-bay timber master's house adjoining "with rooms for guests and entertainment"; "largely derelict in the early 19th century", reordered as a National schoolroom c. 1830 — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [VCH Oxon xviii, Ewelme](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/oxon/vol18/pp192-234); CONFIRMED-digest for the 38 × 18 ft); Wainfleet (1484; two storeys, red brick with diaper, twin octagonal three-storey angle towers — "one serving as a staircase tower on the left and one as a bell tower on the right", "similar to Eton College, Tattershall Castle and Hampton Court" — CONFIRMED-digest, [HE 1224243](https://historicengland.org.uk/listing/the-list/list-entry/1224243)); Winchester College's original "School" — 46 × 29 ft, four oak posts, "triple rows of stone seats" for the prefects, later "Seventh Chamber" when the 1683–87 School (78 × 35 ft inside, "the finest and largest school in England") replaced it (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [VCH Hants v](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/hants/vol5/pp14-19)); Eton's founder's design: "west of the gate a schoolroom 70 feet long", "a uniform breadth of 24 feet" (LOCAL, Willis & Clark i, p. 358), and the built Lower School with "Long Chamber" (the boys' dormitory, "about 166 feet" between the Head Master's chamber at one end and the Usher's at the other; "all the King's schollers and choristers shall ly in the Long Chamber and ... the Scholemaster and Usher shall lodge in their Chambers at the ends ... to preuent disorders" (1661); an external staircase to the first floor; a "vice" to the Usher's chamber; "two rows of pillars" inserted under Provost Wotton (1624–39) "to support the floor of Long Chamber"; "a new chamber for the College boys" 1506–7 — LOCAL, Willis & Clark i, pp. 430–431, 462). (3) The master's house: Ewelme's; Adderbury's college-provided lodging; Charlbury's "house and garden"; Magdalen's "rooms over the school destined for the master and usher" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, VCH Oxon i). Grades of school: "Grammar Schools: Taught Latin and Greek to students already literate"; "Petty/Reading Schools: Taught 'the three R's'"; at Adderbury "if the parishioners would pay for an usher to teach the three R's he should be lodged in the school house", 6d a quarter for English and 12d for Latin (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, VCH Oxon i). Decline: the chantry school dies with the chantry (1547–8: Deddington "came to an end"; Banbury's hospital school "broken up and its endowment dissipated"; Ewelme's almshouse school "alone in preserving a continuous existence"); the schoolroom becomes a prison (Banbury), a derelict (Ewelme), a library (Wainfleet "now Library").

**(b) Measured.** Ewelme 38 × 18 ft (≈ 11.6 × 5.5 m ≈ 63 m²; CONFIRMED-digest); Winchester School 46 × 29 ft (1394; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, VCH) → 78 × 35 ft (1687); Eton design 70 × 24 ft (LOCAL, W&C i); Long Chamber ~166 ft (LOCAL); Stratford guildhall two storeys (dims NOT FOUND — the search was refused); Wainfleet two storeys + three-storey towers (dims NOT FOUND; HE 1224243 not opened). Fixtures measured: none for forms/desks (Winchester's "tables and forms date from Elizabeth's era" — VCH; Wotton's two rows of pillars). NOT FOUND: any measured song school, petty school, or almonry school room; Orme's *Medieval Schools* (the type-study) is not online.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The purpose-built school is an ENDOWMENT artefact (Ewelme's duchess, Wykeham, Waynflete, Henry VI) — every measured schoolroom is a foundation at the ceiling of prosperity; the ordinary town grammar school of the 14th–16th c. is in the evidence only as a host (church, churchyard, guildhall, town house). (ii) A school "did not need lodgings" (day pupils) — the boarding college (Winchester, Eton) is family L's court parti, not a school. (iii) The negation search ("school held in church no schoolhouse") was the sub-lane's FIRST search — and it found the answer everywhere: the host IS the medieval norm; purpose-built schoolrooms are datable from 1437 in the fetched set. (iv) Bicester's school under a library, Stratford's under a guildhall, Ewelme's under a dormitory: the schoolroom is the GROUND floor of a two-storey cell in every built case.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** Hosted floor: inherited from the host — the church's `LOBBY{PORCH}` chamber stair, the guildhall's `STAIR_HALL{EXTERNAL}` (Stratford's jettied upper floor; Eton's external stair to Long Chamber — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED / LOCAL). Built: a single `THROUGH_ROOM` schoolroom with the master's desk at one end and forms down the sides (the Winchester "triple rows of stone seats" = a tiered `GALLERY_OVER`-less bench rank — a FIXTURE rank, not a cell); `STAIR_HALL{VICE; STAIR_TURRET}` to the master's/usher's chamber (Wainfleet's staircase tower; Eton's vice) — the school reuses the PORCH-CHAMBER idiom (room over, vice beside); a `CROSS_PASSAGE` through Eton's Lower School range ("now divided into two portions by a through passage" — post-medieval). The Long Chamber = a DORMITORY through-room with the masters' chambers at BOTH ends as guardians (the addendum's `GUARDIAN_ADJACENT` law, doubled).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** Hosted: the school's books in the church chest or the library over the porch/chancel chapel (Bicester) — fixtures of the host. Built: a `CLOSET{STUDY}` for the master; the boys' chests (a fixture); the bell turret (Wainfleet's bell tower — a FIXTURE tower); no pantry/buttery (day school) — the boarding school's are family L's. Adjacency: the school sits in the CHURCHYARD (Henley, Banbury) or beside the almshouse (Ewelme) or over the guildhall — `REQUIRED_NEAR(PARISH_CHURCH | GUILDHALL | ALMSHOUSE)`.

**(f) Typed proposal.** NO CATALOG ENTRY — proposed rows for the owner: `Song/reading school` (village+; HOSTED), `Grammar school` (town+; HOSTED → OWN BUILDING by endowment), `Cathedral/almonry school` (city; HOSTED in the close/precinct — families F/G). `parti: NONE_HOSTED` (floor) → `SCHOOLROOM` (a `HALL`-family single cell 38 × 18 to 78 × 35 ft, one or two storeys, master's chamber over or beside, stair turret, bell) → `COLLEGIATE_COURT` (the boarding school: family L). `hostedIn ∈ {PARISH_CHURCH.NAVE | .PORCH_CHAMBER | .CHANCEL_CHAPEL | .CHURCHYARD_HOUSE | CHANTRY_PRIEST_HOUSE | GUILDHALL.UPPER | TOWN_HOUSE | COUNCIL_CHAMBER | MONASTERY.ALMONRY/GUEST_HOUSE | HOSPITAL.CHAPEL_CRYPT | ALMSHOUSE}`. `fixtures[]`: MASTER_DESK{raised}, FORMS{rows 2–3; stone|oak}, USHER_DESK, BOOK_CHEST, BELL, PILLARS (a floor-support fossil), HAT_PEGS. Structural: `timberFrame | brick{diaper}`, `twoStorey{school below; dormitory|master above}`, `stairTower`. Licensing: tier village (hosted, by the priest — the catalog's L762 desc is the floor), town (a chantry/guild school hosted; an endowed schoolroom by a FOUNDATION EVENT — the same endowment input §Σ names), city (cathedral school in the close; the college); era (chantry/guild schools ≤ 1547 — a dated KILL of the host by the Chantries Act; endowed schoolrooms 1437+; the purpose-built town grammar school mostly 1550–1700 in the Oxfordshire set: Thame 1569, Adderbury 1589, Steeple Aston 1639); culture: the continental cathedral/collegiate-church school and the Latin school in the town hall are the same hosts. HOME: `INTERIOR_KINDS.learning` = reading + stacks (+ study) — a LIBRARY template, not a schoolroom; NO TYPED HOME for the form-ranked schoolroom; `ROOM_KINDS.hall` is the nearest. VERDICT: **HOSTED** at village and (mostly) town; **OWN BUILDING** only by endowment at town/city; the boarding school is family L.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) A hosted institution whose HOST is killed by a dated event (the chantry, 1547) — the second `hostedIn` fallback case (family J's monastery was the first). (2) The schoolroom-under-dormitory / under-library two-storey cell — `REQUIRED_BELOW` in the learning register. (3) The catalog gap is real and cheap to fill: three rows, all HOSTED by default, with an endowment-licensed building.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** English: the chantry and guild school in the church, the endowed brick schoolroom from 1437, the boarding college (L). Continental: the cathedral school and the town Latin school (often in the town hall — R-INST-1's council hall hosts it), the Jesuit college from 1551 (L: Collegio Romano "large classrooms"). Beyond: the temple school / the madrasa's teaching iwan (L) — learning hosted in the faith building is the floor everywhere; the detached schoolroom is an endowment everywhere.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** None (the Academy of magic → family L + R-INST-5).

---

## §13 · FAMILY L — the college, the university and the academy: Bardic college (city L2101 "Formal musical education. Amphitheater, dormitories. 10,000+ for small campus." — Entertainment shelf, `education` tag) · Academy of magic (metropolis L2332 "Formal institution of arcane learning. Full curriculum, research facilities, visiting scholars." — Magic shelf; LEARNING half only) · (NO CATALOG ENTRY) university / secular college

*Provenance: sub-lane KL evidence; LOCAL Willis & Clark vol. 3 (the parent read Part III "The Component Parts of a College": the collegiate plan, the gateway, the chambers and studies, the hall, the library) and vol. 1 (Eton/King's).*

**(a) Analogue.** THREE partis, in historical order. (1) **The hosted university** — "Initially medieval universities did not have physical facilities ... Classes were taught wherever space was available, such as churches and homes"; "A university was not a physical space but a collection of individuals banded together as a universitas"; "Soon, however, universities began to rent, buy or construct buildings" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Medieval university](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_university)); Oxford's scholars "lodged at first in houses already in existence, purchased by the founder" (LOCAL, W&C iii, p. 248): Tackley's Inn (early 14th c., a five-bay vaulted cellar, a two-bay hall roof; "occupied by Adam de Brome in 1324 as part of the accommodation of his new society, later Oriel College" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [RCHME Oxford, Secular](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/rchme/oxon/pp156-188)) — the DWR1A burgage house as a hall of residence; Cambridge's "hostels and inns" where "scholars lodged with townspeople or rented houses communally under a principal" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [RCHME Cambridge, Sectional Preface](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/rchme/cambs/lxxii-lxxxviii)); the university's own FIRST buildings: Oxford's Congregation House (c. 1320–27, 45¼ × 18 ft, two storeys, a vaulted lower room, "the upper story served as a library" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [RCHME Oxford, Ecclesiastical](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/rchme/oxon/pp125-156)) built onto the parish church (St Mary's — the university HOSTED in the church, at the city grade); the Divinity School (1427–90, 86½ × 31 ft, stone vault 1480–83) with Duke Humfrey's library over it (c. 1470) and the Schools Quadrangle (1610–24, 109½ × 102 ft) whose doors are inscribed "School of Metaphysics", "School of Logic", "Schools of Languages, Geometry, and Arithmetic" ... "Schools of Jurisprudence and Medicine" — the lecture room per faculty (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [RCHME Oxford, Bodleian](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/rchme/oxon/pp1-10)); Cambridge's Old Schools: a Divinity School begun c. 1350, "completed in 1400" with the Regent House over it (96¼ × 28¼ ft, "three-tiered stages for benches" along the sides), the Canon Law School (roofed 1454) and Civil Law School (1457–c. 1470) each "with library above" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [RCHME Cambridge, University buildings](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/rchme/cambs/pp9-25)). (2) **The collegiate court** — W&C's thesis: the college plan "has so remarkable an analogy with that of a monastery" yet "it is impossible to maintain any theory of direct derivation"; "The dormitory, however ... was never employed in colleges, where the Scholars, Fellows, and other inhabitants have always been lodged in chambers"; the earliest colleges had "little else than chambers to lodge the inhabitants, a refectory or hall, and a kitchen ... Their devotions were performed in the parish church, their books were kept in a chest in the strong room, and the master ... occupied an ordinary chamber"; the quadrangle "was not made till long after their establishment"; the plan is derived "from that of a manor house" (Haddon Hall) (LOCAL, W&C iii, pp. 247–248, 266). The FIRST complete plan: New College (site 1379; foundation stone 1380; occupied 1386; "chapel and hall dominated the north side, with the Warden's lodging in a gatehouse on the west, fellow chambers on the south, and a library over the garden gate's eastern passage. A muniment tower rose at the southeast angle"; the chapel "a long choir of five bays, and a short aisled nave of two bays" (the antechapel "for side altars and secular purposes"); the hall "like a solarium above ground" reached by "a stone staircase in the muniment tower"; the library "lit by nine windows on the east and the west"; the kitchen with "three great bread ovens" and "a covered passage connected the Longhouse latrines"; the Warden's oratory "with a squint into the chapel" and a detached kitchen across the lane; ground-floor chambers "held four fellows each; upper chambers held three", with "study closets (studia sive loca studiorum)"; seniors' "cocklofts" in the roof by the 16th c. — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [VCH Oxon iii](https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/oxon/vol3/pp144-162)); the Cambridge model: "a gateway, a chapel, a hall and buttery, a kitchen" and "chambers totally enclosing a roughly rectangular court" by the late 14th c.; gate TOWERS from King's Hall's King Edward's Tower (1428) and King's (1441); "detached or nearly detached kitchens at Jesus, Peterhouse, and Trinity Hall"; Queens' "arcaded walks" from the 15th c. as "open corridors rather than elaborate cloisters"; Caius's 1565 court "open on one side for health reasons" ("lest for lack of free ventilation the air should become foul" — the statute) adopted by Emmanuel, Jesus, Pembroke, Trinity, Peterhouse, then by Wren "on account of its superior architectural beauty" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, RCHME Cambs preface; LOCAL, W&C iii, pp. 275, 279). THE CHAMBER-AND-STUDY (the college's defining cell): "the chambers in all the early colleges ... were built in two floors, without garrets"; "each chamber, with few exceptions, was occupied by several persons, who dwelt, slept, and pursued their studies therein"; the statutes fix the count — Merton 1270 "three at least", Queen's Oxford 1340 "two persons at least", Peterhouse/Clare/King's Hall "two only", New College 1400 "at least three" upper and "four in each of his lower chambers" ("each provided with four windows and four studies"), King's 1443 two and three, Magdalen 1479 four upper with "two, 'beds on wheels'" (truckle beds), Brasenose 1524 three and four; the study = a closet "nestled under the landing of the staircase" or at the room's corners, lit by its own single-light window — the Legge Building contract (1618–19): storeys 18 and 19 ft wide within walls, 9 ft high, the half-storey 8 ft 6 in; the garret chamber "nine feet wide, and about twenty feet long, but ... only six feet eight inches high"; studies "about four feet wide and from five to six feet long"; widths "from three feet six inches to nearly five feet, and their depths from four feet six inches to five feet"; a study doorway "two feet wide, and five feet six inches high"; "the four corners of the chamber were occupied, two by the beds, and two by the studies"; a St John's inventory (1630) lists a chamber's "six studies" each "with 2 shelues, lock and key and a table" (LOCAL, W&C iii, pp. 297–313). THE STAIRCASE: "In the earlier colleges at Cambridge the stairs run in a single long flight between two parallel partitions to an upper landing, on which, to the right and left, are the outer doors of the upper chambers"; after 1590 "a staircase ... which does not extend to the back wall, but has two flights or more to each storey, or else winding stairs ... so as to leave space for studies behind them" (LOCAL, W&C iii, p. 313) — and the 19th-c. INVERSION: Keble (1868–70) "arranging rooms along corridors rather than around staircases, in order that the scouts could supervise the comings and goings of visitors" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Keble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keble_College,_Oxford)) — the corridor licensed by SURVEILLANCE. The boarding school as college: Winchester (hall 62 × 29 × 40 ft high; chapel 93 × 27; Outer Court 200 ft long; Fromond's chantry 36 × 18 × 28 ft with the priest's chamber over) and Eton (the founder's design: a tower-gateway "40 feet wide" with "two rooms, one for muniments, the other for relics, plate, and jewels", "resembled the Muniment Tower at Winchester"; chambers "each 26 feet long by 18 feet wide"; the library "52 feet long by 24 feet wide" on the first floor; a cloister whose "central space ... was to be reserved as a burying-place"; a belfry "20 feet square within the walls, and 140 feet high" with "no egress" but a door to the cloister — LOCAL, W&C i, pp. 358–359). (3) **The continental college and the university palace** — the Collegio di Spagna, Bologna (1365–67, Gattapone): "two-story design organized around a central courtyard with porticoed galleries", student chambers "facing the exterior with fortified walls and crenellations", a chapel off the court, lecture spaces, a library and a refectory; "the prototype for subsequent Spanish collegiate institutions" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [it.wikipedia Collegio di Spagna](https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collegio_di_Spagna)); the Collège des Bernardins, Paris (from 1248; a Cistercian students' house with a great refectory, a cellar, dormitory over — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coll%C3%A8ge_des_Bernardins)); the Archiginnasio, Bologna (1562–63, Terribilia): "a porch some 139 meters long comprising thirty arches", "a central courtyard with two lines of loggias", two staircases to the upper floor "where lecture halls were distributed" — "Each of the legisti and the artisti had five classrooms in their respective wings"; the anatomical theatre of 1636 "shaped like an amphitheater and made from fir wood" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Archiginnasio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archiginnasio_of_Bologna)); the Collegio Romano (1551; built 1582–84, Ammannati: "two large, colonnaded courtyards and large classrooms"; > 2,000 students by 1620; an observatory tower on the church roof 1787 — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collegio_Romano)); the madrasa — Mustansiriyya, Baghdad (1227–33): "a basic four-iwan plan fit into a two-story rectangular building with a large courtyard", each law school in a corner, with "a library, hammam (bath), hospital, pharmacy, food storage, kitchen, and student residences arranged across two floors with connecting corridors", 80,000 volumes, "thirty orphans", "lodgings, clothes, food, and monthly allowances" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Mustansiriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustansiriya_Madrasa)). THE LECTURE HALL and the THEATRE (the catalog's "Amphitheater"): Padua's anatomical theatre (1594–95; "an inverted cone inserted in a cylinder, arranged in steps"; six tiers (the it.wiki) / "seven rings" (en.wiki) — CONTESTED count; the upper tier 7.56 × 6.92 m, the lowest 3.49 × 2.97 m at 1.84 m above the floor, railings 1.07 m, 40 cm of standing room per tier; "up to five hundred students could sit" (de Brosses 1739); preceded by a temporary wooden theatre of 1583 "dismantled annually" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [it](https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_anatomico_di_Padova) + [en](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatomical_Theatre_of_Padua) Wikipedia); Leiden 1596 in a former church; Bologna 1636–37; Uppsala 1663 in a cupola (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Anatomical theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatomical_theatre)); the Regent House's tiered benches (Cambridge c. 1400). Decline and re-use: the dissolved chantry college; the college rebuilt "since the Reformation" (only six: Clare 1638, St Catharine's 1673, Exeter 1615, Oriel 1620, University 1634, Queen's 1693 — LOCAL, W&C iii, p. 275); the Collegio Romano seized 1870; the friary's hall made a college library (Christ Church's frater, 1613).

**(b) Measured.** Courts: New College Great Quad 177½ × 125½ ft; its cloister 161 × 116 ft, 12 × 8 bays (both PARENT-VERIFIED 2026-08-23 verbatim from RCHME Oxford); Merton Mob Quad 65½ × 66 ft (1308–78) and Fellows' Quad 102 × 108 ft (1608–10); Queens' Front Court 98 × 84¾ ft (1448–49), Cloister Court "average 89 ft by 69 ft"; Christ Church Tom Quad 276½ × 271 ft (1525–29); Trinity Great Court "273 ft by 325 ft average"; the Schools Quad 109½ × 102 ft; Winchester Outer Court 200 ft long. Halls: New College 114½ × 40 ft (PARENT-VERIFIED); Christ Church 114½ × 39¾ ft, eight bays, hammer-beam; Merton 78 × 27 ft (13th c.); Queens' 27¼ × 52½ ft "including the Screens" (five bays, 1449); Trinity 40½ × 101¼ ft "including the Screens 9¾ ft wide overall" (1604–5, "based for size upon that of the Middle Temple"); Winchester 62 × 29 × 40 ft. Chapels: New College choir 102 × 33 + antechapel 80 × 37 ft; Merton choir 102 × 28 (1289–94), crossing 26 × 24, transepts 34–35 × 29 ft; Queens' 67 × 19¾ ft incl. antechapel (1454); Trinity 204½ × 34 ft incl. a 69½-ft antechapel (1555–67); Winchester 93 × 27 ft. Libraries: Queens' 67¾ × 20 ft (first floor); Eton design 52 × 24 ft; Merton's two rooms 20 ft 6 in wide, 38 ft 6 in and 56 ft 6 in long (LOCAL, W&C iii); New College "nine windows on the east and the west"; the Divinity School 86½ × 31 ft with the library over. Gates: Queens' gatehall 18¾ × 12 ft; Trinity Great Gate hall 19 × 23 ft; Eton's tower-gate 40 ft wide with muniment + treasury rooms; New College bell tower 20 × 18 ft; Eton's belfry 20 ft square × 140 ft. Chambers: Eton 26 × 18 ft; Legge Building storeys 18–19 ft wide × 9 ft high; garret chamber 9 × 20 ft × 6 ft 8 in; studies 3 ft 6 in–5 ft × 4 ft 6 in–6 ft, doors 2 ft × 5 ft 6 in (LOCAL, W&C iii); Queens' Long Gallery 79¼ × 12 ft (the addendum's LONG_GALLERY at college scale — narrower than its BROAD bucket); Combination Room 31 × 21¼ ft. Lecture: Regent House 96¼ × 28¼ ft tiered; Archiginnasio porch 139 m / 30 arches, 5 + 5 classrooms; Padua theatre 7.56 × 6.92 m top tier, "500". (All SUB-LANE CONFIRMED from the RCHME/VCH pages cited unless marked LOCAL.)

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The college is NOT a monastery (W&C's thesis): chambers, not a dormitory; the chapel, library, lodge and gate "wholly wanting in the earlier ones" — the court parti ACCRETES from a manor-house plan, which refutes any grammar that derives COLLEGIATE_COURT from CLAUSTRAL. (ii) Padua's tier count: six (it.wiki, measured) vs seven (en.wiki "seven rings ... symbolic") — the measured source wins; record 6 (PLAUSIBLE: the seventh is the floor ring). (iii) The closed quadrangle was "the pride" of pre-Reformation colleges and the OPEN court a post-1565 sanitary/aesthetic rule — era-graded enclosure. (iv) The hosted university (rented halls, the parish church's Congregation House) is the floor for 150 years (Bologna/Paris/Oxford 12th c. → first owned buildings 1320s); the catalog's "10,000+ for small campus" (Bardic college) is a designer's proxy — the evidence licenses the university by a CHARTER/FOUNDATION event in a city that can be small (Oxford, Cambridge, Padua, Salamanca). (v) Negation: the sub-lane's "Bologna Paris no buildings rented rooms" search was REFUSED by the cap; the Wikipedia statement stands single-sourced. (vi) The Bardic college's "Amphitheater, dormitories" has a measured analogue ONLY in the anatomical theatre (1583 temporary → 1595 permanent) and the Long Chamber — both post-1500; a medieval "amphitheater" for learning does not exist in the fetched record (the Regent House's tiered benches are the closest).

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** The STAIRCASE-ENTRY plan = `STAIR_HALL{MODULE; flights: 1 (to 1590) | 2+ (after)}` serving two chambers per landing, each chamber a `THROUGH_ROOM` with study closets at the corners and "under the staires" — the addendum's `STAIR_HALL{MODULE}` (Berwick's barracks, one stair per four rooms) is this plan's military cousin; the study under the landing is a storage-grade closet inside the stair cell. The GATE = `LOBBY{GATE}` + `CROSS_PASSAGE{GATE}` with the Warden's lodging / muniment / treasury OVER it (New College's warden in the gatehouse; Eton's two rooms over the gate) — the addendum's GUARDIAN_ADJACENT and OVER-THE-GATE archive in one cell. The COURT = `EXTERIOR_WALK{COURT_RING}` enclosed (≤ 1565) or three-sided (after); Queens' "arcaded walks ... open corridors" and the Archiginnasio's 30-arch porch + double loggia = `GALLERY{ARCADE | LOGGIA_COURT}`; the college cloister (New College 161 × 116 ft; Eton's as a burying-ground) = `GALLERY{CLOISTER_WALK}` with a cemetery garth — a FUNERARY walk, not a study walk (W&C i). The HALL's `SCREENS_PASSAGE` (Queens' and Trinity "including the Screens 9¾ ft wide" — the first MEASURED screens bay in the tranche: ≈ 3 m) with buttery/pantry opposite and the kitchen detached (Jesus, Peterhouse, Trinity Hall) — DWR1A's hall house at college scale; New College's hall raised "like a solarium above ground" with a `STAIR_HALL{VICE}` in the muniment tower — a first-floor hall. The "covered passage" to the Longhouse latrines (New College) = `CORRIDOR{CONNECTING}` in the 1380s — another program-licensed early corridor (the monastic slype's cousin); Mustansiriyya's "connecting corridors" on two floors (1233) the same. The LECTURE HALL = a `THROUGH_ROOM` with tiered benches (the Regent House) → the THEATRE = a `CENTRALIZED` cell with a `STAIR_HALL{TIERED}` as its whole interior (Padua: the audience stands on the stair; 40 cm per tier). Keble's corridor (1868) = `CORRIDOR{DOUBLE_LOADED}` licensed by supervision — the addendum's licence date (the PLAN's corridor c. 1650+) holds; the college was the last to adopt it.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `CLOSET{MUNIMENT}` in a TOWER (New College's four-stage muniment tower with "simple lierne vaults"; Merton's 1308–9 treasury, "quadripartite stone vaults in two bays", stone-slab roof; Eton's over-gate pair "muniments" + "relics, plate, and jewels") — R-INST-1's bundle, CONFIRMED by Steane at Windsor and here at three colleges (stone vault, stone roof, tower position, stair off the hall); `CLOSET{TREASURE}` the second over-gate room; `CLOSET{STUDY}` ×3–6 per chamber (the college's SIGNATURE closet: 3 ft 6 in–5 ft × 4 ft 6 in–6 ft, own window, lock and key, shelves, table, seat — measured above); `BUTTERY` + `PANTRY` off the screens; `KITCHEN{detached | attached; ovens ×3}`; `CELLAR` (Tackley's five-bay vault; the "cellars beneath both parlours" idiom); `ATTIC{COCKLOFT | GARRET{studies}}` — the Legge garret chambers with two studies each, later "a lumber-room for packing-cases and fuel" (a dated wear state); the LIBRARY as a first-floor long room (family M) — "their books were kept in a chest in the strong room" at the floor (W&C); the madrasa's "food storage, kitchen" and pharmacy; the Combination Room (a parlour). Adjacency laws stated: muniment OVER the gate or IN the tower with the stair from the hall; library OVER a gate-passage or a school (never at grade); kitchen DETACHED (three early Cambridge cases) or at the hall's lower end; the lodge at the gate (guardian); the cemetery in the cloister garth (Eton) — `REQUIRED_BELOW` inverted: burial under the walk.

**(f) Typed proposal.** `parti: COLLEGIATE_COURT{enclosure: CLOSED (≤ 1565) | THREE_SIDED (after); court 65 × 66 ft → 273 × 325 ft; gate tower; chapel+antechapel; hall(screens 9¾ ft); chambers on staircases; library over; muniment tower; kitchen detached|attached; cloister optional}` (the English/Spanish college; the madrasa is the same court with iwans for lecture halls and cells round it — `COLLEGIATE_COURT{iwans: 4}`); `parti: SCHOOLS_QUAD` (the university's own: lecture rooms per faculty round a court, libraries over them — Oxford 1610–24; Cambridge 1350–1470; the Archiginnasio's porch + loggia court with 5 + 5 classrooms and a theatre); `parti: NONE_HOSTED` (the 12th–14th-c. university: rented halls, the parish church's Congregation House, masters' houses); `parti: THEATRE{tiers 6–7; top 7.56 × 6.92 m; capacity ~500}` as a cell of SCHOOLS_QUAD (the catalog's "Amphitheater" — era ≥ 1583, and a medical faculty). `functions[]`: GATE_LODGE, HALL, BUTTERY/PANTRY, KITCHEN, CHAPEL{choir + antechapel (the antechapel "for side altars and secular purposes" — a hosted civic function)}, CHAMBER{occupants 1–4; beds: principal + truckle}, STUDY{×1–6 per chamber}, LIBRARY{first floor; windows both sides}, MUNIMENT_TOWER, TREASURY, LODGE (Warden's: hall, study, oratory with squint, detached kitchen), COMBINATION_ROOM, LONG_GALLERY (Queens' 79 × 12 ft), CLOISTER{burial garth}, BELL_TOWER{20 ft sq; 140 ft}, LATRINE_RANGE ("Longhouse") + covered passage, LECTURE_ROOM{per faculty; tiered benches}, THEATRE, OBSERVATORY (Collegio Romano 1787 — family N), PORCH/LOGGIA (Italian). `fixtures[]`: STUDY_CLOSET{window, door 2 × 5½ ft, shelves, lock}, TRUCKLE_BED, FORMS{tiered}, CATALOGUE_FRAME (library — M), CLOCK (1455), SQUINT, BREAD_OVENS{3}, DISSECTION_TABLE + RAILINGS{1.07 m}. Structural: `courtRing`, `gateTower{3–4 storeys}`, `stoneVault{muniment}`, `hammerBeam{8 bays}`, `brick{diaper}` (Eton/Wainfleet), `crenellated` (Collegio di Spagna — a fortified college), `timberCone` (Padua). Licensing: tier city (the Bardic college; the university by CHARTER event — population is a proxy); metropolis (the Academy: the SCHOOLS_QUAD + COLLEGIATE_COURT + THEATRE + OBSERVATORY set — the Collegio Romano/Archiginnasio grade); prosperity by ENDOWMENT (every college is a foundation — the §Σ input again); era (hosted ≤ c. 1320; court 1380+; open court 1565+; theatre 1583+; corridor 1868); culture (English staircase plan vs Italian loggia-court vs the madrasa's iwan court; the Spanish college fortified). HOME: `INTERIOR_KINDS.learning` (reading/stacks/study) fits ONE cell (the library); NO TYPED HOME for the court, gate, chambers-on-staircases, study closets, hall/screens, muniment tower, theatre, or the hosted university. VERDICT: OWN BUILDING (a court) at city and metropolis; the floor (12th–14th c.) is HOSTED in burgage houses and the parish church; the Bardic college's "dormitories" are CHAMBERS (W&C: colleges "never employed" a dormitory) — or, if the owner wants the catalog's word, Eton's Long Chamber with masters at both ends.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) The STAIRCASE-ENTRY plan is a third circulation SYSTEM beside through-room and corridor: N stairs, 2 doors per landing, no horizontal connection between staircases — the validator's reachability must accept a court whose ranges are NOT internally connected (each staircase is its own reachable set; the court is the connector). (2) The STUDY is a CLOSET inside a chamber with its own window — a storage-class cell used for work; `CLOSET{STUDY}` needs `lightReq: OWN_WINDOW` (the single-light windows between chamber windows are the fossil that dates it). (3) A corridor licensed by SURVEILLANCE (Keble) — a third licence mechanism after program (slype) and fashion (the 17th-c. house). (4) The catalog needs a `University` row (city) and a `Grammar school` row (town); the Academy of magic's learning half is the SCHOOLS_QUAD + court + theatre + observatory bundle, fully typed here without magic.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** English: the closed court of chambers on staircases with hall, chapel, library and muniment tower — a manor house multiplied, never a monastery; the university itself owns only its schools and library. Italian: the porticoed court with loggias (Collegio di Spagna; the Archiginnasio's 139-m porch) and the lecture halls on the upper floor; the theatre as a wooden cone; the Jesuit college with two courts and 2,000 students. Iberian: the Salamanca Escuelas (1415–33; unreached — the fetch returned photographs only) and the fortified Bologna prototype. Beyond: the madrasa's four-iwan court with cells on two floors, a library, a bath and a hospital in ONE building (Mustansiriyya 1233) — the most complete learning precinct in the evidence, and the one where the lecture hall is an open-fronted vaulted cell (the iwan) on the court: `COLLEGIATE_COURT{iwans}` covers it.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** Academy of magic: the learning half = SCHOOLS_QUAD + COLLEGIATE_COURT + THEATRE + LIBRARY (M) + OBSERVATORY (N); "research facilities, visiting scholars" = the Collegio Romano's laboratories and the college's guest chambers; magical conventions → R-INST-5. Bardic college: the court + the theatre (post-1583) + the Long Chamber; performance → R-INST-4.
