## §14 · FAMILY M — the library: Great library (metropolis L2348 "Largest repository of knowledge in the region: thousands of volumes, map archives, historical records." — Magic shelf, `education` tag ×2) · (NO CATALOG ENTRY) library below metropolis (the chest, the armarium, the parish library, the cathedral/college library room)

*Provenance: sub-lane MNO (killed at ~06:16Z after 67 tool calls; four of its last searches REFUSED by the cap). The sub-lane downloaded and grep-read TWO type-studies which the parent then read LOCAL: J. W. Clark, *The Care of Books* (1901; `RINST3-MNO-clark-care-of-books.txt`, archive.org OCR) and B. H. Streeter, *The Chained Library* (1931; `RINST3-MNO-streeter-chained-library.txt`); plus Willis & Clark iii ch. "The Library" (LOCAL, family L's file). Every figure below marked LOCAL was read by the parent in those texts.*

**(a) Analogue.** The library is a LADDER of five states, and the evidence dates each. (1) **The CHEST and the ARMARIUM** (a fixture) — "In the Middle Ages books were originally kept in cupboards, called almeries (armaria), and in chests, or they were chained to desks in a church. Carrels in the cloister were provided for reading. The Chained Library begins in England about 1320; but not all books were chained" (LOCAL, Streeter, Synopsis, p. 1); the early colleges' "books were kept in a chest in the strong room" (LOCAL, W&C iii, p. 248); the common cloister-press "armarium commune" in a recess by the church door (Durham's "Norman arched recess", Worcester's "cloyster awmery" still in use 1518); the Cistercian BOOK-ROOM cut "from the west end of the sacristy" between transept and chapter house — Fossa Nuova "14 ft. long by 10 ft. broad", Kirkstall c. 1150 (LOCAL, Clark pp. 84–86) = Le Thoronet's armarium 3 × 3 m (family F). (2) **The CARREL** (a reading fixture in the cloister walk) — the Rites of Durham: "in every wyndowe iij Pewes or Carrells, where every one of the old Monks had his carrell, severall by himselfe ... all fynely wainscotted and verie close, all but the forepart, which had carved wourke that gave light in ... And in every carrell was a deske ... no greater then from one stanchell of the wyndowe to another"; "over against the carrells against the church wall did stande certaine great almeries of waynscott all full of bookes"; the library-walk was the walk NEXT THE CHURCH "partly for the sake of warmth, partly to secure greater privacy", the other walks "kept clear for the Sunday procession"; a carrel "must have closely resembled a modern sentry-box", its door "of open work, partly that the work of the occupant might be supervised"; carrels at Westminster 1258–83, Bury 1327 (smashed in a riot), Evesham 1367–79, Abingdon 1383–84; Gloucester's twenty STONE carrels (1370–1412) "4 ft. wide, 19 in. deep, and 6 ft. 9 in. high, lighted by a small window of two lights" (Clark sat in one with a borrowed chair and a folio); Durham's eleven windows "so that thirty-three monks could have been accommodated" (LOCAL, Clark pp. 90–98). (3) **The LIBRARY ROOM over something** (15th c.) — "in most of the large monasteries a special room was constructed to hold books. Reading went forward, as heretofore, in the cloister ... These conventual libraries were usually built over some existing building, or over the cloister" (LOCAL, Clark p. 106): Durham's over the old sacristy (c. 1446, "44 ft. 10 in. long, by 18 ft. wide, with a window at each end, 13 ft. wide, of five lights"; cost £90 16s); Canterbury's over the Prior's Chapel (1414–43, c. 60 × 22 ft); Gloucester's over the slype with eleven windows on ONE side; Winchester's over the passage between chapter house and transept; Worcester's over the nave's south aisle (130 ft 7 in × 19 ft 6 in × 8 ft 6 in high, twelve windows, "a circular stone staircase ... access to which is from the outside only"); Groenendael's over the whole north cloister (175 ft) "so that its windows faced the south"; the four "typical of Cathedral libraries built over a cloister": Lincoln (a TIMBER structure over the east walk, originally five bays, walls 9 ft 8 in high, bays 7 ft 9 in, a 15th-c. carved roof, a door from the cloister's upper level; two bays demolished 1789 "and the Stair case therto removed"), Salisbury (1444–45: "certain schools suitable for lectures, together with a library ... shall be built as soon as possible over one side of the cloister"; curtailed 1758 because "much too heavy to be properly supported by the Cloysters"; now 66 × 20 × 12 ft 9 in), Old St Paul's (over the east walk of the Pardon churchyard cloister; the cloister demolished 1549 "excepting onely that side where the Library was" for Somerset House's stone), Wells (the south end of "a long, narrow room over the east pane of the cloister, approached by a spiral staircase from the south transept", 162 × 12 ft, the library 106 ft of it, thirteen roof-spaces with two windows a side each, "fitted up as a library in 1472"); Lichfield's DETACHED brick library (1489–93, c. 60 × 15 ft, "approached by a flight of stairs", on a colonnade like Noyon's, destroyed 1757) (LOCAL, Clark pp. 106–108, 118–124). The FIRST purpose-built: Cobham's over Oxford's Congregation House (c. 1320; windows 7 ft 6 in centre to centre), Merton (1371–78), New College ("for the first time part of the Founder's plan (1379)") (LOCAL, Streeter pp. 6–7; W&C iii). (4) **The FITTINGS LADDER** (Clark's three systems, Streeter's correction): "the 'Lectern-system', the 'Stall-system' (Duke Humphrey's room in the Bodleian, and Hereford), and the 'Wall-system'"; "The Lectern-system prevailed at Cambridge till 1600"; "The Stall-system a combination of a lectern with an almery. It first appears in the original fittings of Magdalen College, Oxford, 1480"; the "Three-Decker" standard "first seen at Hereford, 1590"; "The Wall-system, on the grand scale, is found in the Escorial at Madrid, begun in 1563. It was introduced to England by Sir Thomas Bodley in the new wing of his library, which he began in 1610, known as Arts End" — with a GALLERY; "The Wall-system, without chains, is seen in eighteenth-century libraries" (LOCAL, Streeter Synopsis pp. 1–2). The lectern survivors: Zutphen (1561–63, "attached to the church of SS. Peter and Walburga"; "built against the south choir-aisle of the church, out of which a door opens into it"; about 60 × 26 ft, four octagonal columns, two aisles; "eighteen bookcases, or desks; namely, ten on the south side of the room, and eight on the north"; each 9 ft long × 5 ft 5½ in high to the finial, desk edge 2 ft 6½ in off the floor; "Seats and desks are ranged round the room alternately"; double desks "like an ordinary church lectern", no shelf above or below; 18 desks, 316 volumes — LOCAL, Clark pp. 153–156; Streeter: desks 9 ft, 2 ft 8 in apart, bench 1 ft 10 in high × 10½ in wide, "foundation-beams" — LOCAL, Streeter p. 11); Lincoln's lectern "with two shelves" (LOCAL, Clark p. 156); Cesena (1452, Matteo Nuzio; "always public"; on the first floor over convent rooms; 133 ft 4 in × 34 ft, three aisles on two rows of ten fluted marble columns, eleven bays each lit by two windows, side aisles c. 12 ft, central aisle 8 ft 3 in barrel-vaulted — "If the central alley were wider it might be taken for the nave of a basilica"; 29 cases per aisle, a raised wooden floor 3½ in between each pair, 2 ft 3 in between case and wall; each case a desk + shelf + seat with a sloped back, 10 ft 2½ in long, 4 ft 2½ in high, desk to desk 4 ft 1 in, desk slope 45°; "measurements were taken by myself, with a tape, in September, 1895" — LOCAL, Clark pp. 199–203); Florence's Laurentian (1523–71, Michelangelo; "raised high above the ground in order to secure an ample supply of light and air, and is approached by a double staircase of marble"; 151 ft 9 in × 34 ft 4 in, 15 windows a side at 7 ft 6 in off the floor; 44 desks a side; the Wikipedia figures: reading room 46.20 × 10.50 × 8.4 m, the ricetto 10.50 × 10.50 × 14.6 m with Ammannati's three-flight stair "occupying half the vestibule floor" — LOCAL, Clark pp. 234–235; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Laurentian Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurentian_Library)); Leiden (print of 1610: "a long and lofty room, of moderate width", four three-light windows a side, eleven cases a side, a single row of chained books per case — LOCAL, Clark p. 425 via W&C iii); the Vatican's Latin Library (Sixtus IV: ten banchi "38 palms each, or about 27 ft. 9 in.", the room 34 ft 8 in wide — LOCAL, Clark p. 226). The stall survivors: Hereford's chapter library (fitted 1394 over the WEST cloister — "the walk of the cloister is 13 feet wide", the library over it "might have been a little wider, say 18 feet", three bays of 12 ft 6 in ≈ 36 ft inside; the books moved to the Lady Chapel in 1590 "and the erection of a school on the ground where the Library had once stood"; presses 9 ft 8–9 in × 2 ft 2 in × 8 ft, "unplaned oak, very rough", 6 ft 5 in apart — LOCAL, Clark pp. 171–175; Streeter pp. 54–55, 87–88, 115); Merton (two rooms 20 ft 6 in wide, 38 ft 6 in and 56 ft 6 in long; cases 7 ft 5 in × 1 ft 5 in × 6 ft, desks 1 ft 3 in each side, the chain-bar "two feet from the ground"; central alley 5 ft tiled; twelve + twenty cases — LOCAL, Clark pp. 179–181; W&C iii p. 442: cases "about 4 feet apart", bench 9 in); Trinity Hall (c. 43 × 20 ft, six windows a side, six cases a wall with "a double sloping desk, with a single shelf for books beneath it", no chaining traces — LOCAL, W&C iii pp. 450–451); Peterhouse (1431, "about 60 feet long by 20 feet wide", desks 1447–50 with locks); the Bodleian's presses 11 ft 3 in long, Corpus 7 ft 6 in — "due to corresponding variations in the width of the several rooms"; press ends "averaging 8 ft. 3 in. by 2 ft. 1 in."; "A large folio requires a shelf 1 ft. wide"; spacing Corpus 6 ft, Bodleian 6 ft 6 in, Hereford 6 ft 5 in (LOCAL, Streeter pp. 55, 115). The wall survivors: the Escorial (1563–84; "over the portico through which the building is entered"; 212 × 35 × ~36 ft, barrel-vaulted and frescoed; windows "nearly 13 ft. high, and extend down to the floor"; cases by Herrera 1584, > 12 ft high, desks 2 ft 7 in off the floor, four shelves, the top at 9 ft "so that a ladder is required" — LOCAL, Clark pp. 267–270); the Ambrosiana (1603–09; "a single hall, on the ground floor, 74 ft. long by 29 ft. broad", cases 13 ft high behind wire, a staircase at each corner to a gallery — LOCAL, Clark p. 271); Wren's Trinity (1676: "placed on a cloister, open both to the east and to the west ... The level of the library floor was made to correspond with that of the first floor of the chambers"; "the windowes of the Library rise high and giue place for the deskes against the walls" — Wren's memoir, LOCAL, Clark pp. 277–280; the Sansovino model "raised his library on a cloister"). (5) **The PARISH and the SCHOLAR'S library** — Grantham (1598: "a small room, 16 feet from north to south by 14 feet from east to west, over the south porch of the parish church, approached by a newel stair from the south aisle"; three cases "about 6 ft. high and 6 ft. long"; a 1642 gift "to provide firewood for the library fire" — the vicar's winter study; LOCAL, Clark pp. 257–258); Langley Marish (1623: a room off the church "entered through the squire's pew", panelled 15 in from the wall as painted cupboards, a fireplace; kept by the four almshouse tenants); Wimborne (1686: "about fifteen feet square, over the vestry", a turret stair, two shelves round three sides); Wootton Wawen's single Florentine-type desk with nine chained books in the south aisle (1693) (LOCAL, Clark pp. 258–262; W&C iii p. 428 n.); the scholar's chamber (15th c.): a trestle desk with ink-horn holes, a second desk for the open book, "Behind the writer's chair is his book-chest", tiled floor, a fire, a bed (LOCAL, Clark p. 314); Urbino's studiolo "11 ft. 6 in. by 13 ft. 4 in.", panelled 8 ft high in intarsia, "a small cupboard ... a few shelves. These are the only receptacles for books" (1476); Montaigne's tower library: "the third story of a Tower, of which the Ground-room is my Chappel, the second story an Apartment", "The figure of my Study is round ... set up upon five degrees of Shelves round about me ... sixteen paces' Diameter", with a wish for "a Gallery of an hundred paces long, and twelve broad" because "Every place of retirement requires a Walk" (LOCAL, Clark pp. 314–315). Decline: the Dissolution's book-wreck — Bale (1549): "some to scoure theyr candelstyckes, and some to rubbe theyr bootes. Some they sold to the grossers and sopesellers, and some they sent ouer see to the boke bynders ... whole shyppes full"; "the contentes of two noble lybraryes for .xl. shyllynges" (LOCAL, Clark p. 247); the over-cloister library "much too heavy" and cut down (Salisbury 1758); the timber library over the walk demolished (Lincoln 1789); chaining "not abandoned until the end of the last century" (King's to 1777 — LOCAL, Streeter p. 2); the Mustansiriyya's 80,000 → "400,000 volumes ... may have been exaggerated" (family L).

**(b) Measured.** (All LOCAL from Clark 1901 / Streeter 1931 / W&C 1886 unless marked.) Rooms: Fossa Nuova book-room 14 × 10 ft; Durham 1446 library 44 ft 10 in × 18 ft; Canterbury c. 60 × 22 ft; Worcester 130 ft 7 in × 19 ft 6 in × 8 ft 6 in; Wells 162 × 12 ft (106 ft the library); Lincoln bays 7 ft 9 in, walls 9 ft 8 in; Salisbury 66 × 20 × 12 ft 9 in (post-1758); Lichfield c. 60 × 15 ft; Hereford over a 13-ft walk, ~18 × 36 ft; Merton 20 ft 6 in × 38 ft 6 in / 56 ft 6 in; Trinity Hall c. 43 × 20; Peterhouse c. 60 × 20; Queens' 67¾ × 20 (family L); Eton design 52 × 24; New College 9 windows a side; Zutphen c. 60 × 26 ft; Cesena 133 ft 4 in × 34 ft (aisles 12 / 8 ft 3 in / 12); Laurentian 151 ft 9 in × 34 ft 4 in (46.20 × 10.50 × 8.4 m); ricetto 10.50 × 10.50 × 14.6 m; Vatican Latin room 34 ft 8 in wide; Escorial 212 × 35 × 36 ft; Ambrosiana 74 × 29 ft; Grantham 16 × 14 ft; Wimborne ~15 ft sq; Urbino studiolo 11 ft 6 in × 13 ft 4 in; Montaigne's round study "sixteen paces" across; Maragheh's library building 330 m² (CONFIRMED-digest, [UNESCO astronomical heritage](https://web.astronomicalheritage.net/show-entity?identity=29&idsubentity=1)); Pergamon's four rooms 42 × 49 ft, 23 and 33 × 39 ft off a two-storey colonnade 37 ft broad (LOCAL, Clark pp. 10–11). Fittings: Gloucester stone carrel 4 ft × 19 in × 6 ft 9 in; Zutphen desks 9 ft long, 5 ft 5½ in high, 2 ft 8 in apart, bench 1 ft 10 in × 10½ in, desk edge 2 ft 6½–9½ in; Cesena cases 10 ft 2½ in × 4 ft 2½ in high, 4 ft 1 in apart, 45° slope, 2 ft 3 in from the wall, a 3½-in raised floor; Merton 7 ft 5 in × 1 ft 5 in × 6 ft, bar 2 ft up, 4 ft apart, bench 9 in, alley 5 ft; Hereford presses 9 ft 8–9 in × 2 ft 2 in × 8 ft, 6 ft 5 in apart; Bodleian 11 ft 3 in, Corpus 7 ft 6 in; press ends avg 8 ft 3 in × 2 ft 1 in; a folio shelf 1 ft wide; chains 2 ft 10 in–4 ft 8 in; window centres Cobham 7 ft 6 in, Zutphen/Merton/Wells 5 ft 6–7 in, Queens' 5 ft 5–9 in between lecterns; Escorial cases > 12 ft, desk 2 ft 7 in, top shelf 9 ft; Ambrosiana cases 13 ft; Grantham cases 6 × 6 ft; Turton church cupboard 7 ft 5 in × 3 ft 3 in × 14 in; Laurentian floor panels 8 ft 6 in. Counts: Zutphen 316 vols / 18 desks; Cîteaux 509 vols in the room + 740 scattered (15th-c. catalogue: "Books chained on desks before the Chapter-House", "on the small desks in the cloister" — LOCAL, Clark pp. 105–106); Durham 33 carrels; Hereford ~1,500 chained (family G); Mustansiriyya 80,000 (L). NOT FOUND: Duke Humfrey's room dims in the fetched set (86½ × 31 ft is the Divinity School under it — family L); the Vatican rooms' lengths; the Pergamon colonnade's library function is "theory" (Clark's word).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) Clark's chronology (lectern → stall → wall as stages) is "to be modified" by Streeter: the lectern-system persisted at Cambridge to 1600 and in France to 1508; the stall-system "possibly a local Oxford form"; Queens' lectern spacing (5 ft 5–9 in) vs Zutphen's (2 ft 7–9 in) proves TWO benches per bay, "unless endowed with the neck of a giraffe" (LOCAL, Streeter pp. 11–13) — a measured refutation of a type-study by its successor; the grammar takes Streeter's spacing buckets. (ii) The "library" of the early college and monastery was a CHEST; the reading place was the CLOISTER; the room was for LOAN and REFERENCE (Clark) — three institutions the catalog's one word conflates. (iii) The over-cloister library is structurally marginal (Salisbury's "much too heavy"; Lincoln's timber) — a wear/collapse driver built into the parti. (iv) The wall-system "first introduced into a library at the Escorial" is Clark's own claim, qualified by himself ("I cannot construct a genealogical tree ... with the Escorial at the root") — CONTESTED by its author. (v) The catalog's "Great library ... thousands of volumes" at METROPOLIS only: the evidence has a 316-volume PUBLIC library in a Dutch town church (1563), a 1,249-volume abbey (Cîteaux), and a parish library over a porch (1598) — the library exists at town tier; the great library (Cesena's public one, the Laurentian, the Escorial, the Ambrosiana, the Bodleian) is an ENDOWMENT at city+. (vi) Negation: the sub-lane's "library was a chest not a room" question is answered by Streeter's first sentence — the floor is a fixture; no search was needed.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** The library is a `GALLERY{level UPPER; enclosure ENCLOSED}`-shaped room by DERIVATION — it sits OVER a cloister walk (Wells 162 × 12 ft IS the walk's footprint; Groenendael 175 ft; Hereford 13 → 18 ft), over a slype, a porch, a sacristy, a chapel, a school, an aisle — with a `STAIR_HALL{VICE | NEWEL | TURRET}` from the transept (Wells, Salisbury — a stair that "originally led only to the roof"), the south aisle (Grantham), the vestry (Wimborne's turret), "from the outside only" (Worcester), or a flight from the court (Lichfield, Merton); the ceiling inverts it — the Laurentian's ricetto with a three-flight marble stair filling half its floor (`STAIR_HALL{DISPLAYED}`), Wren's Trinity raised ON a cloister open both sides (`GALLERY{ARCADE}` below). Inside: the CENTRAL ALLEY between two rows of cases (Merton 5 ft tiled; Cesena 8 ft 3 in barrel-vaulted — "the nave of a basilica"; Hereford's "face-ends" on the avenue) is a `THROUGH_ROOM` spine with bays off it — the library room is the one learning cell whose internal circulation is MEASURED: alley 5–8 ft, bay pitch 4 ft 1 in (Cesena) / 5 ft 6 in (Zutphen, Merton, Wells) / 6–6½ ft (stall presses) / 7 ft 6 in (Cobham, Queens'); the wall-system adds a `GALLERY{GALLERY_OVER}` round the room (Arts End 1610; the Ambrosiana's corner stairs) and a ladder (Escorial). The carrel = a `LOBBY`-sized study box IN the cloister walk (4 ft × 19 in stone; the wooden "sentry-box" one window-light wide) — a fixture that subdivides a GALLERY. Montaigne's ideal = a round study + a 100-pace walk ("Every place of retirement requires a Walk") — the library's circulation DEMAND stated by a user.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** The library IS the storage family's learning member: `CLOSET{ARMARIUM}` (a recess or 14 × 10 ft book-room by the chapter house — fixture → cell), `STORE{BOOK_CHEST}` (a fixture: "an old irebounde cofre having hie feet" — LOCAL, Streeter; the college's chest in the strong room), `LIBRARY_ROOM{system: LECTERN | STALL | WALL; chained: bool; width 12–35 ft; length 36–212 ft; windows both sides at 5½–7½ ft centres (one side only at Gloucester/Worcester); lightReq: SIDE_WINDOWS_BOTH (the defining law: cases at right angles to the windows so light falls from the reader's left — Clark on Cesena/Vatican) | WALL_WINDOWS_HIGH (the wall-system: windows "rise high and giue place for the deskes against the walls" — Wren); fire: Grantham's "library fire" is the ONLY heated library in the set — the norm is UNHEATED (Montaigne: "In Winter ... I am not so continually there"), a FORBIDDEN_NEAR(HEARTH) law by practice; adjacency: OVER the walk/slype/porch (REQUIRED_ABOVE), off the choir aisle (Zutphen: "a door opens into it"), beside the chapter house (armarium), the Cistercian "wall close to the door of the church"}`. The fittings are STORAGE FIXTURES with measured pitches (b): lectern (books lie flat, one row, chain to a bar), press (shelves + desk + seat, 2–3 decks), wall-case (4 shelves to 9 ft + ladder/gallery), cupboard (Turton), panelled cupboards (Langley), catalogue frame at the press end. The muniment room (R-INST-1; family L's tower) is the library's sibling: "one for muniments, the other for relics, plate, and jewels" (Eton) — the same over-gate, vaulted, keyed cell.

**(f) Typed proposal.** NO CATALOG ENTRY below metropolis — proposed rows: `Parish library` (town; HOSTED over the porch/vestry — 15 × 15 ft), `Chapter/college library` (city; OWN ROOM over a walk — 40–130 ft × 12–22 ft). `parti: NONE_HOSTED` (the chest/armarium floor: a fixture of the church, monastery or college) → `LIBRARY_ROOM` (a cell of its host: over a walk/slype/porch/school/chapel; `system` as above) → `LIBRARY_BUILDING` (the detached/raised library: Lichfield 1489 on a colonnade; Cesena 1452 over convent rooms; the Laurentian on the cloister range; Wren's Trinity on an open cloister; the Ambrosiana on the ground with a portico; the Escorial over a portico) — a `HALL`-family long room on a `GALLERY{ARCADE}` or an undercroft. The catalog's "Great library" = `LIBRARY_BUILDING{system: WALL; gallery; length 150–212 ft; public: bool (Cesena "always public"; Zutphen "for the use of the public")} + MAP/RECORD ROOMS (the muniment bundle) + a reading room` at city+ by ENDOWMENT. `fixtures[]`: CHEST{iron-bound; 3 ft 11 in × 20 × 18 in}, ARMARIUM_RECESS, CARREL{stone 4 ft × 19 in × 6 ft 9 in | timber one-light}, LECTERN_DESK{9–10 ft; double; 45°; bar + chains}, PRESS{7½–11¼ ft × 2 ft 2 in × 8 ft 3 in; 2–3 decks; seat; desk; lock + hasp; catalogue frame}, WALL_CASE{12–13 ft; wire grille; ladder}, GALLERY_RAIL, BOOK_CHAIN{2 ft 10 in–4 ft 8 in; swivel}, LIBRARY_FIRE (rare, licensed by a gift), GLOBE/MAP_PRESS (the catalog's "map archives" — PLAUSIBLE fixture; no medieval map press was found). Structural: `overCloister{timber | stone; weight-limited}`, `raisedOnArcade`, `barrelVault` (Cesena, Escorial), `flatCarvedRoof` (Laurentian), `marbleColumns{2 × 10}`, `sideWindows{n: 6–15; centres 5½–7½ ft}`. Licensing: tier town (parish library, hosted, 1598+ in the English set), city (chapter/college room, 1320+; the public town library 1452 Cesena / 1563 Zutphen), metropolis (the endowed great library 1523+); era decides the SYSTEM (lectern ≤ 1600 Cambridge / 1508 France; stall 1480–1700 Oxford; wall 1563 Escorial / 1610 Bodleian / 1682 first college); prosperity by ENDOWMENT (Wykeham, Bodley, the Malatesta, the Medici, Philip II, Borromeo) — the §Σ input a fourth time; culture: the Italian public library on marble columns vs the English timber room over a walk vs the Spanish frescoed vault vs the Dutch church annex. HOME: `INTERIOR_KINDS.learning` = reading + stacks (+ study) — this IS the library's template and the only learning cell the engine has; NO TYPED HOME for the fittings systems, the over-walk position, the carrel, or the chest-as-library. VERDICT: HOSTED (a fixture or a room of the church/monastery/college) at every tier below the endowed great library; OWN BUILDING only at the endowment ceiling (city/metropolis).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) The library is sized by its HOST'S walk (over-cloister = the walk's width + ~5 ft) — the second `sizeFrom: host` rule (the charnel was the first). (2) Bay PITCH is the library's measured unit (4–7½ ft by system) — a fixture-rank rule that fixes room length from volume count: Zutphen 316 vols/18 desks ≈ 18 per desk; Cîteaux 509 in ~6 banche + 3 armaria + chests — the catalog's "thousands of volumes" implies 100+ presses = a 150–200-ft room (the Laurentian's 88 desks; the Escorial's 212 ft) — a number audit the grammar can run. (3) An UNHEATED cell by practice with one licensed exception — `FORBIDDEN_NEAR(HEARTH)` with an `exception: ENDOWED_FIRE`. (4) The engine's `learning` template (reading/stacks/study) matches the LECTERN/STALL room; the WALL system with a gallery and the carrel-in-a-walk are untyped.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** English: the chest → the carrel in the walk → the timber room over the walk (15th c.) → Oxford's stall presses (1480) → Bodley's wall + gallery (1610); chained to 1777. Italian: the PUBLIC library on marble columns at first-floor level (Cesena 1452; the Laurentian 1571 with its stair-vestibule; the Ambrosiana 1609 on the ground floor with a portico) — the library as a civic monument a century before England. Spanish: the frescoed barrel-vaulted wall-library over a portico (1584). Dutch: the church-annexed public library with lecterns (1563). Ancient/beyond: Pergamon's rooms off a two-storey colonnade 37 ft broad "twice as wide as that on the east side" — "libraries in the ancient world were usually connected with colonnades" (Clark) — and the Mustansiriyya's library inside the madrasa court: the library-on-a-walk is the one constant across the whole register, from Pergamon's stoa to Wells' cloister to Wren's arcade — `LIBRARY_ROOM{over: GALLERY}` is universal.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** The Great library sits on the MAGIC shelf; its learning half is fully typed above (building, fittings, pitch, light, fire law); magical contents and the Mages' district's libraries → R-INST-5.

## §15 · FAMILY N — the sage, the observatory, the anchorhold and the oracle: Sage's quarter (city L2132 "Multiple scholars and researchers." — Adventuring shelf, `education` tag) · (NO CATALOG ENTRY) observatory · hermitage/anchorhold · oracle

*Provenance: sub-lane MNO evidence (its four oracle/hermitage searches were REFUSED by the cap; the Wikipedia pages it fetched carry few figures); LOCAL Clark 1901 for the scholar's chamber, Urbino and Montaigne.*

**(a) Analogue.** Four small institutions, three of them ONE-PERSON cells. (1) **The scholar's house** (the catalog's "quarter" = a street of them) — the 15th-c. scholar's room: a trestle desk with ink-horn holes, a second desk for the open book, a book-chest behind the chair, tiled floor, a fire, a bed, a hanging (LOCAL, Clark p. 314 — a chamber of DWR1A's house with two fixtures added); the ceiling: Urbino's studiolo 1476 (11 ft 6 in × 13 ft 4 in, intarsia panelling 8 ft high, a cupboard and a few shelves, "a place of retirement for the Duke when he wished to be alone") and Montaigne's tower (chapel below, apartment with "withdrawing Room and Closet" above, the round library on the third floor, "sixteen paces' Diameter", five tiers of shelves, a cabinet with a fireplace, and the wish for a 100 × 12-pace gallery — LOCAL, Clark pp. 314–315); the Vicars' Close (family G: 42 one-man houses 6.1 × 4 m per floor in a 140-m street, hall below, chamber above, "a fireplace in the front wall", a shared hall/treasury and a chapel/library at the ends — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicars%27_Close,_Wells)) is the measured "scholars' quarter" of the English register — a STREET of minimal study-houses with a common hall; the beguinage is its continental cousin (the UNESCO page returned 404 to the sub-lane — UNREACHED). (2) **The observatory** — ancient/Islamic: Maragheh (1259; a circular central building "diameter is 22m", wall base 0.80 m, a "1.5m-wide" entrance corridor into a "3.1m-wide corridor that marks the meridian line" with "6 rooms" each side, a mural quadrant of c. 40 m radius (5.5 m survives), five circular out-buildings for smaller instruments, "a separate library building with an area of 330m²", a metal workshop; 16 units excavated — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [UNESCO astronomical heritage, Maragheh](https://web.astronomicalheritage.net/show-entity?identity=29&idsubentity=1); a digest gives 28 m — CONTESTED, the excavated 22 m wins); Samarkand (Ulugh Beg, c. 1420: "three stories high, 48 metres in diameter", ~45 m high, an arcaded gallery round the circumference; the sextant of 40.04 m radius "embedded in a trench about two metres wide, and dug into a hill in the plane of the meridian", an 11-m arc surviving — CONFIRMED-digest, the Ulugh Beg search); Renaissance: Uraniborg (1576–80: "square, about 15 meters on a side" (16 × 16 m, tower 19 m), two semicircular towers 6 m across N and S, "galleries for the instruments", an alchemical laboratory in the cellar, a printing office and a paper mill, in a geometric walled garden — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [UNESCO astronomical heritage, Uraniborg/Stjerneborg](https://web.astronomicalheritage.net/index.php/show-entity?identity=100&idsubentity=1)) and Stjerneborg (1584, 80 m south: "five round towers with conical domes, called 'crypts'", "mostly underground", one instrument per chamber "under a rotating canopy"; instruments 1.5–2.7 m radius — ibid.); the state observatory: Paris (1667–71, Perrault — no figures on the fetched page), Greenwich (1675–76, Wren/Hooke: "the first purpose-built scientific research facility in Britain", £520, "largely recycled materials", set "13 degrees away from true North, somewhat to Flamsteed's chagrin", the "20-foot-high Octagon Room" with Tompion's clocks, housing "the Astronomer Royal, his assistant and his family as well as the scientific instruments" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Royal Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Observatory,_Greenwich)); the college roof-observatory (Collegio Romano 1787 — family L). (3) **The anchorhold and the hermitage** — the anchorhold: "Most anchoritic strongholds were small, perhaps no more than 3.7 to 4.6 m (12 to 15 ft) square, with three windows", a "hagioscope" or squint "in the common wall facing the sanctuary" (CONFIRMED-digest, the anchorhold search); measured: Compton "2.0 x 1.3 metres, with a loft where the recluse may have slept" (PARENT-VERIFIED 2026-08-23 verbatim) (1185–early 14th c.; two cells, "one south of chancel, one at east end as potential oratory for anchorite-priest"); Leatherhead "2.4 metres square", window "53cm square"; an unnamed 15th-c. anchorite-priest's cell "8.85 by 3.65 metre"; Chester-le-Street's "four-roomed anchorhouse" (the Anker's House — its page 404'd); early cells "lean-to, timber structures rather than the solid domiciles of stones which first came into view in the 14th century"; later ones "small one- or two-roomed structures" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [buildingconservation.com anchorites](https://www.buildingconservation.com/articles/anchorites/anchorites.htm)); the hermitage: Warkworth (c. 1400, "an outer portion built of stone and an inner portion hewn from the sandstone cliff", "a chapel and a smaller chamber, each having an altar", a chantry 1489–1536 — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warkworth_Hermitage) + [EH](https://www.english-heritage.org.uk/visit/places/warkworth-castle-and-hermitage/history/)); Knaresborough's Chapel of Our Lady of the Crag (licensed 1408–09 "to excavate there beneath the quarry in order to make and hold a chapel"; "12 ft long, 8 ft wide and 7 ft high"; altar, piscina, vault, bosses — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapel_of_Our_Lady_of_the_Crag)); the eremitic COLONY: Skellig Michael (6th–8th c. to c. 1200; six beehive cells, the largest "14.5 × 3.8 metres and is 5 metres high"; oratories 3.6 × 4.3 m and 2.4 × 1.8 × 2.4 m with a door 0.9 × 0.5 m; "no more than twelve monks and an abbot"; 600 steps — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skellig_Michael)); Camaldoli (c. 1012–26; "venti celle" in five rows beyond a gate, each one-storey "a chiocciola": "portico, vestibolo, camera, studio, oratorio, legnaia, bagno" with a walled garden — the first five cells and "un piccolo oratorio" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [it.wikipedia Eremo di Camaldoli](https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eremo_di_Camaldoli)) = family F's Carthusian cell-house at its origin; Northamptonshire's hermitage chapels (family A). (4) **The oracle** (a BOUNDED POSSIBILITY only — the deity doctrine forbids the rite; the BUILDING is a cella with a hidden inner room) — Didyma (crepidoma "almost 60 by 120 meters", stylobate 51 × 109 m, columns 19.70 m, "25 meters high walls of the sekos" enclosing an OPEN court with a naiskos 8.24 × 14.23 m; the great doorway's threshold "1.5 meters above the floor of the pronaos" (an unenterable door — the focus is SHOWN, not entered) and "two long constricted sloping tunnels built within the thickness of the walls" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Didyma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Didyma)); Delphi (6 × 15 peristyle; "the pronaos, the cella, the inner sanctuary, and vault or chamber" — no figures; the tholos of Athena Pronaia 14.76 m across with 20 Doric + 10 Corinthian columns; theatre ~4,500; stadium 177 × 25.5 m for 6,500 — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Delphi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delphi)); Dodona (cult from the Bronze Age with "No buildings"; a small stone temple only in the 4th c. BC; a wall round the tree c. 290 BC — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dodona)) — the oracle at its floor is family I's grove; Epidaurus (the temple "six columns by eleven, measuring ca. 80 feet"; the abaton unmeasured — the incubation hall is family J's ceiling); the Parthenon as the cella type (cella 29.8 × 19.2 m in a 69.5 × 30.9 m peristyle; columns 13.72 m; the opisthodomos as a TREASURY "contained the monetary contributions of the Delian League"; pronaos windows 3 × 2.5 m — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthenon)); the Maison Carrée (26.42 × 13.54 m on a 2.85-m podium, pronaos "almost a third of the building's length", 17.1 m high — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maison_carr%C3%A9e)).

**(b) Measured.** Scholar: Urbino 11 ft 6 in × 13 ft 4 in; Montaigne ~16 paces round; Vicars' Close house 6.1 × 4 m/floor; observatory: Maragheh 22 m (28 digest) dia., corridors 1.5 / 3.1 m, 6 + 6 rooms, quadrant r ≈ 40 m, library 330 m²; Samarkand 48 m dia., 45 m high, sextant r 40.04 m in a 2-m trench; Uraniborg 15–16 m sq, tower 19 m, side towers 6 m; Stjerneborg five crypts, 80 m off; Greenwich Octagon Room 20 ft high, £520, 13° off north; anchorhold 2.0 × 1.3 / 2.4 sq / 3.7–4.6 sq / 8.85 × 3.65 m, window 53 cm sq; hermitage chapel 12 × 8 × 7 ft (rock-cut); Skellig cell 14.5 × 3.8 × 5 m, oratories 3.6 × 4.3 and 2.4 × 1.8 × 2.4 m, door 0.9 × 0.5 m; Camaldoli 20 cells in 5 rows, 7 rooms each; oracle/temple: Didyma 51 × 109 m, sekos walls 25 m, naiskos 8.24 × 14.23 m, threshold 1.5 m; Parthenon 69.5 × 30.9 m, cella 29.8 × 19.2 m; Maison Carrée 26.42 × 13.54 m, podium 2.85 m; Delphi tholos 14.76 m. NOT FOUND: the Anker's House plan; Warkworth's room sizes; the Epidaurus abaton; Uraniborg's room program beyond galleries/cellar; Paris Observatory figures (all four targeted searches REFUSED).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) Maragheh 22 vs 28 m — excavated vs narrative; report 22. (ii) The anchorhold's "12 to 15 ft square" (the digest's general figure) vs the measured 2.0 × 1.3 m (Compton) — the general figure is a CEILING, Compton the FLOOR; the 8.85 × 3.65 m priest's cell is a HOUSE. (iii) The observatory is a 17th-c. building in the European register (Uraniborg 1576 is the first; Greenwich 1675) — before that it is a TOWER ROOM or a roof (the college's 1787 roof tower); the Islamic observatory (1259, 1420) is the measured medieval type and is BEYOND the register. (iv) The oracle: every fetched oracle site is a TEMENOS with a cella — the grove (Dodona, no buildings for a millennium) is the floor, the walled sekos with a naiskos the ceiling; the catalog has no oracle row and the deity doctrine forbids the function; only the BUILDING (a cella with an inner hidden room and an unenterable door) is typed. (v) Negation: the sub-lane's searches for the hermitage and oracle measured plans were REFUSED — these four rows rest on Wikipedia figures single-sourced; P1c must verify.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** The scholar's house: a `THROUGH_ROOM` chamber with a study `CLOSET`; the tower-study stacks chapel / apartment / library on a `STAIR_HALL{VICE}` (Montaigne) — the tower as a one-person precinct; the quarter = a `STREET` of unit houses with a hall-gate (Vicars' Close). The observatory: a `CENTRALIZED` cell (round, 22–48 m) with a MERIDIAN CORRIDOR as its spine (Maragheh's 3.1-m corridor — a `CORRIDOR` whose direction is astronomical, not architectural; the first corridor in the tranche licensed by a SIGHT-LINE), an `EXTERIOR_WALK{GALLERY}` round the circumference (Samarkand's arcaded gallery), instrument `GALLERY`s (Uraniborg), the sextant TRENCH dug into the hill (a `VERTICAL`-class cut), the underground crypts with rotating canopies (Stjerneborg — a cell whose roof moves), the roof terrace (Greenwich/Paris). The anchorhold: NO circulation — the cell is SEALED (one door walled up at enclosure — the addendum's `approach: SINGLE` taken to ZERO: `approach: NONE`; joints = three WINDOWS: the squint to the altar, the service hatch to the world, the light); its loft by ladder. The hermitage: rock-cut chapel + chamber, reached by boat/steps (Warkworth; Skellig's 600 steps — an `EXTERIOR_WALK` that IS the institution's boundary). The oracle-cella: a `LOBBY{PRONAOS}` (a third of the length) → a cella → a hidden inner room reached by `CORRIDOR{MURAL; sloping tunnels}` (Didyma) — the only mural tunnels in the tranche with a stated purpose (to bypass the unenterable door).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** The scholar's: `STORE{BOOK_CHEST}`, `CLOSET{STUDY}`, a cupboard and shelves (Urbino); the observatory: an instrument store (the five out-buildings; Stjerneborg's crypts ARE instrument stores with a seat), the library building (330 m²), the workshop, the alchemical cellar, the paper mill (Uraniborg — a learning precinct with INDUSTRY); the anchorhold: none (the squint is a joint, the hatch a fixture); the hermitage: the "legnaia" (woodstore) and "bagno" of Camaldoli's seven-room cell — a one-person house with its own service set; the oracle-temple: the OPISTHODOMOS as the state treasury (the Parthenon) — `CLOSET{TREASURE}` at the back of the cella, reached only through it; Delphi's "treasuries" (small cella-buildings for dedications — unmeasured).

**(f) Typed proposal.** NO CATALOG ENTRY for three of four; the Sage's quarter is `parti: STREET{houses: DWELLING + STUDY; n; common hall; chapel+library at the end}` (the Vicars' Close model) or a row of `TOWER_STUDY` (Montaigne). `OBSERVATORY{parti: CENTRALIZED{dia 15–48 m; storeys 2–3; meridian corridor; galleries; roof terrace} | TOWER_ROOM (the pre-1576 floor); out: instrument crypts, library, workshop, garden; era ≥ 1259 (beyond) / ≥ 1576 (Europe)}`. `ANCHORHOLD{parti: NONE_HOSTED (a cell on the church's north chancel wall); 2.0 × 1.3 → 4.6 m sq; rooms 1–4; approach NONE; windows 3 (squint, hatch, light); loft; era 1185–1540}` — the smallest faith cell in the tranche. `HERMITAGE{parti: TEMPLE_CELLA (floor: a rock-cut chapel 12 × 8 × 7 ft + chamber) | CELL_COLONY (Skellig: beehive cells + two oratories + a church, 12 + 1 persons; Camaldoli: 20 seven-room cells in rows behind a gate — family F's CARTHUSIAN origin); siting: cliff, crag, island, forest; access: boat, steps}`. `ORACLE (bounded possibility; not a catalog row; function forbidden by doctrine): parti: TEMPLE_CELLA{pronaos (⅓ length); cella; inner room (naiskos 8 × 14 m in an open sekos, or a "vault or chamber"); unenterable door (threshold 1.5 m); mural tunnels; opisthodomos treasury; peristyle 6 × 11–15; podium 2.85 m} in a TEMENOS{wall; tholos; theatre; stadium; treasuries; the tree/spring (family I)}`. `fixtures[]`: TRESTLE_DESK{ink-holes}, SECOND_DESK, BOOK_CHEST, SHELF_TIERS{5}, SQUINT, SERVICE_HATCH, LOFT_LADDER, ALTAR (hermit's), MURAL_QUADRANT{r 40 m}, SEXTANT_TRENCH{2 m wide}, ROTATING_CANOPY, OCTAGON_ROOM{20 ft high}, MERIDIAN_LINE, TRIPOD/FOCUS (oracle — unmeasured), TREASURY_DOOR. Structural: `rockCut`, `corbelledBeehive`, `brick{Uraniborg}`, `underground{crypts}`, `marbleColonnade{19.7 m}`, `podium`. Licensing: the Sage's quarter by tier city + a collegiate/cathedral institution to host the street (the Vicars' hall); the observatory by a PATRON event (Hulagu, Ulugh Beg, Frederick II of Denmark, Louis XIV, Charles II) at any tier — a one-off; the anchorhold by a bishop's licence and a parish to feed it (village+); the hermitage by a lord's licence (Warkworth, Knaresborough) and a wild site; the oracle by culture beyond the register. HOME: `ROOM_KINDS.study/chamber/cells` exist; NO TYPED HOME for the observatory's meridian corridor or crypts, the sealed cell with `approach: NONE`, the rock-cut chapel, or the cella-with-inner-room. VERDICT: Sage's quarter = OWN BUILDINGS (a street of dwellings) HOSTED by a collegiate institution; observatory = OWN BUILDING (rare, patron-licensed); anchorhold = HOSTED (a cell against the church); hermitage = OWN MICRO-BUILDING on a wild site; oracle = NO ROW (a beyond-register cella).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) `approach: NONE` — a cell reachable by NO door (the anchorhold): the validator's reachability (S9) must accept a deliberately unreachable cell whose joints are windows. (2) A corridor licensed by a SIGHT-LINE (the meridian) — the fourth corridor licence (program, fashion, surveillance, astronomy). (3) A cell whose ROOF is a moving fixture (the rotating canopy) and a cell that is a TRENCH (the sextant) — `VERTICAL{CUT}`. (4) The one-person institution (anchorite, hermit, scholar) is a DWELLING with one faith/learning fixture — three catalog rows could be one row with a `solitary: bool`.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** English: the anchorhold on the chancel's north side, the rock-cut hermitage licensed by a lord, the vicars' street, the chained parish library; Italian: the eremo of seven-room cells in rows (Camaldoli) and the ducal studiolo; French: the tower-study (Montaigne) and the state observatory (1667); Danish: the first purpose-built European observatory in a geometric garden (1576) and its underground successor; Irish: the cliff-top beehive colony. Beyond: the Islamic observatory is the measured medieval type (22–48 m round, meridian corridor, 40-m instruments, a 330-m² library — an institution England had no building for until 1675); the Greek oracle-temple is the cella-with-hidden-room whose door cannot be entered — the `TEMPLE_CELLA` parti's defining attribute (`focus: SHOWN_NOT_ENTERED`).

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** The Sage's quarter is on the Adventuring shelf; "researchers" = the observatory + library + workshop bundle (Uraniborg's precinct is the historical "research facility"); the oracle's RITE → R-INST-5 (never here); the anchorhold/hermit as "hermit sage" → the same cell.

## §16 · FAMILY O — the scriptorium and the archive-as-learning: NO CATALOG ENTRY (the catalog's Chroniclers' exchange L1706 is R-INST-2's trade archive; the Village scribe L710 is R-INST-1's; the muniment room is R-INST-1's Family C, measured at Windsor by Steane)

*Provenance: a PARENT MERGE — no sub-lane was assigned; the evidence is family F's carrels (Clark LOCAL), family M's armarium and chest (Clark/Streeter LOCAL), family L's muniment towers (VCH/RCHME SUB-LANE CONFIRMED; W&C LOCAL), family G's records repository (Westminster), and the CIRC addendum's §4.4 `CLOSET{MUNIMENT}` bundle (Steane, Windsor — cited, not restated).*

**(a) Analogue.** The scriptorium is NOT a room in the fetched evidence — it is the CLOISTER WALK with carrels: "every one of the old Monks had his carrell ... and there studyed upon there books, every one in his carrell, all the after nonne" (the Rites of Durham); carrels "for the readers and writers" (LOCAL, Clark pp. 90–93); Carlin's type-plan puts a "scriptorium" in the east range with the chapter hall and staircase (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, family F) — PLAUSIBLE as a named cell, unmeasured anywhere in the set; Clark's own conclusion is that the WRITING place was the carrel in the walk next the church "for the sake of warmth" and the BOOK place was the armarium/chest, then the 15th-c. room over the walk (family M). The archive splits by OWNER: (1) the parish's — the CHEST (three locks; registers from 1538 — family C); (2) the college's — the MUNIMENT TOWER (New College's four-stage tower "housed the archive chambers above, each fitted with 'simple lierne vaults'"; Merton's 1308–9 treasury "quadripartite stone vaults in two bays", roof "of stone slabs"; Eton's over-gate "one for muniments, the other for relics, plate, and jewels" "resembled the Muniment Tower at Winchester" — family L); (3) the cathedral's/monastery's — the treasury and the chapter house's vestibule; Westminster's chapter house as the State's "repository for State records until 1863" and the Pyx Chamber "behind double doors with multiple locks; the keys to the outer doors were kept by the Chamberlains of the Exchequer" (family G); (4) the town's — R-INST-1's muniment room over the gate (Windsor's Aerary measured by Steane; the addendum's bundle: stone vault, tile floor, two iron-sheathed doors, ≤ 1 barred window, no heating, single newel, keys all different, ≤ 30 m², first floor/tower/over the gate); (5) the scholar's — the book-chest behind the chair (family N). The "historical records" and "map archives" of the catalog's Great library (L2348) are (3) + (4) hosted in (family M's) library building — the Laurentian sits on the cloister range; the Bodleian's Arts End with its gallery is the records room's ceiling; no medieval MAP ROOM was found (family M). The chronicle-as-learning: the catalog's Village musician "keeper of local history through song" (family H) and the Chroniclers' exchange (R-INST-2) — the oral and the traded archive are PERSONS, not cells.

**(b) Measured.** Carrels: Gloucester 4 ft × 19 in × 6 ft 9 in (stone, ×20); Durham 3 per window × 11 windows = 33; Canterbury's glazed cubicles c. 1470 (family F/M, LOCAL Clark; SUB-LANE CONFIRMED). Armarium/book-room: Fossa Nuova 14 × 10 ft; Le Thoronet 3 × 3 m; Kirkstall c. 1150 (LOCAL/SUB-LANE). Muniment: Merton treasury 2 bays vaulted (dims NOT FOUND); New College 4 stages (NOT FOUND); Eton design gate 40 ft wide with two rooms (LOCAL, W&C i); Windsor's Aerary — R-INST-1's figure (not restated); Hereford's transept-triforium "Treasury" chamber 39 ft 7 in × 18 ft 6 in with three round windows 9 ft 6 in across and openings into the transept (LOCAL, Streeter p. 115). Chest: St Weonards 2.6 × 0.61 × 0.58 m; Eckington 0.87 × 0.54 × 0.52 m (family C); "an old irebounde cofre having hie feet" 3 ft 11 in × 20 × 18 in (LOCAL, Streeter p. 117). No measured SCRIPTORIUM room exists in the set (searched by the sub-lanes as "scriptorium" in the St Gall plan's room list — the plan names one, unbuilt).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) "Scriptorium" as a room is a type-plan word (St Gall, Carlin) with NO measured survivor; the evidence says the writing place was a fixture-row in a GALLERY. The grammar should NOT mint a scriptorium cell — it should mint CARRELS on the cloister walk (family F) and a study CLOSET in the college chamber (family L). (ii) The archive is owned by FIVE institutions at five grades and is a chest at four of them — the cell appears only where R-INST-1 found it (the muniment tower/gate room) and where family G found it (the chapter house as a state store). (iii) Negation: none run (no sub-lane) — this family is a PARENT MERGE and its one absence (no scriptorium room) is an absence in the other families' evidence, not a searched null.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** The carrel row subdivides a `GALLERY{CLOISTER_WALK}` into one-light boxes (family F/M); the muniment tower's `STAIR_HALL{VICE; single newel}` off the hall (New College: the hall's stone stair IS in the muniment tower) — the addendum's `GUARDIAN_ADJACENT` + `approach: SINGLE`; Hereford's treasury chamber BEHIND the triforium with "window-like openings ... into the Transept" — an archive cell with a view into the church (the addendum's `lightReq: VIA_CORRIDOR` inverted: the store watches the nave); the Pyx Chamber off the east walk behind "double doors with multiple locks" = `LOBBY{DOOR_LOBBY}`.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `CLOSET{MUNIMENT}` — R-INST-1's bundle CONFIRMED by three colleges and a cathedral in this tranche (stone vault ×3, stone-slab roof ×1, tower ×3, over-gate ×1, beside the hall stair ×1, divided keys ×2); `CLOSET{TREASURE}` paired with it (Eton; Winchester; Hereford's "Treasury"); `STORE{BOOK_CHEST}` and `CLOSET{ARMARIUM}` (family M); the parish chest (family C); the STATE archive hosted in a faith building (Westminster 1540–1863); Stationers' stock in a cathedral crypt (Old St Paul's 1666 — family G). Adjacency: the archive cell is ALWAYS up (tower/over gate/first floor/triforium), ALWAYS vaulted, NEVER heated, and reached by ONE stair from a guarded room.

**(f) Typed proposal.** NO CATALOG ENTRY; no new row recommended — the family resolves INTO others: `SCRIPTORIUM → GALLERY{CLOISTER_WALK}.fixtures[CARREL ×n]` (family F) and `COLLEGIATE_COURT.CHAMBER.CLOSET{STUDY}` (family L); `ARCHIVE → CLOSET{MUNIMENT} (R-INST-1's bundle) hosted in {PARISH_CHURCH (chest, fixture) | COLLEGE (tower/over-gate) | CATHEDRAL (treasury/chapter-house vestibule) | TOWN (gate room — R-INST-1) | LIBRARY_BUILDING (the records room — family M)}`. `fixtures[]`: CARREL, DESK{ink-holes, weights}, CHEST{locks 1–3; iron-bound; high feet}, PRESS, KEY_SET{divided}, DOUBLE_DOORS, BARRED_WINDOW, VIEW_OPENING (Hereford). Licensing: by the HOST's tier and endowment; era: carrels 1258+ (Westminster), stone carrels 1370+, muniment towers 1308+ (Merton), the over-gate pair 1440s (Eton/King's design), the state repository 1540+. HOME: `ROOM_KINDS.records` exists (R-INST-1 placed the muniment there); NO TYPED HOME for the carrel row or the archive-with-a-view. VERDICT: HOSTED everywhere — an archive is a cell of its owner's building; a scriptorium is a fixture-row on a walk.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) The same `CLOSET{MUNIMENT}` bundle recurs across FOUR tranches (civic gate, college tower, cathedral treasury, library records) — one cell type with a `hostedIn` pointer, not four. (2) A fixture-row (carrels) that SUBDIVIDES a circulation cell without becoming cells — the addendum's "too small to be a cell is a fixture" rule applied to a gallery's bays. (3) The catalog needs no scriptorium row; it needs the carrel fixture on family F's walk and the study closet on family L's chamber.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond.** English: carrels in the walk next the church (timber, then Gloucester's stone), the muniment tower; continental: the Cistercian book-room cut from the sacristy (Fossa Nuova, Le Thoronet), the Carthusian cell's own study (Camaldoli's "studio"), the Italian public library's desks as the writing place (Cesena's seat-desk-shelf unit); beyond: the madrasa's library and the observatory's 330-m² library building (N) — the archive as a BUILDING appears only where a patron endows it.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** The Academy of magic's "research facilities" and the Mages' district's "libraries" → families L/M + R-INST-5; no magical archive convention here.

## §17 · FAMILY P — the continental and beyond-register parti families: mosque-court · synagogue-hall · centralized (cross-in-square, domed octagon, round church, baptistery) · stave church · meeting house · temple-cella (no catalog row; the closed `parti` enum's members, each a FAMILY under the deity doctrine)

*Provenance: sub-lane IP evidence (see §10 header), plus families C/G for the hall church, the basilica and the Italian detached campanile/baptistery. Every building below is a BOUNDED POSSIBILITY of a parti family; no rite is described; rite-specific vocabulary is translated to the typed FUNCTION.*

**(a) Analogue and (b) Measured, per parti family.**

**P1 · `MOSQUE_COURT` (the hypostyle hall + forecourt + orientation wall).** Kairouan (founded 670; rebuilt 836; perimeter ~405 m; sides 127.60 / 125.20 / 72.70 / 78 m; > 9,000 m²; prayer hall 70.6 m wide × 37.5 m deep, "17 aisles of eight bays, the central nave is wider", 414 columns (> 500 in all) reused from Roman and Byzantine sites; the court "approximately 67 by 52 metres" (90 × 72 with its galleries), an underground cistern on 7-m pillars; the tower 31.5 m on a 10.7-m square base with 129 steps (c. 836); the orientation niche "2 metres long × 4.5 metres high × 1.6 metres deep" (862–63), the stepped pulpit 3.93 × 3.31 m with eleven steps, the screened enclosure 2.8 × 8 × 6 m (11th c.); walls 1.90 m thick; corner towers 4.25 m; nine gates — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Great Mosque of Kairouan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Mosque_of_Kairouan)); Córdoba (785–86: "roughly square floor plan measuring 74 or 79 meters per side", half hall and half court, ~120 columns, 11 aisles → 19; extended 8 bays (64 m long) 833–48, 12 bays (45 m) 961–76, 8 naves (47.76 m east) 987–88 to "8600 square meters"; final 180 × 130 m, ~850 columns; tower 47 m on an 8.5-m base (958); a cathedral nave "inserted into the middle of the former mosque's prayer hall, starting in 1523" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Mosque–Cathedral of Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosque%E2%80%93Cathedral_of_C%C3%B3rdoba)); the domed variant: Süleymaniye (1550–57; dome 26.5 m across and 53 m high over a hall "approximately 58.5 by 57.5 meters" — "a single vast space dominated by its central dome"; forecourt "roughly 47 by 57 meters" with a colonnaded peristyle; four towers at the court corners, two of 76 m; the complex (külliye) "four madrasas, hospital, public kitchen, public baths, caravanserai, medical school and hadith school, primary school" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Süleymaniye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%BCleymaniye_Mosque)). FUNCTION GRAMMAR: an ORIENTATION_WALL with a niche (FOCUS) and a stepped READING/PREACHING platform beside it; a CONGREGATION floor that is a hypostyle grid (columns at ~3–4 m pitch) or a single domed square; an ABLUTION court with a cistern/fountain before the hall; a TOWER for the call; a screened ENCLOSURE for the ruler; gates on three sides. The congregation does NOT walk an axis — it fills a grid facing a wall: the anti-BASILICAN parti.

**P2 · `SYNAGOGUE_HALL` (the twin-nave or single hall with a raised reading platform in the middle and the ark on the orientation wall).** Prague's Old-New (late 13th c., after 1254; "15 x 9 m., divided lengthwise into two naves by two octagonal piers" (PARENT-VERIFIED 2026-08-23 verbatim, Princeton); six five-ribbed vaulted bays; "Nine steps lead from the street into a vestibule" (a sunken floor); twelve narrow windows; the reading platform "set between the piers" with a 15th-c. wrought-iron enclosure; the ark "in the middle of the customary eastern wall" up five steps; "Women sit in an outer room with small windows looking into the main sanctuary"; foundations > 1 m with corner buttresses — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Old New Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_New_Synagogue) + [Princeton Mapping Eastern Europe](https://mappingeasterneurope.princeton.edu/item/altneushul-old-new-synagogue-in-prague.html)); Worms (1034; rebuilt 1175 "a pair of Romanesque columns supporting groin vaults"; a women's hall added 1213 alongside; a study hall ("Rashi Shul") adjoining; the ritual bath 1186 "southwest of the synagogue"; destroyed 1938, rebuilt 1961 from salvaged stones — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Worms Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worms_Synagogue)); Amsterdam's Esnoga (1671–75, Bouman; "130 feet in length and 100 feet in width", the hall "approximately 36 by 19 meters" (CONFIRMED-digest; a number audit: 130 × 100 ft = 39.6 × 30.5 m is the BUILDING, 36 × 19 m the HALL — consistent); "a single, very high rectangular space"; four basalt columns carrying three timber barrel vaults; twelve granite columns carrying the women's galleries; 72 windows; "1,200 men and 440 women"; the ark on the east wall, the reading platform "on the other side of the room, opposite of the ark" (the longitudinal Sephardic type); a sand floor "to absorb dust, moisture and dirt"; 3,000 piles; a forecourt of low buildings: "the winter synagogue, offices and archives, homes of various officials, the rabbinate, a mortuary, and noted Ets Hayim library", a ritual bath, classrooms — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Portuguese Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Synagogue_(Amsterdam)) + [Morashá](https://www.morasha.com.br/en/diaspora-communities/the-snoga-of-amsterda.html)); the storeroom for worn texts — Cairo's "a window- and doorless room, only accessible from the ... galleries by climbing a ladder to an opening up in the wall", "relatively large", ~400,000 fragments (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Cairo Geniza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo_Geniza)); the ritual-bath building: Speyer 1128 (a pool; the UNESCO and Speyer pages 404'd — UNREACHED), Sopron 14th c. "within an old synagogue" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Mikveh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikveh)). FUNCTION GRAMMAR: a congregational HALL (twin-nave 15 × 9 m → single high hall 36 × 19 m), the FOCUS (ark) on the orientation wall up steps, the READING_PLATFORM in the MIDDLE (medieval) or at the far end (Sephardic) — two foci on one axis; a sunken floor (steps DOWN from the street); a separate WOMEN'S room with windows into the hall (medieval) → GALLERIES on columns (17th c.); an attached STUDY HALL; an ABLUTION building (the bath) nearby; a TEXT STORE with no door (a `CLOSET` reached by ladder through a hole — `approach: HATCH_ONLY`, the addendum's prison-pit joint in a faith register); a forecourt of service houses.

**P3 · `CENTRALIZED` (the dome over a square or octagon; the round church; the baptistery).** Hagia Sophia (532–37; 82 × 73 m, 55 m high; the rebuilt dome "20 Byzantine feet (6.25 m) higher than before" on pendentives "whose diameter was between 32.7 and 33.5 m"; "Upper gallery", narthex + exonarthex, an atrium, a treasury building (skeuophylakion) — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia)); the cross-in-square: "a quadratic naos divided by four columns or piers into nine bays", the inner five "a quincunx", the centre domed, the arms barrel-vaulted, the corners groin-vaulted; "The tripartite sanctuary comprises three apses" — the centre for the focus, the north "for the preparation" and the south which "houses liturgical vestments and texts" (the SACRISTY as a third apse); a narthex, later an exonarthex and a side chapel; "this nine-bay core unit was also employed for the construction of halls within residential structures"; from the early 9th c.; variants: the Athonite triconch (11th c.), the compact narthex-less (S. Italy, Cappadocia) — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Cross-in-square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-in-square)); Hosios Loukas (the Theotokos church 959–63 "the oldest example of the cross-in-square type in the country"; the Katholikon c. 1011–12 "the earliest extant domed-octagon church, with eight piers arranged around the perimeter of the naos", the dome "without a drum" on four squinches, "surrounded by galleries and chapels on all four sides", a crypt of "nine groin-vaulted bays" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Hosios Loukas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosios_Loukas)); Daphni (c. 1080–1100; "A square bay in the center ... covered by a broad dome", squinches to an octagon, a Greek cross of four equal barrel-vaulted arms — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Daphni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daphni_Monastery)); Aachen (octagon 14.46 m, 31 m high, in a 16-sided two-storey circuit with the throne on the upper level — family G); the ROUND church: Østerlars (c. 1160; "Nave external diameter: 16 meters"; a hollow central column 6 m across "with small room ('oven')"; three storeys, the top "an open shooting gallery"; an oval chancel and a double-arched apse; a conical roof; a detached bell tower "originally entrance/gate" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Østerlars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%98sterlars_Church)) — a church that is ALSO a keep (R-INST-1's M′ at village scale); the BAPTISTERY: Pisa (1152–1363; 34.13 m across, 54.86 m high, "leans 0.6 degrees"; an octagonal font of 1246 and the 1255–60 pulpit — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Pisa Baptistery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pisa_Baptistery)) and Florence (octagon 37.0 × 32.5 × 39 m; 11th/12th c. by fabric; "between Florence Cathedral and the Archbishop's Palace" — family G); the Protestant centralized church: Amsterdam's Noorderkerk (1620–23, de Keyser: "a Greek cross, with four arms of equal length", "Annex buildings occupy each corner", a small central tower — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Noorderkerk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noorderkerk)); Willemstad's octagon and Burntisland's square (both fetches 404'd/cut — UNREACHED). FUNCTION GRAMMAR: the congregation stands UNDER a dome or round a centre (no axis to walk); the focus in an apse or at the centre; galleries for a separated class (the empress; women); a narthex as the one axial lobby; the sacristy as a THIRD APSE; the round plan doubles as a DEFENSIBLE tower (Østerlars) or a FONT house (the baptistery as a separate building for one fixture).

**P4 · `STAVE` (the timber post-church with a raised central roof and an external walk).** "Norway originally had approximately 1,000 to 2,000 stave churches ... Today, only 28"; "Type A (Single-Nave): Simple churches without internal free-standing posts ... four heavy sill beams on stone foundations with corner posts and wall planks fitted into grooves"; "Type B (Raised Roof) ... free-standing internal posts" (the Kaupanger and Borgund groups); predecessors "post in ground construction"; "external galleries (svalgang) for climate protection and processions" (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Stave church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stave_church)); Haltdalen (Type A; nave "4,8 x 5,8 m (27,84 m²)", chancel "2,9 x 3,4 m (9,86 m²)", "tilsammen ... 37 m²" — PARENT-VERIFIED 2026-08-23 verbatim; timber "shortly after 1159"; moved 1704, 1881–82, 1937; a full-scale replica built 1998–2000 — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [no.wikipedia Haltdalen](https://no.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haltdalen_stavkirke)); Urnes (c. 1130–31, dendro 1129–30; 16 staves; "The roof of the central compartment is 2 metres higher than the roof of aisles"; the predecessors Urnes I nave ~6.5 × 5 m + chancel 3 × 3 m, Urnes III nave ≥ 7.2 × 5.2 m, 8.5–9 m to the gable, chancel 3.5 m wide; the present church seats 100, chancel opening 180 cm — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [no.wikipedia Urnes](https://no.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urnes_stavkyrkje) + [en](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urnes_Stave_Church)); Borgund (timber felled winter 1180–81; "triple-nave ... of the Sogn-type", twelve free-standing columns, an external "ambulatory, in the form of a porch ... under the overhanging shingled roof" (younger than the 14th-c. apse), "Norway's only remaining free-standing stave-work bell tower" (mid-13th c.), sills and 14 svalgang logs replaced 1687–89, ~18,000 shingles — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [en](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borgund_Stave_Church) + [no](https://no.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borgund_stavkyrkje) Wikipedia); Heddal ("the largest": 20 m long, 26 m high, 20 masts in the nave + 6 in the chancel, two aisles, three towers, a svalgang; 180–183 seats; dendro mid-1200s, a rune of 1167; heated to 5–8 °C for conservation — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [no](https://no.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heddal_stavkyrkje) + [en](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heddal_Stave_Church) Wikipedia); the English palisade cousin Greensted (51 split oak logs; dendro 1053 + 10–55; a 16th-c. brick chancel, a 17th-c. weatherboarded tower; HE 1124095 returned Cloudflare 403 — family C). The Russian timber ceiling: Kizhi (1714: 37 m high, 22 domes, "octahedral frame with four two-stage side attachments", 20 × 29 m, "no nails"; the heated winter church 1764, 27 m, 9 domes, 28 × 8 m; a bell tower 30 m on a 6 × 6 m frame "divided into three interior rooms"; an enclosure wall 300 m with a gate 14.4 × 2.25 m facing east, "symbolic division between the holy ground and the outside world" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Kizhi Pogost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kizhi_Pogost)). FUNCTION GRAMMAR: the two-cell plan (nave 5 × 6 → 20 m; chancel 3 × 3) on sills; a RAISED central roof on 12–20 posts (the clerestory without stone); an EXTERNAL covered walk round the building "for climate protection and processions" = `GALLERY{EXTERNAL_AMBULATORY; OPEN_ONE_SIDE; GRADE}` — the processional ring OUTSIDE the church; a DETACHED bell tower; a SUMMER/WINTER church pair (Kizhi: the heated one is the small one); a walled enclosure with an east gate; the whole building MOVABLE (Haltdalen moved three times — a timber church is a FIXTURE-grade building: `movable: true`).

**P5 · `MEETING_HOUSE` (the plain preaching hall with no focus wall: the stand, the benches, the gallery, the shutter).** Brigflatts (1675; "random rubble, mostly lime-washed"; "The rectangular building is aligned east-west with the porch facing south. The full-height 3-bay meeting room is to the east with the former women's meeting room (now part of a cottage) to the west"; "a 2-storey gabled porch" with "stone benches to either side"; "An internal draught lobby leads to the meeting room and a doorway to the left leads into the library"; "the minister's stand on the south side, fixed benches to the east, dado panelling, galleries on three cylindrical oak posts on the north and east sides with turned balustrades, wide staircase to the west side with a dog gate ... and a moveable (now fixed) screen to the west wall with fielded panelling and turned hat pegs"; a wooden floor 1681, the west stair and L-shaped gallery 1711–15 (£5), a plaster ceiling 1715, the balustrade 1720, "In 1749 a partition was installed to separate the women's meeting room in the west gallery"; a stove "sometime in the nineteenth century"; a stable and gig house with "a room above it, the latter later used as a schoolroom" (1709); a warden's cottage made from the women's gallery 1900–05 — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Quaker heritage, Brigflatts report](https://heritage.quaker.org.uk/files/Brigflatts%20LM.pdf), LOCAL `RINST3-IP-brigflatts.txt`); Jordans (1688, "built in just three months"; red brick, hipped tile roof; "the meeting room, an entrance lobby with gallery over and, at the back, a library"; the former cottage's ground floor with "a large open fireplace" and "A vertical sliding shutter opens up to the meeting room"; the room "lit on the north and west sides", "a floor of orange-red bricks laid on bare earth"; "The dado ramps up at the north end for the sounding board of the elders' stand ... on the sounding board are four hat pegs"; "a gallery, originally used for women's business meetings, with vertical sliding shutters between oak posts"; benches "run the length of the two side walls"; "stabling for twenty horses" behind, the hayloft converted 1867; a caretaker in "the loft and the room below it" 1798–1910; a burial ground since 1671 with "nearly 400 burials ... few are marked" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Quaker heritage, Jordans report](https://heritage.quaker.org.uk/files/Jordans%20LM.pdf), LOCAL `RINST3-IP-jordans.txt`); the American Old Ship (1681; "backless wooden benches" → box pews 1755; side galleries 1730/1755; a hammer-beam roof "like the inverted frame of a ship" — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Old Ship Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Ship_Church)); Guyhirn's 1660 chapel with close-set pews and the pulpit as focus (family B); the Huguenot temple (Charenton 1623 — fetch cut off at the kill: UNREACHED). FUNCTION GRAMMAR: NO orientation wall and NO altar — a rectangular room with the STAND (a raised bench with a sounding board) on one LONG side, fixed benches facing it and loose benches round the walls, a GALLERY on posts on two or three sides reached by a stair with a dog gate, a SHUTTERED PARTITION that opens a second room (the women's meeting) into the first — the cell boundary is a MOVING fixture; a porch with stone benches; a library off the lobby; a stable range with a schoolroom over; a burial ground with no markers; a stove only in the 19th c. (unheated by practice, like the library).

**P6 · `TEMPLE_CELLA` (the windowless focus-house in a precinct; the congregation outside).** The Maison Carrée (26.42 × 13.54 × 17.1 m on a 2.85-m podium; "Cella (inner chamber): Small and windowless"; the pronaos "almost a third of the building's length"; the door 6.87 × 3.27 m; six columns in front, twenty engaged — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Maison Carrée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maison_carr%C3%A9e)); the Parthenon (cella 29.8 × 19.2 m in 69.5 × 30.9 m; the rear room as a treasury; pronaos windows 3 × 2.5 m "a rare feature"; church → mosque → ruin 1687 — family N); Didyma's open sekos with a naiskos (family N); the Romano-Celtic square cella 5–16 m with an ambulatory 8.5–22 m for "circumambulation" in a temenos "marked off by a wall, palisade and ditch" (family I); the CAVE variant — the Mithraeum: "either an adapted natural cave, cavern, or building imitating a cave", "A singular entrance/vestibule positioned opposite an apse", "An altar on a pedestal, often recessed within the apse", "Raised benches along side walls for ritual meals", "frequently constructed within or below an existing building" (San Clemente); London's "built partly underground", now 7 m below street level (SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Mithraeum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithraeum), [London Mithraeum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Mithraeum)). FUNCTION GRAMMAR: the FOCUS lives in a small dark room; the congregation is OUTSIDE (the altar in the temenos) or on benches in a sunken hall (the cave type — a HOSTED or UNDERCITY cell under another building); a deep porch; a treasury behind the focus; a podium and steps.

**P7 · the continental BASILICAN variants (already measured in families C/G):** the `HALL` church (Landshut 1389–1500, tower 130.6 m; Munich 109 × 40 × 37 m for 20,000 standing; Girona's single nave 22.98 m wide × 35 m high, 85 m long, "the widest Gothic nave in the world" vs Reims 14.65 m and Paris ~12 m — SUB-LANE CONFIRMED, [Wikipedia Girona Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girona_Cathedral)); the `PREACHING_NAVE` (Gesù 75 × 35 m; Sant'Andrea); the Italian DETACHED campanile + baptistery (Pisa's tower 55–57 m; Florence's octagon 37 m); the French chevet with radiating chapels (Saint-Denis 5; Amiens 7); the Baltic brick basilica (Lübeck 102 m, vault 38.5 m, "about 70 other churches" on its model); the Breton enclos (wall, triumphal gate, calvary, ossuary — family E).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (i) The Esnoga's "130 × 100 ft" vs "36 × 19 m" — building vs hall, reconciled by audit. (ii) Maragheh-style unit slips recur: the Celtic "80 square meters" (family I). (iii) Borgund's svalgang is YOUNGER than its apse (14th c.+) — the external walk is an ADDITION to the stave parti, not its origin; Heddal's is medieval. (iv) The cross-in-square's "nine-bay core unit was also employed for the construction of halls within residential structures" — a faith parti that is also a DOMESTIC hall plan (the addendum's `THROUGH_ROOM` 3 × 3 grid) — the parti is not faith-specific. (v) The round church is a keep (Østerlars' "open shooting gallery") — `CENTRALIZED` and R-INST-1's `M′` overlap; the grammar must allow a faith building with a military floor. (vi) Four continental/Protestant targets UNREACHED (Speyer Judenhof, Charenton, Willemstad, Burntisland — 404s and the kill); the synagogue-hall's ritual-bath building and the Huguenot temple are single-sourced or absent — P1c queue. (vii) Negation: the sub-lane's "timber church not stone" question is answered by the 1,000–2,000 → 28 survival ratio — the timber floor of family C was the NORM in the northern register and the record is its stone survivors.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452) — what each parti ADDS to the §452 set.** `MOSQUE_COURT`: a GRID floor (no axis; circulation = the column pitch, 3–4 m), an ABLUTION court as the `LOBBY` (47 × 57 to 90 × 72 m), gates on three sides, a tower stair (129 steps). `SYNAGOGUE_HALL`: STEPS DOWN from the street into a vestibule (a sunken `LOBBY`), a two-focus axis (ark ↔ platform), windows-into-the-hall from a women's room (a `VIA_WINDOW` light/sight joint, no door) → galleries on columns, a ladder-hatch to a doorless store. `CENTRALIZED`: no processional axis — a ring (Aachen's 16-sided circuit; Hosios Loukas's "galleries and chapels on all four sides"; the Romano-Celtic ambulatory) = `GALLERY{AMBULATORY}` round a centre; the upper gallery for a class; the narthex/exonarthex as two lobbies in series; the round church's `STAIR_HALL{VICE}` in a hollow 6-m central column. `STAVE`: the EXTERNAL ambulatory `GALLERY{EXTERNAL_AMBULATORY}` under the eaves, the detached bell tower, a 1.8-m chancel opening (the screen passage at its narrowest: 180 cm), a movable building. `MEETING_HOUSE`: a `LOBBY{PORCH; stone benches}` + draught lobby, a stair with a DOG GATE to a three-sided `GALLERY{GALLERY_OVER}`, a SHUTTER that merges two cells (a `joint.severable` that is a whole wall), the stand on the LONG side (the axis is transverse). `TEMPLE_CELLA`: a deep `LOBBY{PRONAOS}` (⅓), a podium stair, the focus room with `approach: SINGLE` and no windows, the cave type's single vestibule-to-apse axis with side benches, sunk below grade (`joint.target: UNDERCITY`). `HALL`: the aisles at nave height — the three-vessel floor becomes ONE room 23–40 m wide.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** `MOSQUE_COURT`: the cistern under the court (a `VERTICAL{SHAFT}` + vault), the screened ruler's enclosure (a cell-in-a-hall), the complex's kitchen/hospital/baths/caravanserai (R-INST-4); `SYNAGOGUE_HALL`: the TEXT STORE (doorless, hatch-only — the most restrictive `CLOSET` in the tranche), the archive and library houses in the forecourt, the bath building, the mortuary; `CENTRALIZED`: the sacristy as a THIRD APSE (the only sacristy that is part of the sanctuary's own geometry), the treasury building (skeuophylakion) beside Hagia Sophia, the round church's "oven" room in the column; `STAVE`: none inside (the bell tower is detached; the winter church is the heated store of the congregation); `MEETING_HOUSE`: a library off the lobby, a stable for twenty horses with a hayloft/schoolroom, the women's room as a cottage (re-use), a stove (19th c.); `TEMPLE_CELLA`: the opisthodomos treasury (Parthenon: the state's money behind the focus), the cave type's side benches (fixtures). Adjacency laws stated: the bath SW of the synagogue (Worms); the text store ABOVE the galleries (Cairo); the treasury BEHIND the focus (Parthenon); the heated church SMALLER than the unheated (Kizhi).

**(f) Typed proposal — the closed `parti` enum, with what each member carries.** `parti ∈ {BASILICAN (C/G; sub-forms HALL, PREACHING_NAVE, CATHEDRAL), CLAUSTRAL (F; the precinct parti — a COURT of cells round a church), COLLEGIATE_COURT (L; the manor-house court with chambers on staircases; sub-form iwan-court), MOSQUE_COURT (P1: hypostyle grid + ablution court + orientation wall + tower; or domed square), SYNAGOGUE_HALL (P2: twin-nave/single hall + sunken entry + two foci + women's room/gallery + study hall + bath + doorless text store), CENTRALIZED (P3: dome/octagon/round/Greek cross; sub-forms CROSS_IN_SQUARE{9 bays; 3 apses}, DOMED_OCTAGON, ROUND{keep}, BAPTISTERY{one fixture}, GREEK_CROSS{corner annexes}), STAVE (P4: two-cell on sills; raised roof on 12–20 posts; external ambulatory; detached bell tower; movable; summer/winter pair), MEETING_HOUSE (P5: transverse axis to a stand; benches; three-sided gallery; shutter-partition; porch with benches; library; stable; unheated), TEMPLE_CELLA (P6: windowless focus room in a precinct; pronaos ⅓; podium; treasury behind; sub-forms PERIPTERAL, SEKOS_OPEN{naiskos}, ROMANO_CELTIC{square cella + ambulatory}, CAVE{sunken; benches; hosted/undercity}; the shrine-house floor of family A), NONE_HOSTED, NO_BUILDING}`. Each member's `functions[]` are listed in (a)/(d)/(e) above; the licensing field that SELECTS the member is CULTURE (the owner's `EUROPEAN_FANTASY_BASE` makes BASILICAN the default, STAVE the northern timber floor, CENTRALIZED the baptistery/keep/eastern option, MOSQUE_COURT/SYNAGOGUE_HALL/TEMPLE_CELLA the beyond-register bounded possibilities, MEETING_HOUSE the post-1650 dissent option) — never deity rank or theology. HOME: `INTERIOR_KINDS.faith` (nave front / sanctuary back / vestry) renders ONLY the BASILICAN two-cell; every other member has NO TYPED HOME (no grid floor, no dome ring, no sunken entry, no external ambulatory, no transverse stand, no windowless focus). VERDICT: all six beyond/continental members are OWN BUILDINGS where they occur; the meeting house's floor is HOSTED (a farm kitchen: Jordans met "in the homes of various Friends" and a farm kitchen "built around 1624" before the 1688 house — the §462 test holds for dissent too).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) The `faith` template is ONE parti of a nine-member enum — the engine's `INTERIOR_KINDS.faith` needs a `parti` field before any non-basilican culture can be rendered. (2) Four circulation primitives arrive with the beyond members: the GRID floor (no axis), the SUNKEN ENTRY (steps down from the street into the lobby), the EXTERNAL ambulatory (a gallery outside the envelope), the SHUTTER-WALL (a severable joint that is the whole partition). (3) Two storage primitives: the doorless hatch-only store and the sacristy-as-apse. (4) A MOVABLE building (stave) — `movable: true` on a building, not only on fixtures. (5) A faith building that is also a keep (round church) and a domestic hall plan (cross-in-square) — the parti enum is shared with R-INST-1 and DWR1A at two members.

**(h) Continental vs English — and beyond, in two sentences.** The English register is one parti (BASILICAN two-cell → aisled → cathedral) with a stone bias that hides its timber floor; the continent adds the hall church, the preaching nave, the detached campanile and baptistery, the chevet, the stave church and its external walk, the round church-keep, and (after 1600) the centralized Protestant plan and the plain meeting house. Beyond the register the closed enum needs exactly three more families — the court-with-orientation-wall (MOSQUE_COURT), the hall-with-two-foci-and-a-sunken-entry (SYNAGOGUE_HALL), and the windowless focus-house in a precinct (TEMPLE_CELLA, whose cave sub-form is hosted underground) — each fully describable by circulation, storage and fixture FUNCTIONS without a word of doctrine.

**(i) Fantasy/magical note.** None — every member is a cultural parti; the owner's setting picks the culture weights; the catalog's Magic-shelf rows (Academy, Great library, Mages' district) take the LEARNING partis of L/M, not these.
